#2741. Knicks 121, Cavaliers 102: “The most Mike Brown victory imaginable”

It's easy to get lost in today's world. Everything is spinning so fast, so many things are out of control. Prices, taxes, wars, narratives, diabolical devices, our sense of togetherness. Life sometimes feels like an endless list of things that, no matter how much you scratch off, no matter what you decide not to worry about, gets longer by the minute. The NBA is no exception, a cutthroat world infected by influencing influences that expects daily reinvention, that wants to turn humans into brands and expand like a kraken gobbling up the last little dollars from the deepest pits of the coral reef.

As you watch another foul-baiting baller be rewarded for his deeds, followed by the 23rd commercial for guaranteed grifting, it can be tough to find the joy here from time to time, to remember why you ever thought this was fun. But then Jose Alvarado runs into the picture and steals a ball, only to rise up moments later for a three. He jumps for joy as he hits it, just like the little kid he once was on the playgrounds of the city that he now represents on the biggest stage there is in basketball. And you smile along with him.

There is something inherently right about a guy like Jose Alvarado. Sure, he is an annoying pest defensively, loudmouthed, confident on his good days, cocky on his better ones. A guy you would probably hate if he wasn't on your team. Not even in the fun way of hating an opponent, if there is one. No, you really can't stand that little bugger, like, at all. Yet even if you root for some other franchise whose name doesn't rhyme with clicks, deep down inside your basketball fan's heart, you have to appreciate a guy, a man, a kid like Jose Alvarado. Mike Brown surely does.

It didn't even take Alvarado's tide-turning performance in the Knicks' 121-102 destruction of the Cleveland Cavaliers in yesterday's NBA Cup game, which was, as PolyphonicSpreewell so beautifully put it in the Postgame Thread, "the most Mike Brown victory imaginable." It didn't take his 16 points in the fourth quarter or his 21 in total. But the light shining on Jose surely was a reminder that the big little Puerto Rican embodies what this New York basketball team is. Sure, they can beat you with Xs and Os, with the might of majestic superstars, with firepower that can put a New Year's fireworks display to shame. But there is more that makes the Knicks the Knicks. A group that has a heart and a soul beyond what is expected of them, some inner power they can turn to when things go sideways. Something that lets them play and, more importantly, enjoy the game innocently, freely. How it was supposed to be experienced.

Alvarado is not your type of guy? Pas de souci, says Pacôme Djenon Dadiet! It didn't matter that he didn't really hit shots consistently yesterday or, frankly, ever before. He still plunged into the action versus the James Hardens and Donovan Mitchells of the world like the Knicks' bench was a cliff and his name was Orlando Duque. Maybe not as gracefully, but definitely just as committed and energetic. And, most importantly, without any fear. There was no fear in Tyler Nickel, either, who at some point seemed as far away from real NBA minutes as his birthplace in the Shenandoah Valley is from Madison Square Garden. But don't worry, bench guy, Mike Brown's got a map, and he knows where to find you.

With injuries to OG Anunoby, Deuce McBride, and Jordan Clarkson, plus ineffective and almost shockingly bad nights from Jalen Brunson and Karl-Anthony Towns, the coach seemingly didn't have to search yesterday. He just hurled everyone he had on his pine into the action and watched them stick it to the Cavs. Mo Diawara hit a three. James Wiseman dominated the paint for stretches in the fourth quarter. He didn't look out of place; he didn't look like he didn't know what he was doing. Rather, he played like a man possessed, not by some evil power or the One Ring to rule them all, but by the unwavering confidence and belief of a bald, happy-go-lucky man who coaches champions.

These champions include a much-maligned Mikal Bridges, who carried the team throughout the entire second quarter. They also include Josh Hart and Landry Shamet, who did their thing and then some. They are all guys who had rough goes in the past but who never found themselves in Mr. Brown's doghouse. They deserve to be singled out for pushing the ball in transition, for point-of-attack defense, for just being themselves. But frankly, who doesn't? Everyone did a little and then a lot of what was needed from them. I'm sure Tyler Kolek was one of the biggest cheerleaders despite his rough night on the court. It all speaks to the culture that Mike Brown has bestowed upon this team, that he has created with all of his players. All of them. All these guys who know what hard work is. Who relish every opportunity they get.

And who have a way of playing basketball that is as much fun as you can imagine.

#2740. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2738. How Trump’s latest tariff moves hand India an edge over rival Asian exporters

The latest U.S. tariff rates announced Wednesday have signaled that Washington is willing to offer India an edge over its competitors if New Delhi agrees to finalize a long-pending trade deal.

The Office of the United States Trade Representative has proposed a tariff of 17% on Indian exports if New Delhi "were to reach an Agreement on Reciprocal Trade" with Washington. It added that without a trade deal, India could face tariffs of up to 25% under Section 301 investigations encompassing excess production capacity that harms U.S. interests.

Meanwhile, the USTR has proposed tariffs of 18% on Bangladesh, Cambodia, Thailand, and 20% on China and Vietnam, subject to their respective trade deals, handing India a competitive advantage over rival exporters.

The U.S. expects the announcement of a lower tariff rate will encourage New Delhi to sign off on a trade deal before the G20, Atman Trivedi, partner at Washington-based DGA Albright Stonebridge Group, told CNBC.

India has got a "better bargain with Washington than its main competitors," and it may be enough for the Indian government "to declare victory and secure domestic backing for the deal," Trivedi said.

In February, U.S. President Donald Trump had announced a deal with India, claiming that New Delhi had accepted to "Buy American" at a much higher level after the U.S. agreed to slash tariffs to 18% from 50% earlier.

The India-U.S. trade deal has been pending with both sides indicating there was little room to grant concessions, while the Supreme Court annulling Trump tariffs allowed New Delhi to demand preferential tariffs that give it an edge over competitors.

"Today's announcement increases the urgency of reaching an agreement," Ronak D. Desai, visiting fellow at Hoover Institution, Stanford University, told CNBC. He added that while the prospect of a 17% tariff may strengthen the economic case for the deal, the threat of a 25% tariff could stir the sensitivity of the Indian government.

"The Modi government will be particularly sensitive to any perception that India is conceding under American Pressure," Desai said.

India's commerce ministry on Wednesday said it "does not have structural excess capacity in any sector," denying the findings of the USTR investigation, but added that it remains committed to "working closely with the United States towards the early conclusion of the India-U.S. Bilateral Trade Agreement."

Despite India facing steep tariffs, which were raised to 50%, New Delhi's trade surplus with the U.S. rose to $57.3 billion in 2025 from $45.2 billion in 2024.

Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi and Trump are likely to meet in December for the G20 leaders' meeting in Florida, and several experts believe the two leaders could seal the trade deal on the sidelines of the summit.

#2737. Maryland men’s basketball blows late lead in 75-71 loss to San Diego State

With five minutes remaining in its game against San Diego State, it appeared Mike McNair Jr. and Maryland men's basketball had engineered a storybook comeback in the Players Era Tournament.

Playing basketball on the West Coast for the first time in over two years, the California native scored 16 points as part of a 19-7 Maryland run that turned the game on its head. But the Terps made just two baskets over the final 4:50 of the game, with one coming as time expired.

For the second time in three days, Maryland came up short in a gritty game, as a 16-3 San Diego State run sealed a 75-71 loss for the Terps.

With the defeat, they fell to 0-4 against teams with a winning record.

Every point scored by McNair and the rest of Maryland's guard group was critical. San Diego State completely neutralized the Terps' frontcourt, holding Pharrel Payne and Tomislav Buljan to one combined field goal and nine combined points.

The defensive efforts were led by Jeremiah "Bear" Cherry, who finished with nine blocks, a San Diego State record, alongside 10 points, six rebounds and three steals.

After struggling mightily from 3-point range against Tennessee in their first game, the Terps began Thursday with a concerted effort attacking the paint. But San Diego State was ready, logging three blocks in the opening three minutes as Maryland started 0-for-10 from the floor.

The Aztecs couldn't create a sizable early lead, though. The Terps succeeded defensively as much as they struggled offensively, smothering the Aztecs and denying them second chances.

Seven minutes and 55 seconds into the game, Andre Mills scored the Terps' first basket, a transition layup immediately after San Diego State's sixth turnover of the game.

With Buljan benched after picking up two quick fouls and Payne being marked out of the game, DJ Wagner drove Maryland's offense, scoring or assisting five of Maryland's first six baskets.

The point guard also broke a team streak of 20 consecutive misses beyond the arc halfway through the first frame after leaping to corral a ball and turning it into a quick 3-point attempt.

While the Terps' characteristic 3-point struggles continued, their rebounding struggles were new. The Aztecs dominated the glass, particularly in the absence of Buljan.

Before the Croatian took the bench with his second foul, the Terps outrebounded the Aztecs 9-8. For the rest of the frame, San Diego State outrebounded Maryland 13-5, pushing the lead as high as 10 points.

But suddenly, with five minutes remaining in the first half, the Terps flipped a switch. Four consecutive 3-pointers fell for Maryland, each scored by a different player.

That cut the San Diego State lead to three points. However, Maryland couldn't come closer than that on either side of the half.

Finally, 6:23 into the second half, the Terps earned their first lead. McNair drained a pair of catch-and-shoot 3-pointers, and Kaden House earned and converted two free throws to cap an 8-0 run — Maryland's longest run of the game. McNair finished with a season-high 22 points and six made 3-pointers.

The Terps grew their lead to as many as seven points. But the Aztecs refused to roll over, earning back the lead with 3:10 remaining.

The third lead change of the night was the deciding one, with San Diego State ending the game 7-of-7 from the free throw line to drop Maryland to 3-4 on the season.

1. McNair homecoming. When the Terps took on San Diego State — whose campus McNair grew up an hour away from — the California native had his father and fiancée sitting in the front row.

Midway through the second half, McNair exploded onto the scene, scoring 16 of 19 points as Maryland turned the game around. When he subbed off the court with eight minutes remaining, his family, coaches and teammates all lined up in a row for high-fives and celebrations — celebrations that were short-lived for the Terps.

2. A balanced offensive effort undoes Maryland. Only six San Diego State players scored in the game, but all six of them got into double digits. The Terps were stout defensively early on, but they allowed the Aztecs to shoot 50% from the floor in the second half.

3. Better showing from Baba Oladotun. While the freshman hasn't immediately lived up to his sky-high expectations, Oladotun was an important part of Maryland's rotation Thursday. He had four points on 2-of-6 shooting and also added three assists, two rebounds and a block. He clearly earned the trust of Williams, playing 24 minutes — the most of his career by a significant margin.

#2736. Vance says he was assured Jensen Huang was not under DOJ investigation

Vice President JD Vance said Wednesday that Attorney General Todd Blanche told him Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang was not under investigation.

"I was told by the Department of Justice and multiple officials in our national security apparatus that he was under no investigation," Vance told reporters in Ohio on Wednesday after being asked what assurances he had gotten that Huang was not under investigation. "Not even that he was about to be prosecuted; that he's under no investigation."

Vance spoke two days after President Donald Trump told CNBC in an interview that an investigation had been done.

Trump was asked what Blanche had told him about potential criminal liability for Huang and the question of whether Nvidia chips had ended up in China in violation of U.S. export laws.

"They did a pretty good investigation, and they felt that he, himself, was exonerated," Trump said Monday.

Reuters reported Wednesday that the Department of Justice did not conduct a formal investigation into Huang before Trump pushed for his reinstatement as CEO.

Blanche had concluded there was no evidence Huang had broken any laws based on investigations into Nvidia clients Super Micro Computer and Megaspeed, according to the report.

The DOJ did not immediately respond to a request for comment on the Reuters report. And the White House didn't immediately respond to a request for comment on Vance saying Huang was under no investigation when Trump had said there was one. Nvidia didn't immediately respond to a request for comment about Vance's remarks.

Blanche on Tuesday dodged questions about whether there had been an investigation.

"What the president said is that he talked to a lot of people going into the decision that he made to encourage the board to reinstate him," Blanche said after being asked whether the DOJ had investigated Huang and, if so, what were the results of the probe.

Huang was reinstated as CEO of Nvidia on Monday, after the chipmaker's board of directors said it had received "input from senior levels of the United States government" that Huang was not a national security risk. Trump took credit for Huang's reinstatement in the interview with CNBC shortly after Nvidia's announcement.

Huang resigned following The New York Times reporting multiple affairs, including one with a Chinese businesswoman who is alleged to be a spy for Beijing and who was an executive for the Singapore-based cloud provider Megaspeed.

The reporting added fuel to allegations that Nvidia should have known its artificial intelligence chips were being diverted to China and being used by Chinese companies, violating U.S. export laws.

"The product they create is a very important part of us staying ahead of the SI arms race with China. We really do care about ensuring those chips do not fall into the wrong hands," Vance said, using the Trump administration's preferred acronym for AI, which the president calls "super intelligence."

"We actually see this is an opportunity for Nvidia to focus on complying with American export controls, and we think Jensen Huang is going to take that very seriously," Vance continued.

"We received the assurances that he was not under any significant — he was under no investigation — and the accusations against him were based on anonymous reporting, not on actual evidence," Vance said.

On Tuesday, Trump left the door open to pardon Huang in an interview with the New York Post, but Vance said on Wednesday that the CEO doesn't need one.

"I don't think he needs a pardon at all," Vance said. "I haven't seen any evidence again that he committed a crime. And if there is evidence that he committed a crime, then that's up to relevant authorities to actually bring a prosecution against him."

Huang is scheduled to appear for a closed-door interview with House committees about his dealings with Megaspeed on Tuesday. At the same time, Nvidia will hold its annual conference where it showcases new technology in Washington.

— Kevin Breuninger contributed to this report.

#2735. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

#2734. U.S. proposes new tariffs on 14 trading partners, including China and the EU

The U.S. is preparing new tariffs on 14 economies, including China, the EU, Japan and India, following investigations into what the Trump administration says are excess manufacturing practices that disadvantage U.S. commerce.

The administration launched those and other trade investigations in March, weeks after the Supreme Court unraveled a major piece of President Donald Trump's tariff-heavy economic policy by striking down his "liberation day" duties. Trump has since been accused by critics of his tariffs of trying to revive the canceled duties using other legal authorities.

The latest action targets foreign economies that create, sustain or contribute to structural excess capacity and production in manufacturing sectors that the U.S. finds unreasonable and burdensome or restrictive to its commerce, a senior U.S. official told reporters Tuesday evening.

The new tariffs are "targeted," not blanket rates, and will vary for each economy, and many will not exceed the rates established in previously reached trade deals, said the official, who spoke on condition of anonymity to discuss the forthcoming duties.

The rates, which mostly range from 15% to 20%, are "not really additive" and generally will not stack on top of existing duties, U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer told CNBC's "Squawk Box" Wednesday morning.

"In effect, it'll look more like a top up," Greer said. "When it all nets out, it really will just top up a few percentage points."

An exception is China, which faces a 20% rate under the new tariff policy, "so it will go up some," Greer said. But at the same time, both countries are moving to remove tariffs on some of each other's goods in what Greer called "stability-generating activity through this action."

The senior U.S. official said Tuesday that the 20% rate will come on top of the so-called forced technology transfer-related tariffs of roughly 25%, which were implemented during Trump's first presidential term. U.S. tariffs on Chinese goods will therefore reach about 45%, give or take for different goods, the official said.

Average U.S. tariffs rates on Chinese goods stood at about 36.5% as of July, according to the Congressional Research Service.

Greer also said on CNBC that the new policy likely will not take effect for months.

"We've talked to our trading partners about this. They know what we're proposing," he said. "I think a lot of this is baked in, and I don't think any of these would actually take effect earlier than a couple months from now."

The administration says the tariffs target what it calls "overcapacity" — excess industrial production, often supported by government subsidies, that allows cheaper goods to flood global markets and undercut U.S. producers.

China has disputed the allegations. "The so-called 'overcapacity' does not really exist," China's foreign ministry said Wednesday before the official reports were released by the Office of the U.S. Trade Representative, according to Reuters.

The overcapacity levies are being imposed under Section 301 of the Trade Act of 1974. The Trump administration invoked the same statute to launch investigations into 60 economies over alleged forced-labor issues.

Those investigations, which also began in March, led to broad tariffs of 10% to 12.5% on goods from dozens of countries covering 99.4% of U.S. imports.

The Section 301 actions came weeks after the Supreme Court struck down the near-global tariffs that Trump had enacted under the International Emergency Economic Powers Act.

Shortly after the court's ruling, Trump invoked Section 122 of the Trade Act of 1974 to impose a 10% global tariff. That tariff expired after 150 days, and was immediately replaced with the forced-labor-related duties.

An ongoing lawsuit accuses the Trump administration of citing forced-labor issues as a "pretext" in pursuit of reviving Trump's worldwide tariff regime.

#2733. Taking on Starlink: Europe’s largest satellite factory opens in push to rival Musk

The largest satellite factory in Europe was inaugurated in Belgium this week, boosting the continent's efforts to challenge Elon Musk's Starlink and reduce reliance on U.S. space infrastructure.

Privately held Belgian satellite manufacturer Aerospacelab secured a 2.4-billion-euro ($2.6 billion) contract in September to manufacture 264 of the 348 satellites planned for IRIS², the European Union's answer to SpaceX's Starlink business.

Aerospacelab said this will "form the backbone of Europe's sovereign, secure, and resilient connectivity infrastructure by 2030." It will be named Ectro from Nov. 30.

Its new oval-shaped, 20,000-square-meter facility in southern Belgium, inaugurated by Belgian King Philippe, will be able to produce up to 500 satellites annually, which would make it the world's third-largest satellite production site, according to the Belgian royal palace.

Employees are expected to move into the megafactory in January, and production is slated to begin by the end of 2028, according to AFP.

Satellite connectivity has become increasingly important to everyday life as well as national security.

It provides internet access to remote locations and defense equipment like warships and fighter jets, and lets governments and militaries communicate securely where Earth-based networks are unavailable or disrupted.

The importance of such capabilities has become particularly apparent in Ukraine, as the country's military relies heavily on Starlink connectivity to fight Russian forces. Recent restrictions to the service over Russian-occupied territory reported by Ukrainian officials further underscored the way geopolitical conflict may depend on commercial businesses.

But Europe still has a long way to go in challenging Starlink's dominance.

SpaceX says it has more than 11,000 Starlink satellites in low Earth orbit, making it by far the largest satellite constellation in the world, and plans up to 100,000 satellites.

Among the challengers is Amazon, whose satellite business, Amazon Leo, has deployed over 400 satellites and plans an initial in-orbit constellation of more than 3,200.

Meanwhile, French Eutelsat operates more than 600 satellites in low Earth orbit through its OneWeb constellation. The company is also part of the SpaceRISE consortium alongside SES and Hispasat, tasked with developing and operating IRIS².

The European Commission has called IRIS² a "strategic asset to achieve space sovereignty in the face of commercial satellite constellations, such as Starlink." The aim is for first launches to take place by the end of 2029, with initial services expected soon after.

In August, the Commission and SpaceRISE consortium announced they were expanding the main constellation from 282 to 348 satellites, increasing its secure communications capacity for EU governments by 60%.

Defense and security applications were the main drivers of the expanded scope, the Commission said.

#2732. From Cooper Flagg to Ben Simmons, a look at Montverde Academy’s top NBA draft picks

Montverde Academy (FL) has established itself as one of the premier pipelines for basketball talent in the country, producing NBA stars and some of the sport's most recognizable names.

From No. 1 overall selections to first-round picks who have gone on to become franchise cornerstones, the Central Florida powerhouse has built an impressive résumé at the professional level.

With three players selected first overall in the NBA Draft, here is a look at the 10 highest NBA Draft picks to graduate from Montverde Academy.

The third Montverde Academy product to be selected No. 1 overall, Flagg was the centerpiece of the school's undefeated 2023-24 national championship team. After one season at Duke, he was selected first overall by Dallas in 2025 and won NBA Rookie of the Year in 2026. He is now emerging as the face of the Mavericks' franchise

Cunningham became Montverde's second No. 1 overall selection after leading the Eagles alongside Scottie Barnes and Moses Moody. Following one season at Oklahoma State, he was drafted first overall by Detroit. He has since become a two-time NBA All-Star and led the Pistons' resurgence, including a 60-win season in 2025-26

Simmons became Montverde's first No. 1 overall NBA Draft pick after starring for the Eagles before playing one season at LSU. He won NBA Rookie of the Year in 2018 and became a three-time All-Star with Philadelphia and Brooklyn. His NBA career has since been derailed by injuries, and he is no longer an established NBA roster player.

Russell helped lead Montverde to a national championship before playing one season at Ohio State. He was selected second overall by the Lakers and has gone on to play for several NBA franchises, including Brooklyn, Golden State, Minnesota and the Lakers.

A former five-star recruit, Barrett starred at Montverde before playing one season at Duke. He was selected third overall by the Knicks and spent four-plus seasons in New York before being traded to Toronto in 2023. He remains a key member of the Raptors.

Barnes was part of Montverde's loaded 2020-21 team alongside Cunningham and Moody. After one season at Florida State, he was selected fourth overall by Toronto. He won NBA Rookie of the Year in 2022 and has since become a two-time All-Star and franchise cornerstone.

Duren spent his final high school season at Montverde before playing one year at Memphis. Originally selected 13th overall by Charlotte, his draft rights were traded to Detroit. He has developed into one of the Pistons' key frontcourt players and earned his first NBA All-Star selection in 2026.

Queen was part of Montverde's undefeated 33-0 national championship team in 2023-24. Following one season at Maryland, he was selected 13th overall by Atlanta before being traded to New Orleans. He is now beginning his NBA career with the Pelicans.

Moody helped Montverde capture a national championship during the 2020-21 season before playing one year at Arkansas. He was selected 14th overall by Golden State and has developed into a rotation player for the Warriors. He is currently recovering from a ruptured patellar tendon suffered in March 2026.

Achiuwa graduated from Montverde Academy in 2019 before playing one season at Memphis. He was selected 20th overall by the Miami Heat in the 2020 NBA Draft. He has since played for Miami, Toronto and the New York Knicks.

#2730. Latin America has the copper and energy the AI build-out needs. Infrastructure is the test

Latin America’s copper, energy and mineral resources could play a growing role in supplying the global artificial-intelligence build-out, as data centers and electrification drive demand for power and raw materials.

But for investors, it's less about whether Latin America has the resources than whether governments can turn them into viable projects.

"We are going through an acceleration in energy demand growth. We need every possible energy source available," Michele Della Vigna, head of natural resources research for Goldman Sachs, told CNBC. "Ultimately, what drives economic growth is energy," he added.

Ben Kumar, head of strategy for wealth, investment and public policy at 7IM, suggested Latin America could benefit, given the physical materials needed for AI equipment and data centers. "We are currently neutral in the short term, but if the materials and mining cycle continues, we could see this moving to positive in a few months," he said.

The investment case varies across the region. Chile has copper; Argentina is expanding oil and gas exports; and Brazil has abundant renewable power and significant rare earth reserves.

Chile's copper sector illustrates both the supply potential and the constraints to expanding supply. Copper production fell 8.5% in the first nine months of 2026, according to the state copper commission Cochilco, as lower ore grades, maintenance, and operational disruptions constrained supply.

But demand for refined copper is set to grow 1.6% this year and 2% next year, according to the International Copper Study Group. Supply is struggling to keep pace with demand from expanding electricity grids.

Chile's copper commission has raised its 2027 price forecast to $5.95 a pound, from $5.10. Codelco, the state-owned copper producer, may also have scope to increase supply, including through proposed public-private partnerships.

Rafael Ch, partner and senior analyst for Latin America and emerging markets at Signum Global Advisors, sees demand continuing to grow. "As countries expand electricity grids, they need more copper regardless of which power source they choose," he said.

The scale of the opportunity will depend in part on how quickly energy demand grows. Goldman Sachs forecasts that global power demand for data centers will grow by 170% by 2030. The IEA projected in April that global data center electricity consumption would rise from 485 terawatt-hours in 2025 to 950 TWh in 2030.

The investment case also depends on whether the region can capture more value from the resources it produces. Latin America and the Caribbean refined only 26% of the copper they mined in 2025, according to the IEA.

"Cautious optimism is justified and there will be massive critical mineral demand," said Monica de Bolle, senior fellow at the Peterson Institute for International Economics.

"But improving refining capacity will be crucial to capture more of that value," she added.

Some projects have already secured financing. Chilean explorer Marimaca Copper Corp selected a preferred lender for a $400 million debt facility to build a mine on Oct. 28. The funding will go towards developing an open-pit mine in the north of Chile that will produce 50,000 metric tons of refined copper annually. Production is expected from 2029.

Argentina shows the other side of the opportunity: strong oil and gas resources, but insufficient infrastructure to get more production to global markets.

"I see an opportunity in the infrastructure that gets Argentine oil and gas to market. A producer in Vaca Muerta can have excellent wells and still struggle to expand exports without pipeline capacity and access to ports.

That gives investors a reason to look at transport and export infrastructure alongside production," Rafael Ch, partner and senior analyst for Latin America and emerging markets at Signum Global Advisors, added. Vaca Muerta is Argentina's vast shale basin.

Argentina is also attracting investment in drilling and export infrastructure. International capital markets have reopened to investment in drilling and pipelines in Argentina. That investment has already driven the build-out of a pipeline and port. Argentina will then be able to export far more crude oil. Chevron and American oil tycoon Harold Hamm have also increased their investments in Argentina.

Brazil's case rests partly on its renewable-heavy power mix. About 89% of the country's electricity was generated from renewable sources in 2025, according to the country's Ministry of Mines and Energy.

"That puts Brazil in a strong position to benefit as AI and data centers drive up demand," Stephanie Link, chief investment strategist and portfolio manager at Hightower Advisors, told CNBC.

But while Brazil has the world's second-largest reported rare-earth reserves, its estimated mine production is far smaller than China's. USGS estimates put Brazil's output at 2,000 metric tons in 2025, compared with China's 270,000 metric tons. Brazil's reserves are estimated at 11 million metric tons by the USGS, second only to China's 44 million metric tons.

For investors, the gap between Brazil's vast reserves and limited current production is both the opportunity and the execution risk.

For investors, the policy question is whether governments can make projects viable and keep them on track.

Luis Costa, Citi's global head of emerging markets strategy, said governments need to offer incentives, particularly in the early stages of projects, because infrastructure lags the region's resource wealth. He also stressed that support must last: infrastructure projects can take several years to develop.

The World Bank's Latin America and the Caribbean Economic Update for 2026 projects growth for the region of 2.2%, and highlights the "restricted fiscal space, sticky inflation and high borrowing costs."

"The economic policy and fiscal policy needs to get in line as markets are globally becoming more sensitive to fiscal stories," said Costa.

Brazil will need to establish fiscal credibility to attract investment, particularly if higher public spending puts additional pressure on debt and borrowing costs. "If investors doubt the government can stabilize public debt, companies' long-term financing costs could stay high even as the central bank lowers its policy rate," Ch added.

With Argentina set for national elections in October 2027, political continuity will also matter for energy investment. "A return to tighter currency controls or persistent deficits would make companies more likely to delay projects, even in an oil field with attractive economics," said Ch.

Pro-business President Javier Milei faces a tight race for re-election, though a change in leadership would not necessarily undermine medium- to long-term investment.

"There is also the possibility of a pragmatic Peronist ticket emerging, which markets would be satisfied with," said Nicolas Jaquier, emerging-market fixed-income portfolio manager at Ninety One.

For those governing across Latin America, policies to unlock its wealth of resources will be key for it to emerge as a winner from the AI demand boom.

"Supply chain security is becoming more and more relevant, and if commodities take off Latin America can be a clear and obvious winner," said Kumar.

#2729. S&P warns Nvidia credit rating could be cut over 'rising' management and governance risk

Nvidia's credit rating could be cut by one notch over the next 90 days if the company continues to have "deficiencies" in its management and governance framework, S&P Global Ratings said in the wake of the Jensen Huang's ouster and reinstatement as Nvidia CEO.

"News regarding the CEO and questions regarding potential export control violations have exposed vulnerabilities in Nvidia's management and governance framework, especially in regard to its board structure, composition, and effectiveness," S&P said in a Wednesday note.

Nvidia currently holds an AA rating, the third-highest level.

S&P has placed the chipmaker on "CreditWatch with negative implications," though any potential downgrade would be limited to one notch, to AA-, unless investigations or disclosures lead the agency to conclude that Nvidia's reputation or future prospects have also been harmed.

A downgrade can pressure bond prices, push yields higher and raise borrowing costs. Nvidia priced $25 billion of investment-grade senior notes in June, with maturities from 2028 through 2056, shortly after S&P upgraded the company from AA- to AA.

Nvidia has about $32.5 billion of bonds in the U.S. high-grade corporate bond index, according to Bloomberg.

S&P's Wednesday note follows Moody's Ratings changing its outlook on Nvidia to stable from positive on Nov. 18, citing governance concerns, and leaving that outlook unchanged after Huang's return on Monday.

In its note, S&P cited the "long tenure" of certain board members as a reason their independence may be "compromised" and said Nvidia's management and governance risk was "rising."

The ratings agency added that it remained unaware of any remedial action taken by the board to address what could be "meaningful" management and governance deficiencies.

Chairman Harvey Jones and early investor Tench Coxe have served on Nvidia's board since 1993, while Brooke Seawell joined in 1997. Mark Stevens served from 1993 to 2006 and rejoined the board in 2008.

S&P also flagged questions around geopolitical risk management and internal controls arising from Nvidia's exposure to China.

"While Nvidia management has delivered significant returns for its stakeholders over decades, weak executive oversight and lack of succession planning have brought questions around the "key man" risk to the forefront," S&P's Andrew Chang and David Tsui said.

Chang and Tsui noted that Huang's resignation and subsequent reinstatement did not resolve questions about the board's oversight of executive conduct.

The agency also intends to meet with Nvidia's board and management to discuss plans to address those deficiencies, particularly around "board independence, effective management oversight and a credible leadership succession plan."

Huang returned less than two weeks after Jones said Nvidia's directors did not intend to reinstate him. The board later voted unanimously to bring Huang back, sending Nvidia shares up as much as 36% during Monday's session.

Nvidia shares are down about 12.5% since Monday's close.

#2728. Brendan Schaub Tells Navajo Stirling to Leave City Kickboxing

Former UFC fighter Brendan Schaub encouraged current light heavyweight contender Navajo Stirling to leave his gym, City Kickboxing.

On Thursday, Eugene Bareman, the head coach of City Kickboxing, said that Stirling will not fight his teammate, UFC light heavyweight champion Carlos Ulberg, for the belt.

"Boys from the same gym don't fight. We've had this agreement from the very beginning of the gym. Some things are bigger than fighting. Friendship and the bond you've built with someone are bigger than fighting," Bareman told the media.

Schaub thinks Stirling going along with this is a big mistake.

Taking to his social media, Schaub explained why Stirling is making a big mistake and why he needs to find a new gym immediately.

"Navajo needs to find a new gym. Period. A teams 'code of honor' shouldn't stop a young man's dream. That's just dumb and Navajo will regret it. What's good for Eugene isn't good for Navajo," Schaub wrote on X.

A lot of people don't necessarily agree with some of the things that Schaub says, but in this case, he is 100% correct.

While it's certainly an awkward situation for two teammates to fight each other, the thing is, why else did Stirling get into this sport? Was it to just be the second-best fighter in the world, or does he want to be the champion?

Right now, it appears that City Kickboxing is protecting Ulberg, which seems to be a mistake given that he is currently injured anyways. While not fighting Stirling is fine for Ulberg, since he holds the belt, it's not fine for Stirling, because he is a contender and he needs to fight for the belt, which is his ultimate goal in the sport and in life.

Right now, the teammates, Stirling and Ulberg, have agreed to not fight each other. But as time goes on, things could potentially change, and Stirling may make a wise decision to leave a gym that is holding him back and realize that he needs to start worrying about himself first and foremost.

#2727. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

Catellier ranks No. 667 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.60%. See Williams Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2726. Wealthy Europeans look beyond stocks amid AI selloff. Here’s where they’re investing

Wealthy Europeans are looking to alternative asset classes to build exposure to artificial intelligence themes amid turbulence in public markets.

Goldman Sachs Alternatives' inaugural survey of high-net-worth and ultra-high-net-worth investors found 47% of European respondents saw alternatives as the best way to gain exposure to AI and future tech growth, compared to just 24% of U.S. investors.

The survey found a significant gap between the U.S. and Europe in terms of investor knowledge of alternatives, which include assets like private equity, property and infrastructure. Goldman said 82% of European respondents are familiar with the alternative investments market — compared to 50% of U.S. investors.

Goldman polled 1,000 investors with at least 1 million in investable assets in their local currency across the U.K., France, Germany, Italy and Switzerland. The survey was carried out between June 29 and Aug. 18 this year.

The report on the findings, published Wednesday, did not specify the proportion of respondents who already have a stake in some kind of alternative investment — but wealthy Europeans appeared particularly bullish on the space.

Private equity made up the largest proportion of current alternatives holdings. Among wealthy Europeans with alternatives exposure, 62% said they invested in private equity. The second most commonly held asset class was private real estate, which was backed by 51% of European alternatives investors. Hedge funds were held by 37% of European alternatives owners, and private infrastructure was held by 30%.

One in four respondents who did not already own an alternative investment said they were likely to invest in the market in the coming 12 months. Among those already in the space, 26% said they were likely to increase their allocations over the next year, while just 5% said they planned to decrease their exposure.

Of the European investors who already owned alternatives, 54% said their assets had performed as expected, while 44% said their assets had exceeded their expectations to the upside. Only 2% said their alternative investments had performed worse than expected.

"Recent reforms gave individual investors unprecedented access to alternative asset classes like private equity, venture capital, and private credit," Kyle Kniffen, global head of alternatives for third-party wealth at Goldman Sachs Asset Management, said in Wednesday's report.

He added that wealthy investors "look ready to jump at the opportunity."

"Our survey shows satisfaction among current alts owners is high and adoption looks set to increase," Kniffen said.

Alternative investments have increasingly become a staple of high-net-worth individuals' investment portfolios.

Over the past year, JPMorgan Asset Management's private wealth alternative assets in the Asia-Pacific region have jumped 40%, while BNY Wealth says alternative investments now account for 60% of single-family office portfolios — recently surpassing traditional assets for the first time.

But investing in alternatives carries risks. Private equity, for example, can lock up capital for long periods, and although the number of exits ticked slightly higher last year, the value of those deals fell.

Concerns about private credit, meanwhile, have dominated headlines, with JPMorgan CEO Jamie Dimon warning on Oct. 14, 2025, that "cockroaches" were likely to be lurking in private markets.

— CNBC's Lee Ying Shan contributed to this article.

#2725. ‘Ready for any storm’: UAE minister optimistic on economy as IMF lifts forecast

The UAE is "very optimistic" about achieving 7%-8% growth next year, the country's economy and tourism minister Abdulla bin Touq Al Marri told CNBC after the latest data from the International Monetary Fund showed the country would avoid a recession in 2026.

The IMF last week revised the UAE's real GDP forecast to 0.2% growth in 2026 from a 3.9% contraction predicted a month ago. "The UAE is coming back, and that adjustment within one month shows that there is a huge potential aspect when it comes towards the end of the year," the minister told CNBC's Dan Murphy.

He said the tourism industry was already picking up with occupancy rates returning to "almost near normal" and international carriers including British Airways and Air France rerouting flights to the UAE. The minister expects economic growth to be higher than 0.2% this year.

The Iran war has disrupted economic activity in the UAE, with missile and drone attacks from Tehran and maritime disruptions weighing on energy trade, tourism, aviation and private-sector confidence.

Earlier this month, U.S. President Donald Trump said that renewed attacks on Iran were "highly unlikely" if negotiations produce a "very strong agreement." However, on Monday, Trump threatened that Tehran could face fresh U.S. strikes next year if the two countries failed to strike a deal, as per Fox News.

"We can we cannot promise the world for any storms that will land on our shore, but we can always promise that the UAE's economy is ready for any storm," Al Marri said.

He added that next year the UAE's economy will have a "different" model than the one before the conflict and investments will be focused on building factories and "things that we really need in times of crisis."

The World Bank in October said it expects the UAE's economy to recover next year, driven by rising oil production and exports and a rebound in tourism, trade, transportation, financial services, and construction.

It warned that the improvement in economic activity in the UAE largely depends on the duration and intensity of the Iran war, with a faster de-escalation likely to accelerate the recovery in services exports and private-sector activity.

#2724. Observations after Sixers' 5-game win streak ends with blowout loss to Blazers

Observations after Sixers' 5-game win streak ends with blowout loss to Blazers originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers' five-game winning streak was snapped Monday night by the Trail Blazers.

With a 128-111 defeat at Xfinity Mobile Arena, the Sixers dropped their first game of the year against Western Conference opposition and fell to 14-4. Portland improved to 11-6.

LeBron James had a season-high 23 points and six rebounds. Tyrese Maxey scored 22 points and Jaylen Brown added 20.

Deni Avdija led the Blazers with 31 points and seven assists. Donovan Clingan posted 15 points and 18 rebounds.

The Sixers were missing Joel Embiid (illness). Ja Morant (left hamstring strain) and Shaedon Sharpe (right knee meniscus surgery) were among Portland's sidelined players.

Next on the Sixers' schedule is a Wednesday night game vs. the Heat. Here are observations on their blowout loss to the Blazers:

Jabari Walker, listed at 6-foot-7, defied conventional wisdom by winning the opening tip-off against the 7-2 Cllingan. Maxey then canned a three-pointer.

Brown scored the Sixers' next four points with a pair of free throws and a short jumper. VJ Edgecombe's layup gave the team a 9-2 lead. Brown and Dean Wade both returned Monday after sitting out the last two games with left hamstring injuries.

Avdija got hot in the first quarter. He scored 11 quick points, made his first four field goals and helped the Blazers avoid a serious early deficit.

The Sixers took a 32-30 edge with 9.3 seconds left in the first when Walker jammed in a put-back dunk. The former Portland second-round draft pick played 17 minutes in his second start of the season and continued to look deserving of a rotation spot. He posted 10 points and five rebounds.

Adem Bona was available again following an eight-game absence with a cervical spine sprain. However, Dominick Barlow checked in first to back up Walker.

Barlow had an outstanding start to his night. He scored a clever lefty push shot over Clingan and threw down a dunk in the first quarter. Barlow also made a highlight defensive play in his opening stint, defending Avdija in isolation and rejecting his layup attempt.

Bona then subbed in for Barlow with 3:23 left in the first quarter. He had a positive first possession, grabbing an offensive rebound and driving in for a dunk.

Wade later played at center, too. Barlow logged 16 minutes and had six points. Bona recorded four points and two boards in five minutes. Sixers head coach Nick Nurse ended up using a 10-man rotation and did not play rookie guard Labaron Philon Jr. until fourth-quarter garbage time.

Overall, the Sixers' defense remained considerably worse without Embiid. The team also conceded 15 offensive rebounds, including five to Clingan.

"I think your rim protection and ability to do that with some size is always a focal point of being really good defensively," Nurse said. "And then you've got to do it a little bit by committee (with Embiid out). I thought, for the most part, I was looking at possessions where I liked our help into the paint. They were doing a good job of zinging it out.

"I didn't particularly like our (defense) back out. … There were a couple of times they missed those and still got rebounds. We were compacted in the lane with everybody because we didn't get back out, and then still giving up an offensive rebound as well. That's just playing through the possession, a little bit of energy, all that kind of stuff. … We just didn't do enough pieces well."

Brown delivered tons of efficient offense for the Sixers in the first half and sure didn't appear rusty. His layup late in the second quarter tied the game at 58-all. The five-time All-Star scored 19 first-half points on 8-for-10 shooting.

Avdija exceeded Brown's production, hitting heat-check threes, leaners through contact and just about everything he put up regardless of the defender. He tallied 25 points in the first half and shot 9 for 12 from the field.

James reached 16 points when he capped a high-scoring half by nailing a corner three. The contest was even at 69 apiece heading into halftime.

Monday's game was James' first of the season with three or more made three-pointers. The four-time MVP went 4 for 5 beyond the arc. He'd gone 10 for 46 (21.7 percent) over his first 13 games and was due for a good long-range night.

James was whistled for his third foul early in the third quarter. Brown picked up his fourth with 10:15 to go in the period, forcing him to sit for an extended stretch.

"I wasn't too happy about it. … I think that affected the game," Brown said. "It also affected my rhythm a little bit in the second half. I had to sit (almost) the whole third quarter. Coming back from injury, coming back from a hamstring thing, my body didn't really feel the same after that."

Brown noted postgame that he had "a little bit of soreness and stuff like that."

"I think everything will check out," he said. "The medical staff is on top of everything. Those hamstrings are tricky, so you want to make sure you play it the right way, be safe. I also don't want to miss a lot of games, so I might be out there, but I've also got to be smart. I think I played it the right way today."

Avdija stopped lighting up the Sixers in the third period, but the team still had another unimpressive defensive quarter. Portland posted 32 points in the third and built its lead to double figures in the opening minute of the fourth.

The game devolved into a blowout. An Avdija dunk put the Blazers' advantage at 22 points with 5:15 on the clock and the Sixers didn't have a miracle comeback in store.

Even with all their wins, there's no question the Sixers have been trending downward on defense over the past couple of weeks, allowing regular dribble penetration and plenty of comfortable threes.

They'll aim to raise their level on that side of the floor and expect Embiid's presence to be a major help once he's back in action.

"I think it's just mindset," Walker said. "We're capable when we want to. You need games like this every now and then just to remind you that it's not as easy as it feels sometimes. It takes effort every game.

"I think we can learn a lot from this. It's just part of the growing pains, but I'm confident we'll get back to that (early-season) level of defense. We have what it takes."

#2722. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2720. Great Britain overcome Germany to make first Davis Cup semi-final since 2019

Henry Patten and Neal Skupski clinched victory for Great Britain in a difficult tie against Germany to seal passage to the Davis Cup semi-finals for the first time since 2019.

It has been an eventful week for Patten, with his doubles partner Harri Heliovaara sharing his shock and dismay at the Briton's surprise decision to end their highly successful partnership after three grand slam titles together, only a few days after they were crowned year-end world No 1s.

Patten will compete with Skupski, this year's Australian and US Open champion, in 2027 and the pair immediately demonstrated their potential with an assured victory in the winner-takes-all doubles tie against Germany.

The pair beat seasoned doubles players Kevin Krawietz and Tim Puetz 6-2 7-6(6) in Bologna after world Cameron Norrie edged out Jan-Lennard Struff 6-3 3-6 6-2 and British No 1 Arthur Fery was beaten 6-3 6-2 by world No 1 Alexander Zverev.

Krawietz and Puetz are the most successful doubles pair in German Davis Cup history having won 18 of their previous 20 matches but Patten and Skupski started brilliantly before edging a tight second set.

Earlier on Thursday Norrie battled past the in-form Struff, who is ranked 13 places higher at 39, saving four break points in a crucial game at 2-2 in the deciding set before rattling off 18 of the final 21 points of the contest.

Norrie said: "I knew that my match was going to be a really important one, knowing we're playing against Germany with Sascha Zverev. I knew I had to take care of this one.

"I tried to be aggressive those two games from 2-1 up and I overplayed a little bit. But I stuck to it in that game at 2-2 being 0-40 down and then I just found my tempo.

"I locked it in. I didn't really remember the end of the match. I made every return, every ball, I was moving him side to side and I could see him struggling."

Fery had lost to Zverev twice this year, in the Wimbledon semi-finals and again in Vienna, and their head-to-head remained lopsided as the German needed just 70 minutes to level the best-of-three tie.

Germany boast the top-ranked singles player (Getty)

24-year-old Fery said: "It's tough. He's serving so well, hitting hard from the back of the court so not much time from my end to adjust. It's obviously not easy, just try and do some different things. It feels a little bit like it's all on his racket.

"He's a style of player that I struggle to play against. It's positive, I've played him three times this year. If you'd told me that on January 1, it would have been a good thing."

GB now face a semi-final tie against Spain, led by Carlos Alcaraz, who beat Austria later on Thursday.

Their star player was rested having been lined up to partner Marcel Granollers in the doubles tie, which was not needed after Daniel Merida beat Joel Schwaerzler 6-4 6-4 ​and world No 10 Rafael Jodar edged past ‌Jurij Rodionov 2-6 6-0 6-1.
