#2719. U.S. tariff-hike threats pile pressure on partners to sign trade deals

The Trump administration has unveiled a fresh tariff salvo on more than a dozen trading partners, with China, Vietnam, and India facing some of the steepest potential rates and mounting pressure to conclude trade deals with Washington.

The proposed tariffs, framed as curbing excess industrial capacity, start at 15% for the European Union, South Korea, Switzerland and Taiwan and rise to as much as 35% for Vietnam.

The U.S. Trade Representative's Office said it intends to cap the new duties at lower levels for countries that have completed trade deals or are still negotiating with Washington.

The overall impact on the region is likely to be limited, said Mark Williams, chief Asia economist at Capital Economics. The proposal "puts most of the targeted countries back where they were earlier in the year," he said, after the Supreme Court struck down Trump's emergency tariffs in February.

Still, the proposal could give some countries an incentive to speed up trade talks in order to lock in lower tariff rates, analysts said.

On Wednesday, U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer said that the new policy likely will not take effect for months, with a senior U.S. official saying the duties could take effect as early as January.

China faces a capped rate of 20%, on top of an existing 25% tariff imposed during President Donald Trump's first term. The rate could rise to 40% if the trade truce reached in South Korea last October is not extended beyond January, the USTR said.

Beijing has rejected Washington's claim of excess capacity while vowing to take measures necessary to protect its rights.

On Thursday, Chinese Commerce Ministry spokesperson He Yadong called on Washington to "rectify its wrongdoing and return to the right track of resolving issues through dialogue."

He was responding to Washington's plan to raise tariffs following its Section 301 investigations, while the ministry also urged the U.S. to implement $30 billion of reciprocal tariff cuts agreed by the two sides in late September.

The renewed tensions come despite several conciliatory meetings between Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping this year, as well as what Williams described as Trump's "apparent lack of concern" about allegations that Chinese technology firms have circumvented U.S. chip export controls.

The two leaders are expected to meet for a fourth time this year at the Group of 20 leaders summit in Miami in December.

"While not happy, Beijing seems prepared to live with the new rates provided that they don't exceed 20%," said Wendy Cutler, senior vice president at the Asia Society Policy Institute.

By retaining the option to raise levies if bilateral talks deteriorate, Washington is using the tariffs as leverage to "secure expected shipments of critical minerals and rare earths, and further purchases of U.S. agricultural goods," Cutler said.

Washington has warned that tariffs on Chinese goods could double to 40% if the USTR finds that Beijing is not engaging. "If the trade war did start up again, today's measures provide the U.S. with some ammunition," Williams said.

Even the sweeping actions against the other 13 trading partners appear aimed squarely at China, said Williams, who tallied that the word "China" appears 253 times in the USTR's report on Vietnam, and 315 times in the document on the European Union.

"Foremost among the 'unreasonable acts, policies, and practices' that these countries are criticised for is being slow to restrict imports and FDI from China," Williams said.

While the proposed tariffs generally lift average rates only modestly, India and Vietnam stand out because of the wider gaps between the rates they would face with and without a deal, analysts said.

India and Vietnam face proposed tariffs of 25% and 35%, respectively, but those rates could fall sharply to 17% and 20% if ongoing talks with the U.S. produce reciprocal trade agreements.

The two countries have "a strong incentive" to conclude deals with Washington to secure the lower rates, Cutler said.

Vietnam's trade ministry said Thursday that it was working with the U.S. to complete a trade deal as soon as possible, according to Reuters. India could also gain an edge over some Asian rivals through comparatively lower levies if New Delhi concludes its long-pending trade deal with Washington.

Singapore faces a similar challenge. The proposal sets a maximum potential rate of 20% on its exports, net of existing Section 301 tariffs, which could fall to 10% if it reaches an agreement on reciprocal trade with the U.S. or concludes other negotiations addressing the USTR's findings, according to Singapore's Ministry of Energy, Trade and Industry.

The ministry said it disagreed with the findings and was "very disappointed" with the proposed action, arguing that it failed to account for the structure of Singapore's economy.

Speaking to reporters, Deputy Prime Minister Gan Kim Yong said that about 33%, or 9.4 billion Singapore dollars ($7.2 billion) of direct exports will be affected, and the effective tariff rate on affected Singapore exports will rise to 10.3% from the current 7.8%, should Washington impose the highest levy of 20%. "I'm also very surprised that this has happened because this is not something that you would expect from an important major trading partner," Gan added, saying "We have been working together for many years, and we have an FTA with the U.S.," Gan said, referring to the U.S.-Singapore Free Trade Agreement that was in force since 2004.

Under the FTA, Singapore imposes zero tariffs on U.S. goods, while the U.S. has run a trade surplus with the city-state for more than 20 years, the ministry said. That surplus grew to $33 billion in 2025 from $27 billion in 2024.

Singapore said it would submit further comments during the public consultation period and urge the USTR to reconsider.

Exemptions for semiconductors and other electronics should cushion the impact on South Korea and Taiwan, whose economies are heavily concentrated in those sectors, analysts said.

The USTR singled out South Korea for its concentration in memory-chip production and what it described as outsized government support. Yet it excluded the country's chips from the proposed excess-capacity tariffs.

Taiwan, which faces an excess-capacity tariff cap of 15%, also benefits from exemptions for other electronics, including mobile phones and networking equipment. Its effective tariff rate would rise to about 9% from 6%, Williams estimated.

Switzerland was hit with a shock 39% tariff rate back in 2025, targeting significant exports, from chemicals and pharmaceuticals to gold, watches and chocolates. In November 2025, it negotiated a deal that slashed U.S. duties to 15% in return for a commitment to invest billions and increase manufacturing Stateside.

In March 2026, the U.S. launched further Section 301 investigations into Switzerland over "unfair or discriminatory trade practices," landing on a recommendation to impose an additional 15% tariff.

Switzerland's Economy Ministry told CNBC that the country rejected the allegations raised during the investigations and said it had already presented its arguments during bilateral consultations with the U.S. Treasury.

The ministry added that it assumed the latest recommendation was consistent with the deal struck in November 2025 and said it was in close contact with U.S. authorities as it reviews next steps.

Officials for both Switzerland and the European Union said during trade discussions last year that while they had a trade surplus with Washington in goods, the relationship was balanced when accounting for both trade and services, where the U.S. dominates in areas such as technology and finance.

Brussels said it was assessing the U.S. report but rejected its finding that Europe was a source of industrial overcapacity.

"To the contrary, Europe is among those most exposed to overcapacity," European Commission Spokesperson Olof Gill said in a statement.

"The EU and the U.S. share a real concern about global overcapacity, much of it state-driven and concentrated in third countries. We regret the lack of joint action," Gill said.

The EU expects any measure taken from the investigation to respect the existing joint agreement of a 15% all-inclusive ceiling on EU goods, Gill added.

EU trade ministers are due to speak with U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer on Monday.

Citi analysts noted they expected a hit on European retailers if the measures stay as announced, with the likes of Adidas, H&M, Puma and Primark potentially seeing downgrades on full-year 2027 earnings.

Inga Fechner, senior economist for global trade at ING, said in a Wednesday note that the U.S. "tariff wall keeps changing, but it isn't coming down," adding that "U.S. protectionism is becoming increasingly difficult to unwind."

While the newly Democrat-controlled Congress could try to restrict presidential tariff authority, remove individual duties or apply greater scrutiny via hearings and investigations, actually reversing the measures will be difficult while Trump retains veto power, she said.

"We don't think the blue wave will mark a return to free trade. The Biden years already showed that tariffs in strategically important sectors enjoy support across political camps."

Protectionist moves become more targeted, she added, adding to a complex system of country- and sector-specific duties based on different legal authorities.

— CNBC's Evelyn Cheng contributed to this report.

#2718. Norse Atlantic suspends all flights, warns it may file for bankruptcy after share price collapse

Norse Atlantic suspended all of its flights on Thursday and warned it may have to file for bankruptcy after its stock collapsed on Wednesday following its warning about its "critical" financial situation.

Oslo-listed shares of Norse closed 85% lower on Wednesday.

"The board of directors continues to consider options to secure short-term financing," Norse said in a Thursday statement. "If a short-term financing solution is not secured urgently, it is the board's intention to file for bankruptcy."

The airline added that "there can be no assurance that a viable solution will be found."

Checks by CNBC showed the airline was still selling tickets for flights on Friday and beyond as of 9 a.m. Oslo time on Thursday.

Warning signs first popped up when PK786 from London to Islamabad and PK710 from Manchester to Lahore were cancelled because of "an unexpected operational suspension," the Pakistani flag carrier said on X.

The airline had been set to operate two Pakistan International Airlines flights Wednesday under a wet-lease agreement, in which one airline provides the aircraft and crew to another. The agreement had only been signed in early October.

PIA said it would operate the routes using its own Boeing 777 aircraft from the next day.

Norse's own flight Z0 783 from London Gatwick to Orlando was also shown as canceled Wednesday on live air traffic website Flightradar24. London Gatwick's website listed the flight's status for Thursday as "enquire airline."

In a bid to stave off financial troubles, Norse cut 75 jobs in May as part of an effort to reduce costs by $50 million, after Eivind Roald replaced founder Bjorn Tore Larsen as CEO in November last year.

The Norwegian carrier said Wednesday that its situation depended on securing financing for short-term liquidity needs and that it was in discussions about a potential strategic takeover.

"Norse is also engaged in discussions with selected parties on short term financing, however, there can be no assurances that such discussions will result in a viable solution," the airline said.

The Norwegian carrier has come under strain due to soaring jet fuel costs since the onset of the Iran war.

India's largest airline, low-cost carrier IndiGo, entered an agreement with Norse Atlantic in early 2025 to lease six 787-9 aircraft as it launched flights to Europe and Britain ahead of the arrival of its own widebody planes.

The agreement concluded at the end of October this year, with IndiGo citing airspace constraints, elevated fuel costs and currency pressures.

At its peak in 2024, Norse Atlantic had 4,822 scheduled flights. That number dipped to 4,695 in 2025 and 2,565 in 2026, according to aviation analytics company Cirium. The airline had 122 flights scheduled for November this year.

Norse Atlantic CEO Roald told CNBC in May that he was not "overly concerned" about access to fuel and said the airline had no indication at the time that the crisis would have an immediate impact. The airline said in October that it was continuing to operate at reduced capacity because of "prolonged high fuel prices."

Correction: This story was updated to reflect that Norse shares closed 85% lower on Wednesday.

#2717. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

#2716. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2715. Nvidia sellers seek new buyers as U.S. curbs China AI chip access, sources tell CNBC

Nvidia chip sellers are seeking new buyers for the semiconductor giant's hardware, three people told CNBC, after the Trump administration tightened rules to curb Chinese companies' remote access to the most advanced artificial-intelligence systems.

Companies have put an unusually high volume of Nvidia graphics processing units, or GPUs, on the market since new measures restricting Chinese companies renting access to advanced Nvidia chips were announced on Nov. 16, the people said.

Jack Duhamel, president of AI infrastructure marketplace GPU Mart, and two people familiar with the matter told CNBC the surge in GPUs for sale was directly attributable to the new rule. Duhamel added that one company looking to sell Nvidia chips told GPU Mart the sale was related to the new rule.

The two people said it appeared chip sellers were seeking to offload them because they were originally for use in data centers in which Chinese companies would rent access. They asked not to be named because they were discussing private information.

The Department of Commerce's Bureau of Industry and Security, or BIS, announced an interim final rule as part of a clampdown on Chinese companies bypassing export controls, which effectively ban the sale of the most advanced Nvidia chips to China, by accessing them remotely in overseas data centers. The rule came into effect on Nov. 19.

Deals to find buyers for thousands of B300 GPUs, one of Nvidia's most advanced offerings, have been floated since the rule was announced, said Duhamel and one of the people.

Nvidia declined to comment. BIS did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

Nvidia has been facing increasing scrutiny from U.S. lawmakers over Chinese companies' access to its advanced chips.

The chip giant warned its customers that the proposed Remote Access Security Act — which has passed the House of Representatives but is yet to pass the Senate — would give the U.S. government broader authority to control access to cloud services, potentially exposing both parties to "legal action," sources previously told CNBC.

In July, the U.S. accused a Chinese AI lab, Moonshot AI, of accessing Nvidia chips via a facility in Thailand. CNBC reported on Nov. 5 that the company had sought access to the chips via data centers in Europe.

U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Wednesday that the new rule announced last week was intended to close "loopholes."

"For a long time, we have not allowed the sale of Blackwell chips, which are the highest AI chips or similar chips to China directly," he said.

"Some rules were issued by the Commerce Department last week to close off indirect access, either smuggling through places like Southeast Asia or remote access from data centers in third countries."

During Nvidia's third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 17, then-interim CEO Colette Kress said the company was still studying the controls, but expected performance to remain strong because demand for chips far exceeded supply.

#2714. ‘Your booth is waiting’: Denny’s welcomes Jensen Huang back as Nvidia CEO

Jensen Huang is back at the helm of Nvidia — and Denny's has a booth waiting for him.

The American diner chain welcomed the billionaire's return as Nvidia CEO by revealing that it had reinstated a commemorative plaque marking the birthplace of the artificial intelligence chip giant after learning of its removal on Nov. 13.

"Some things you just don't take off the menu. Jensen Huang is one of them," Denny's said in a post on X.

The plaque marks the booth at a Denny's restaurant in Silicon Valley, California, where Huang and his co-founders discussed the idea for Nvidia, which was founded in 1993.

The restaurant chain said it learned on Nov. 13, a day after Huang's resignation, that the plaque had been removed.

"We put the plaque right back up where it belongs," Denny's said, without specifying who had removed it or why.

Huang's ties to the diner chain, however, stretch back well before Nvidia's founding. He worked at Denny's as a teenager, washing dishes and serving customers before going on to build one of the world's most valuable companies.

The restaurant chain also disclosed that it had contacted Nvidia's communications team on Nov. 10 to express its pride in their shared history.

Huang's return came less than two weeks after his sudden resignation rattled investors and raised questions about corporate governance and succession planning at the chipmaker.

While his return has put those issues in the spotlight, Denny's offered a simpler message to its former employee.

"Jensen, your booth is waiting and the coffee's on. We're always open, 24/7," the restaurant chain said.

#2712. Ragtag Dolphins defensive line holding together through slew of injuries

MIAMI GARDENS — The Miami Dolphins defensive line could be a disaster after losing defensive tackles Zach Sieler, Jordan Phillips and Zeek Biggers to season-ending injuries.

But through all that attrition, a group needing 2025 first-round pick Kenneth Grant and a few unknown defensive tackles to step up, held up strong in its first game so heavily depleted at the position.

And that was against the NFL's No. 2 rushing attack in the Buffalo Bills. They were held to 95 yards rushing and a 3.5 average of yards per attempt.

"I think it was a big team effort," Dolphins coach Jeff Hafley said this week. "From the front guys, the guys on the edge, they played a lot more snaps than they were used to. And then the interior, they stepped up."

Grant and fellow starting defensive tackle Keith Cooper Jr. each played more than 50 snaps, which is no easy task at such a physically demanding position.

"A tremendous credit to them and really the physical shape they were in and the mental shape they were in to do that," defensive coordinator Sean Duggan said.

Grant had been working his way to be able to withstand that kind of workload. He started the season on injured reserve for a toe injury and, after missing the first four games, had been steadily increasing his snap counts.

"In the moment, it didn't even feel like 50," Grant said. "But after, I definitely felt it after. I had to get my body right. I'm pretty good now, shook back and ready to roll."

Grant had two tackles, but he was around the ball often, and his work consuming blocks certainly played a role in allowing rookie linebacker Jacob Rodriguez go for 13 tackles.

"I thought he was active," Dolphins defensive line coach Austin Clark said. "Pursuit to the football. He was around the ball a lot. He was into it. Cooper, who was only brought back off the Carolina Panthers practice squad this month after making the initial 53-man roster off a strong training camp but then being cut in late September, also affected quarterback Josh Allen on a few dropbacks. Cooper sacked Allen once and hit him on another throw.

Said Duggan of Cooper's fierce physicality: "I think he played with the play style we want to play with here. It's something we stress a lot, is we want to play the game a certain way on defense. We want to be known for the way we play as the Miami Dolphins, and he kind of embodied that on Sunday."

Miami's starting defensive ends, Chop Robinson and Josh Uche, like Grant and Cooper, also played 50-plus snaps. Robinson had a hit on Allen that led to the Rodriguez interception. Robinson also lined up slightly differently, closer to the line to help the defensive tackles.

The group executed a clear game plan of containing Allen in the pocket and keeping him behind the line of scrimmage to force him to throw. Allen didn't rush one time against the Dolphins last Sunday, and he consistently put the ball in harm's way.

With high usage for Miami's starting defensive linemen, it's worth monitoring if they can continue to hold up down the stretch playing so many snaps, starting this Sunday against the New York Jets at home.

Still listed as limited were: tackle Austin Jackson (elbow/knee), safety Dante Trader Jr. (shoulder/groin), edge defender Rodney McGraw (shoulder) and linebackers Jackson Woodard (hand) and Ronnie Harrison Jr. (hamstring). Harrison is in his first week of practice back as he is currently on IR.

Wide receiver Omar Cooper Jr. did not practice through his ankle and groin ailments. Edge rusher Joseph Ossai (ankle/foot) and offensive lineman Dylan Parham (knee) were limited. Wide receiver and kick return specialist Isaiah Williams (ribs) improved from limited to full participation.

#2711. Switzerland’s biggest bank set for showdown with lawmakers as capital fight intensifies amid crisis planning

UBS's crisis planning has won qualified support from market regulators, which called its plan "executable" in principle. But Switzerland's biggest bank still faces a major fight over sweeping plans that would force it to hold more capital against its foreign subsidiaries.

FINMA, the Swiss markets regulator, said Wednesday that UBS's resolvability — or its ability to fail safely, without wreaking havoc on the economy — has improved since it integrated rival Credit Suisse, with progress made in implementing the emergency plan across group-wide resolution planning.

The preferred resolution strategy — involving plans to allow for recapitalization and restructuring through the use of loss-absorbing bail-in bonds — is "executable," FINMA said. The regulator formally approved UBS's recovery plan for the first time since its ​Credit Suisse integration.

How UBS would withstand a future crisis remains in sharp focus among Swiss authorities ever since the lender's emergency takeover of stricken rival Credit Suisse in 2023.

UBS is now the country's only global systemically important bank, and the Swiss government wants it to hold stronger capital cushions, specifically in relation to its foreign subsidiaries, which would help it withstand another banking collapse.

UBS has pushed back against the overhaul, saying the measures are disproportionate and would damage its international competitiveness, forcing it to hold an extra $16 billion in capital.

A parliamentary showdown now looms, with the National Council, Switzerland's lower house, scheduled to debate the proposal Dec. 16.

On Tuesday, the lower house's economics committee backed plans to force the bank to support its foreign subsidiaries with 90% high-quality core Common Equity Tier 1, or CET1, capital. The Council of States, the country's upper house, approved the 90% plan in September.

The Swiss government had earlier proposed a 100% threshold. UBS wants it to be no more than 50%.

UBS CEO Sergio Ermotti told CNBC in an interview Oct. 28 that 90% would not allow the company to be a "competitive player" long-term. But he would not be drawn on speculation that the measures could push the bank to relocate out of Switzerland.

In a statement, the bank said the economics committee's recommendation would herald further excessive tightening of Swiss capital requirements, "which are already among the most stringent globally."

FINMA said in its Nov. 25 update that the goal is to avoid government solvency support and emergency law.

"The aim is to enable the authorities, in the event of a crisis, to choose the option that offers the best prospects of success in safeguarding financial stability and ensuring the continuation of systemically important functions," the regulator said.

#2710. What is ‘texture-maxxing’? The food trend putting crunch on the menu

Texture-maxxing is the latest food trend taking social media by storm as consumers, driven by aesthetics and taste, look for a sensory experience.

British supermarket Waitrose's latest food and drink report highlighted "texture-maxxing" as one of the trends of the year. It describes the phenomenon as a multi-sensory indulgence that is "fuelled by texture-maxxing crunch and intense flavour hits," pushing shoppers to fill their trolleys with crunchy peanut butter, dumplings, and trifles.

"This year, the sound of food went mainstream," Waitrose's Senior Development Chef Will Torrent said in the Wednesday report. "Driven by social media's obsession with audible, multi-sensory cooking, we're creating high-contrast meals that combine shatter-crisp crunch with pillowy chew."

"The emphasis in social media content has flipped from narrative voiceovers to ASMR-inspired audio, where the evocative sounds of food are just as important as the visuals," he added.

Food trends on social media have ranged from the obvious to the absurd. Fibermaxxing and proteinmaxxing have taken off in the past few years. Meanwhile, food and beverages like Dubai chocolate, protein coffee, and ube were some of the big social media hits.

Nestlé's USA 2026 trends found that 75% of Gen Z and 80% of millennials sought out texture in snack cravings with the hashtag "crunchtok," amassing 1.5 billion views.

Waitrose noted that shoppers looking to maximize on texture bought its viral No.1 Alphonso Mango Dessert, which involves "cracking through the crisp shell" to access a "smooth mango mousse and passion fruit curd on a crunchy white chocolate base."

Waitrose's TikTok and Instagram videos of eating the dessert were watched by more than two million people, with a whopping 6,173 hours and 36 minutes of play time on TikTok. Its videos featuring crunchy toast also went viral.

The U.K. grocer's sales of jarred chili crunches, which include crispy rayu oils such as itsu Crispy Chilli Oil, saw a 13-fold jump. Meanwhile, crunchy peanut butter outsold smooth.

Dumplings also had a moment, with sales of chicken gyoza dumplings up 116% this year and searches on waitrose.com for gochujang and peanut butter udon noodles jumped 60%.

"We've seen an explosion in demand as shoppers treat dumplings not just as a quick weeknight bite, but as a total sensory experience," Waitrose's Executive Chef Stephen Parkins-Knight said.

"Driven by the rise of texture-maxxing, people want that ultimate contrast: delicate skins, juicy fillings and crispy skirts, topped with very chilli crunch. With innovation ranging from fusion fillings to rich dipping broths, dumplings have taken a delicious centre stage in 2026," he added.

Texture-maxxing also extended to desserts, as shoppers bought twice as many chocolate trifles compared to 2025, and sales of strawberry trifles increased 160%.

Tiramisu was also popular, with searches for waitrose.com's tiramisu recipe page increasing 155% year-on-year.

#2709. LeBron Log No. 6: A Rocky night, a shooting lesson and a fight over ‘Bron'

LeBron Log No. 6: A Rocky night, a shooting lesson and a fight over 'Bron' originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

It was another eventful week for LeBron James.

That's why we started the LeBron Log in the first place.

The King spent his latest week taking his teammates to the movies, fighting over his name in federal court and producing his best three-point shooting performance as a Sixer days after recording a podcast about the craft.

This week also marks 20 years since James faced the Sixers alongside Allen Iverson.

James found an appropriate choice for the Sixers' latest team outing.

He organized a private screening of I Play Rocky on Friday night for players, their families and members of the basketball operations staff. The film follows Sylvester Stallone's fight to get the original Rocky made while insisting that he play the title character.

"I mean, it's Rocky. We in Philly," James said. "I heard it was a great movie, and we had a night off. I've got a few connections, and I was able to bring that to my teammates and this organization for us to watch it. I thought it was a great movie. Unbelievably cast. Everyone looked like exactly who they were portraying. That is Hollywood for you. It was pretty cool."

The Sixers shared photos of James alongside Jaylen Brown, Kentavious Caldwell-Pope, Labaron Philon Jr., Jabari Walker, Tacko Fall and Adem Bona.

"Movie night, courtesy of LeBron," the team wrote.

The nationwide release of I Play Rocky came less than two weeks before Philadelphia celebrates the 50th anniversary of the original film on Dec. 3.

Nick Nurse could not attend because of a previous commitment, but he received positive reviews from his players.

"They went, they said it was very good, they loved it," Nurse said. "The big takeaway was how much Anthony actually looked like Rocky. Or Sylvester Stallone. One in the same."

James' first season in Philadelphia now includes a private Rocky screening. A trip up the Art Museum steps remains available for a future edition.

James and Steve Nash devoted the latest episode of their Mind the Game podcast to the art of shooting.

The two examined shooting form, confidence, repetitions, free throws and some of their favorite jumpers. James also revealed one detail that can affect what he does after catching the ball.

"I'm a guy who don't like shooting against the laces," James said. "So sometimes I'll come up on a quick stop and I'm about to raise and I can feel the laces not fixed. I'll turn that into a pump fake."

The episode was released Nov. 17, while James was experiencing an extended struggle from three-point range. It did not provide an instant fix.

James went 1 for 5 from three against Orlando and missed his lone attempt against Milwaukee. He entered Monday night's meeting with Portland shooting 21.7 percent from deep through his first 13 games.

James made all three of his first-half attempts from three. He ended the second quarter by pump-faking, stepping to his left and beating the buzzer with a corner three that tied the game at 69.

Nobody could see where the laces sat on the catch. The pump fake was clear.

James finished 4 for 5 from three and scored a season-high 23 points on 8-for-13 shooting. It was his third consecutive game with at least 20 points, following 22 against Orlando and 21 against Milwaukee.

The Sixers lost to Portland, 128-111, but James produced his best long-range performance since joining them.

Six days after discussing the art of shooting, he provided a brief demonstration.

James is also attempting to protect one of his most recognizable nicknames.

James and LBJ Trademarks LLC filed a federal lawsuit on Nov. 17 against PlayVision Inc. and its founders over a sports-betting research app initially called "Bron."

The complaint alleges that the company used an AI-generated version of James in an advertisement without his permission. The figure wore a No. 23 jersey with "Bron" across the back and resembled James through its voice, build and tattoos.

It also scrolled through wagers involving NBA players.

The lawsuit alleges that the advertisement falsely created the impression that James endorsed the app and suggested that he considered betting on NBA games. The complaint said the video received roughly 1 million views before it was removed.

"The integrity of NBA competition, and the public's confidence that players are not betting on the games they play, is of paramount importance to the league, its players and its fans," the complaint said. "Any suggestion that an active NBA player is betting on NBA games is therefore far from a trivial matter, and it should not be used for clicks and views."

The app offered odds comparisons, betting trends and tracking tools but did not directly accept wagers, according to its developer.

PlayVision renamed it "Evie" on Nov. 18, one day after James filed the lawsuit. James is seeking an injunction and unspecified damages. The case remains active in the Southern District of New York.

CLEVELAND – NOVEMBER 25: LeBron James #23 of the Cleveland Cavaliers looks to pass off when double teamed by the Philadelphia 76ers November 25, 2006 at The Quicken Loans Arena in Cleveland, Ohio. Copyright 2006 NBAE (Photo by David Liam Kyle/NBAE via Getty Images)

James spent Thanksgiving weekend in 2006 on the other side of the Sixers-Cavaliers matchup.

On Nov. 25, 2006, a 21-year-old James recorded 25 points, 11 assists, four rebounds and one block in Cleveland's 108-95 win over Philadelphia.

Donning the Cavs' old-school orange threads, he played nearly 41 minutes.

Iverson led the Sixers with 31 points. Andre Iguodala added 17 points and eight rebounds, while Kyle Korver made three three-pointers off the bench.

James recorded 11 assists that night. Nearly 20 years later, another 11-assist performance against Indiana moved him past Jason Kidd for third place in NBA history.

The 2006 meeting also came near the end of Iverson's original run with Philadelphia. The Sixers traded him to Denver less than a month later.

James was 21. Iverson was nearing his departure.

Twenty years later, James is wearing a Sixers uniform and Iverson's No. 3 hangs above his home floor.

#2708. Planes, trains and automobiles: The best ways to beat travel fatigue—so you can come home feeling rested and fulfilled

Five-hour flights. Eleven-hour road trips. Fifteen-hour train rides. All potentially exhausting options for the annual tradition of convening around the dinner table with loved ones on Thanksgiving.

An estimated 81.5 million Americans are projected to travel, or have already traveled, at least 50 miles from their homes between Nov. 24 and Nov. 30, according to the American Automobile Association. And their mode of transportation is shifting: Fewer people are flying or driving this year compared to 2025, and more are traveling by buses, trains and cruises, AAA data shows.

High gas prices and soaring plane ticket costs may factor into the shift. As of Wednesday evening, fuel prices are roughly 17% higher than they were on the same date in 2025, according to AAA data. Meanwhile, "months of elevated jet fuel costs due to the conflict in Iran have contributed to Thanksgiving and Christmas airfares reaching 10-year highs," according to fare-tracking platform Hopper.

The alternate modes of transit may open travelers up to new experiences — and new forms of travel fatigue to keep you from returning home feeling rested and fulfilled. You're probably already familiar with the exhaustion you might get from a day of plane travel, for example. In airplanes, "there can be lower humidity, maybe lower oxygen levels at cabin pressure. Sometimes people report feeling more dehydrated or drained. You're adjusting to that change in altitude," says Alaina Tiani, a Cleveland Clinic behavioral sleep medicine psychologist.

But even waiting in places like train stations or cruise ports can feel draining due to sheer sensory overload, Tiani says. And then, depending on your mode of travel, you may still need to deal with cramped seating and limited ability to get up and walk around or drink some water and eat a snack.

Once you arrive at your destination, managing responsibilities like remembering where you put your hotel key and deciding what to eat and how to structure your day can also take a mental toll, says travel writer Shannon O'Donnell, who's written about managing travel fatigue. "All of that actually requires a lot more thinking and planning for your brain," she says, adding: "People just don't understand how those add up and why a relaxing vacation suddenly has them feeling wrecked."

The consequences can sneak up on you, O'Donnell notes. Travel fatigue can "present as maybe a lack of interest in something that you might usually find fascinating," she says. Instead of looking out the window on a plane ride or playing car games with your kids on a road trip, you might disengage and appear apathetic to the people around you.

You might stand a better chance of a fulfilling, restful holiday if you prepare for your travel fatigue. Here, Tiani and O'Donnell share their advice for how to do it:

#2707. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2706. Trump, NATO chief caution Russia as attacks on Ukraine’s energy infrastructure intensify

U.S. President Donald Trump and NATO chief Mark Rutte issued a joint statement on Thursday urging Russia not to take any actions, intentionally or otherwise, that would further expand the Ukraine war.

The statement comes as Ukraine's allies sound the alarm over Russia's growing threat to European security and just one week after Trump's historic trilateral meeting with Russian President Vladimir Putin and Chinese President Xi Jinping in Asia.

Trump and Rutte described their meeting at the Mar-a-Lago club in Palm Beach, Florida on Wednesday as "very impactful," with Russia's nearly five-year-long war in Ukraine and the Iran war among the key topics of conversation.

"We also discussed the importance of Russia avoiding any actions that intentionally, or unintentionally, expand the war, and the importance of obtaining a peaceful resolution to the war as rapidly as possible," Trump and Rutte said in their statement.

Moreover, the two discussed the importance of further increasing defense spending as well as investment in the defense industrial base in both the U.S. and NATO countries, according to the statement.

Trump also said in a post on Truth Social Thursday that he "briefed NATO on the tremendous progress" being made in growing defense production, noting that the focus will be on building Patriot air defense systems, THAAD systems and Tomahawk cruise missiles, as well as "many new Weapons, both Offensive and Defensive, that have not yet been viewed by the Public, or other Countries."

"There has never been a time when the United States has produced more Military Equipment or Munitions than now. Our 'cupboard' will soon be full and, when it is, our Allies and Partners throughout the World will be benefitting greatly," he said in the post.

Trump's meeting with Xi and Putin, in which he described the Russian president as his "friend," sparked alarm among European lawmakers about Washington's commitment to transatlantic security.

NATO's Rutte, a seasoned diplomat known as a consensus builder during his nearly 14 years as Dutch prime minister, has repeatedly sought to de-escalate tensions in the alliance and frequently used flattery to win over the U.S. president.

While some have questioned whether this approach has delivered any tangible benefits, Trump has previously described Rutte as a "great leader" and NATO's "biggest asset."

Russia has stepped up its attacks on Ukraine's energy infrastructure in recent weeks, with further attacks on the central Ukrainian city of Kryvyi Rih overnight disrupting power supplies and bringing municipal electric public transport to a standstill, according to Oleksandr Vilkul, head of Kryvyi ​Rih's defense council.

Ukrainian officials on Wednesday said Russian attacks on the country's power sector had damaged energy infrastructure and caused power outages in five regions as cold winter weather sets in.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz on Wednesday accused the Kremlin of "escalating" threats toward European security, citing actions in the Baltic region, Poland and on the Black Sea, and adding that "Russia knows exactly what it wants. And Russia wants more than Ukraine."

A spokesperson for Russia's Foreign Ministry did not immediately respond to a CNBC request for comment on Thursday morning. The Kremlin has repeatedly denied seeking military confrontation with NATO countries.

#2705. Why isn’t the Las Vegas Raiders defense good enough

Stop me if you've heard this before, but the Las Vegas Raiders defense has got a lot of growing to do to catch up with the offense. They have come within one score of, or surpassed, the 40-point threshold for the sixth time in 11 games.

Head coach Klint Kubiak said they win as a team and lose as a team; refusing to point fingers is noble and expected of the head coach. Defensive coordinator Rob Leonard elected not to take the high road, instead making a snide comment: "Fatigue makes cowards of us all…when you're on the field a long time, mistakes tend to happen more often." But here, we aren't limited by nobility; Leonard would have gotten a pass until he decided to open his mouth and speak on an offense ranking 29th in time of possession but 6th in total points.

So let us start by looking at who has been truly responsible for this lackluster defense. Leonard is the one calling the defensive plays and maintaining a league-worst defensive efficiency allowed, a paltry 48.1% defensive success rate and a 15.9% explosive play rate.

No one is doing jack up front for the Raiders defensive line outside of Maxx Crosby. Over the last three weeks, the Raiders defensive line has 2.5 sacks, five tackles for loss, seven quarterback hits, 1 forced fumble and two pass breakups. Crosby is the owner of every single one of those stats except one quarterback hit from Kwity Paye, Malcolm Koonce and Tonka Hemingway, along with the 0.5 sack, forced fumble and two pass breakups that Folorunso Fatukasi has produced.

Kwity Paye, a high-priced free agent acquisition, hasn't had a sack since Week 6 at home against the Buffalo Bills. Coincidentally, that happened to be the date of his last tackle for loss as well. His last quarterback hit was against Brock Purdy in Week 9. Hardly worthy of his lofty three-year, $48 million ($16 million average) contract.

Cutting him with a June-1 designation saves roughly $6.01 million, with $16.824 million (one year's salary) dead cap. At the rate of one sack every 5-6 weeks, one quarterback hit roughly every 2-3 weeks and a tackle for loss every six weeks, the rubber is going to have to meet the road at some point.

Malcolm Koonce, last year Koonce got a pass for coming off an injured knee and was kept over a more deserving K'Lavon Chaisson. After receiving a good-faith one-year $11.01 million contract, his good start to the 2026 season has faded into oblivion along with his productivity. Over the last three games, he has two tackles and a quarterback hit.

This season, he has accounted for 12 tackles, seven quarterback hits, three sacks, two forced fumbles and 1 tackle for loss. His last 0.5 sack was in Week 5 against the New England Patriots; the quarterback hit against the Denver Broncos on Sunday was his first impact as a rusher in six weeks.

Adam Butler, according to Pro Football Focus, ranks 84th out of 134 qualified interior defensive linemen. Strangely enough, Butler's best grade is as a run defender, ranking 49th of 134 and then comes the fall. His 21 total tackles are outside the Top 50 defensive tackles; his 0.5 sacks are outside the Top 75 defensive tackles; his seven total quarterback pressures are in the bottom third of qualifying defensive interiors.

Eric Stokes has played so poorly as of late that it is baffling how he is still starting. At this point, he has been every opponent's favorite target; more so than even the rookies. When he isn't giving up on plays, he is getting beaten like a drum. Stokes is allowing a passer rating of 115.2, making him the 9th-worst cover corner in the league coming out of week 10. What's worse is that number has been steadily climbing over the last three weeks. In weeks 8-10, he posted a passer rating of 116.1. Additionally, when it comes to penalties, he has four accepted pass interference calls resulting in 58 yards; the first being tied for 5th most and the latter being the 5th most in the league. Stokes may have five pass breakups this season, but he still hasn't recorded an interception since week 4 of his rookie season.

Hezekiah Masses, Jermod McCoy, Darien Porter: All three are rookie cornerbacks who must be afforded an opportunity to learn their craft and experience bumps and bruises along the way. Before blaming them for all the explosive plays generated, we have to understand they are being asked to cover for a league-worst 3.0 seconds to throw and 4th-worst in pocket time allowed, 2.72 seconds.

Individually, they have all experienced success in one way, shape, or form. All of these young defensive backs need to get in the weight room and transform their bodies from college to the pros. Getting acclimated to the league is a process and all of these young players have shown promise.

Masses has three interceptions this year but is still searching for a permanent positional home. Slightly undersized, he has the heart of a lion but needs a good NFL strength and development plan in the offseason. Out wide, he has three interceptions, but his productivity in the slot has been an improvement over veteran Taron Johnson, which offers positional versatility.

He is a better man-coverage corner than soft zone but can do both. A ferocious blitzer who can get home when activated but is still too small to be a nickel linebacker. One of the few blemishes has been his ability to hold contain when he's the last defender on the line.

Porter the second-year cornerback, is a bit of an enigma. Unable to keep his starting job locked down from Masses and McCoy, he hasn't been bad and has proven that, if utilized correctly, he can be an asset to the defense. Porter is a converted wide receiver with size, speed and length who specializes in press-man coverage.

However, the main issue is the press-bail and zone technique he is being asked to play is a style which isn't conducive to what he does best. He is a good and willing tackler, better suited to larger, less quick-twitch receivers. Opposite of Stokes, he is the second-best tackler out past the edges.

McCoy, when healthy, has the tools to be the best of the bunch. Watching him stay balanced, controlled and locate the football was a sight for sore eyes. Playing the ball all the way through the catching process and being able to punch the ball out is what the Raiders need. Tackling in the run game was a problem against the Broncos. All in all, for a rookie making his second start, there are some encouraging signs but also plenty of room for improvement.

Enough can't be said about how disappointing the run defense has been. This season, the 16 rushing touchdowns allowed are tied for the most in the league. The Raiders are allowing 149.5 yards per game, ranking 31st, and 4.82 yards per carry, ranking 30th in the league. Surprisingly, the 51 missed tackles are middle of the pack, so the reality is they are getting outmuscled.

Against the Broncos, the Raiders lost three defensive tackles to injury. Whether they are able to play in the Sunday Night Game against the Cleveland Browns remains to be seen. If they are not, the run defense stands to be even worse.

#2704. Global stocks rally as investors shrug off U.S. tariffs and AI jitters to 'fish for bargains'

Global stocks rallied on Thursday, staging a tentative recovery from an intense AI-driven sell-off and looked past a fresh set of tariffs unveiled by the White House on Wednesday.

U.S. markets will be closed Thursday for the Thanksgiving holiday and will have a shortened trading session on Friday. During Wednesday's regular session, the Dow gained 0.33%, the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite fell 0.62% and the S&P 500 shed 0.25%, with the major averages paring losses seen earlier in the trading day.

In Europe on Thursday, equities rose sharply. The pan-European Stoxx 600 was 1.6% higher by 12:20 p.m. in London (7:20 a.m. ET), with all sectors and major bourses in positive territory.

Germany's DAX led gains on a rise of 1.9%, while the Stoxx 600 Technology index jumped 2.5%. The regional tech index has lost around 12% so far this month, amid jitters around a potential AI bubble bursting and the ousting — and return — of Jensen Huang as Nvidia CEO.

German semiconductor firm Infineon was more than 4% higher in afternoon trading, as chip stocks notched a boost. ASMI and ASML each gained just over 3%, while Be Semiconductor added 3.5%.

Asia-Pacific markets also rose on Thursday, with regional tech stocks also rebounded sharply, led by Japanese chip-related companies.

South Korea's Kospi gained 0.29% to close at 4,711.21, while the small-cap Kosdaq index advanced 0.78% to 655.71 after the country's central bank hiked benchmark lending rates to a 2-year high in its final meeting of the year.

Japan's Nikkei 225 rose 0.86% to 57,499.16 while the Topix edged 0.11% higher to 3,750.4. Australia's S&P/ASX 200 gained 0.82% to 8,763.8.

Hong Kong's Hang Seng index was up 1.52% in its last hour of trade, while mainland China's CSI 300 edged higher marginally, closing at 3,900.55.

SoftBank Group surged more than 7%, while chip-testing equipment maker Advantest rose 4.57%. Lasertec and Disco gained , while Tokyo Electron climbed 0.59%

In South Korea, memory chip giants SK Hynix and Samsung Electronics advanced more than 2% and 0.35%, respectively. The gains followed a bruising session Wednesday amid renewed concerns over the sustainability of artificial intelligence spending.

Russ Mould, investment director at AJ Bell, labeled it a "solid day" for regional markets, suggesting investors were hunting for opportunities in the wake of an intense global sell-off.

"The rollercoaster stock market is back on an upwards path as a new round of Trump tariffs aren't as bad as feared and the US takes the day off to celebrate Thanksgiving," he said in a note on Thursday morning. "It was notable that investors cast their net wide for opportunities, fishing for bargains in the rubble following the tech sell-off and looking at other sectors in the quest for diversification."

But Mould warned investors not to be "too complacent."

"Oil prices heading higher is not good for efforts to tame inflation, neither is the prospect of retaliation by countries on the receiving end of new US tariffs," he said.

The Trump administration on Wednesday unveiled a fresh barrage of tariffs on more than a dozen trading partners, including China, the European Union and India.

Daniela Hathorn, senior financial market analyst at Capital.com, said in a Thursday note that the mood in markets was "cautious rather than uniformly negative."

"European markets are attempting to rebound after yesterday's decline, while U.S. cash markets are closed for Thanksgiving, limiting the chance to test whether Wall Street's late recovery can hold," she said. "The main focus now will be trying to determine how many further increases the Fed considers necessary in early 2027 and whether AI shares can withstand that rate path while investors continue to question the returns on heavy spending."

U.S. inflation data on Wednesday reinforced expectations of further monetary tightening by the Federal Reserve. The personal consumption expenditures price index rose 3.8% year on year in October, accelerating from 3.7% in September, while core inflation came in slightly above expectations at 3.3%.

Mark Haefele, chief investment officer at UBS Global Wealth Management, said in a note on Thursday morning that his team believes investors should maintain strategic allocations to equities.

But he advised market participants to "review current allocations and use market moves as an opportunity to rebalance, refocus, and strengthen portfolio resilience."

#2703. Alabama Shakes up Thanksgiving 2026 With Massive Moves Before Auburn Game

The Alabama Crimson Tide snatched the Thanksgiving spotlight on the recruiting trail. All before taking on Iron Bowl rival Auburn on Saturday. Coach Kalen DeBoer and his staff reeled in not one, but two new verbal commits.

And a third could be on the horizon too after the holiday, per one national recruiting insider.

The Crimson Tide's recruiting blitz launched Wednesday evening. Alabama flipped Minnesota cornerback commit Jyden German to boost the defensive class for the 2027 recruiting cycle.

But 'Bama wasn't through with reeling in the Fort Myers, Florida talent German. Alabama energized Tide fans on the holiday by landing a past Defensive Player of the Year winner from the junior college football ranks.

Edge rusher Dylan Gill out of Hutchinson Community College in Kansas is on board for this class. Gill made his decision in the early morning hours of Thanksgiving.

German is the son of Miami star Jammi German. But he dove into why Alabama became home in the end with Blake Byler of On3/Rivals.

"I fit what they need in the secondary and they have made that clear to me. For me, it's just the amount of professionalism. They are very professional program who preach and practice excellence," German said.

Gill admitted to On3 that 'Bama was a childhood dream school.

"Alabama has always been a childhood team that everybody wants to play at one day. I want to learn from the best and be coached by the best. SEC football is different. That's another big reason, SEC football is just different," Gill said.

Between the two, it's Gill who'll have three more years of eligibility left when he arrives to Tuscaloosa. And Gill is also a Miami native too, handing DeBoer and Alabama two 305 region talents. Except Gill may not be the only JUCO talent to come off the recruiting board during the final weekend of Nov. 2026.

The Tide are expecting one more prominent visitor for the famed Iron Bowl showdown. This one also comes with Miami ties, but is a past five-star signing for the Hurricanes who also needed to go the junior college route.

"Alabama is still pursuing a second JUCO prospect this cycle — Long Beach City College edge rusher Hayden Lowe, who is scheduled for an official visit this coming weekend," Byler said.

Lowe rose to five-star status for Oaks Christian High School in Westlake Village, California. He also briefly committed to USC before Hurricanes edge rusher coach Jason Taylor helped flip him to Miami.

Now Alabama is positioned to pull off the massive hat trick on the recruiting trail if the Tide completes the sales pitch for Lowe.

#2702. SpaceX, Musk pledge to donate a share each to active Trump Accounts

SpaceX said Wednesday it will donate one share of the company's class A stock to every active Trump Account, and CEO Elon Musk would personally match the gift.

"SpaceX is building the infrastructure of the future and working to make life multiplanetary," the company said in a statement. "We believe ownership of the company creating these systems should extend to the next generation of Americans for their long-term benefit."

The White House announced in October that nearly 70 million Trump Accounts had been created, including more than 60 million through auto-enrollment. It's unclear how many are considered active.

Musk said in a social media post on X on Thursday that the share from SpaceX would be transferred next month, without providing further details. "The share directly from me will be transferred when my lockup expires in June next year," he added.

Eligible children must have their account claimed to enable contributions from family members, friends and employers, and to receive the government's one-time $1,000 seed contribution. Auto-enrolled children can receive philanthropic contributions without a parent or guardian claiming an account, according to TrumpAccounts.gov.

Representatives for SpaceX and the U.S. Department of the Treasury did not immediately respond to a request for further details about the planned gift.

SpaceX Chief Operating Officer Gwynne Shotwell had previously agreed to make a personal gift of over 2 million SpaceX shares to Trump Accounts, with eligible account holders expected to receive them in December.

In October, Elon Musk posted on X that he would give a share of SpaceX to every kid in America next year.

Trump Accounts, also known as 530A accounts, are open to any U.S. child under 18 with a Social Security number.

Parents, guardians, grandparents and others can collectively contribute up to $5,000 per child per year in after-tax dollars up until the year before the beneficiary turns 18. The annual contribution limit is indexed for inflation after 2027.

Since the July 4 launch, families and friends have collectively contributed more than $1 billion to Trump Accounts. Total deposits now sit at more than $9 billion, CNBC first reported.

Children born from 2025 through 2028 may receive a one-time $1,000 deposit from the Treasury. Other funds may also be available, depending on certain criteria.

The Treasury rolled out temporary regulations at the end of September that allow Trump Accounts to hold stock donations, a move officials have said is meant to encourage more philanthropic gifts.

Contributing stock can benefit donors, who are able to avoid capital gains taxes triggered by selling appreciated stock. But families can face restrictions related to selling that donated stock. The policy also means some children's wealth accumulation could be tied to the performance of individual companies.

Under U.S. Department of the Treasury guidelines, families cannot refuse stock gifted to Trump Accounts and generally cannot sell it until the earlier of five years or the end of the calendar year in which the beneficiary turns 17.

SpaceX shares have been under some pricing pressure as the company negotiates a possible merger with Elon Musk's automaker Tesla. The companies have established two special committees to handle merger negotiations, and hired advisers for a potential mega-deal.

CNBC's Sam Meredith, Kevin Breuninger, Kate Dore and Jess Dickler contributed to this story
