#2701. Spain’s Pablo Hernández de Cos set to lead European Central Bank

Pablo Hernández de Cos is set to succeed Christine Lagarde as president of the European Central Bank next year.

He received backing from euro zone leaders at the European Council Summit, multiple media reported on Thursday.

The role still requires formal appointment after consultation with the European Parliament and the ECB's Governing Council, but neither has veto power and the decision follows intense political wrangling to reach a consensus on who should serve the eight-year term.

Reuters reported that de Cos was set for the nomination after the Netherlands agreed to back Klaas Knot, the only other candidate, citing European diplomatic sources.

De Cos is a 55-year-old economist and career central banker who rose through the ranks of the Bank of Spain, starting as a researcher in the 1990s and working in various departments before becoming governor from 2018 to June 2024.

He chaired the Basel Committee on Banking Supervision from 2019 to 2024, and has been General Manager of the Basel-based Bank for International Settlements since July 2025.

Both de Cos and his main rival for the ECB top job, Knot, were widely seen as candidates with deep knowledge and experience in monetary policy and financial stability — attributes that took on particular importance at a time of resurgent inflation, fragmentation and political uncertainty.

Lagarde had extensive experience in financial institutions but was criticized by some early in her tenure for a lack of central banking experience.

De Cos gained sufficient political backing in closed-door discussions and negotiations since Lagarde said she would step down on Feb. 7, 2027, over seven years after she became the first woman to helm the ECB. That was despite a final push by the Netherlands at the European Council meeting this week backing Knot.

Representatives from the euro area's biggest economies — Germany, France, Italy, Spain and the Netherlands — have been horse-trading as they look to secure representation on the central bank's six-member Executive Board.

Influential roles as chief economist, expected to go to Germany's Markus Brunnermeier, and head of the Markets Portfolio, for which France's Laurence Boone has reportedly been tapped, also change hands in 2027.

Analysts say de Cos's commentary and actions over more than two decades at Spain's central bank paint him as moderately dovish — inclined to favor lower interest rates to support economic growth — but also as a pragmatist who has been willing to adapt.

That has included support for strong rate hikes during the energy price shock that followed Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022, but also giving staunch backing for rates below zero and the huge stimulus package launched by then-ECB President Mario Draghi to reboot the euro zone economy in 2019.

De Cos quipped in 2024 that he was an "owl" after being asked if he was a hawk or a dove, emphasizing his focus on data and the situation at hand.

"It is true that Hernández de Cos supported the ECB's uber-dovish monetary policy during the period when inflation was persistently below target. However, his monetary policy views may well be regime-dependent," Christian Kopf, head of fixed income and foreign exchange at Union Investment, said in emailed comments.

"We are now clearly in a higher-inflation regime, with core inflation in the euro area expected to remain above the ECB's target through 2029."

Kopf added that de Cos was the candidate with the "strongest technical understanding of monetary policy and central banking," which he said was important at a time when the world is "witnessing an assault on central bank independence, not only in the United States but also in countries such as Japan."

"By appointing a career technocrat such as Hernández de Cos as ECB President, the European Council would send a strong signal that Europe will not waver in its commitment to price stability and that the euro will remain a hard currency," Kopf said.

Spain's de Cos as president, Germany's Brunnermeier as chief economist and France's Boone as head of market operations represent a "carefully crafted package deal," Kopf also noted, because it balances representation from northern and southern European countries. The result was partially due to the knock-on effects of the German government pushing hard for the chief economist position, he said.

Along with a desire among politicians for influence, a north-south balance is seen as important as the northern countries are seen as leaning more hawkish — more inclined to opt for rate hikes and restrictive policy to tame inflation — while southern are perceived as more dovish.

John Orchard, chairman of the Digital Monetary Institute at think tank OMFIF, said that as a "central banker's central banker," de Cos had "managed to earn respect from typically hawkish northern observers."

Another supportive factor of de Cos's candidacy was the stellar growth rate of many of those southern economies in recent years.

Once the "sick men" of the euro area's 2010s sovereign debt crisis, Portugal, Italy, Spain and Greece have become some of the bloc's strongest growth drivers in recent years.

Factors such as strong tourism, relative political stability, increased foreign investment and strengthening public finances have boosted southern economies. Meanwhile, the euro zone's traditional powerhouse Germany has stuttered amid soaring industrial costs and a struggling autos market.

Orchard said that some believed that Lagarde's presidency had ushered in an "esprit de corps" at the ECB, with policymakers focused on the interests of the whole European economy.

That would, in theory, reduce the importance of nationality among leadership and of traditional notions of hawkish northern nations and dovish southern ones. However, Orchard also noted the recurrent theme of national representation in the recent debate and the perception of Knot as a hawkish choice.

Diverging performance is a core challenge for any leader of the ECB, which must set monetary policy for 21 economies all growing at different speeds and with their own domestic priorities and inflationary pressures.

But it has become particularly pressing this year as the bloc's second-biggest economy, France, is teetering on the edge of its own potential debt crisis.

Moves in French markets and whether the ECB needs to take more extreme measures beyond continuing to urge fiscal discipline will remain in focus for de Cos.

The ECB Executive Board's view of what measures could be taken to prevent market disorder and under what conditions was a key topic under discussion when choosing who should fill the roles, according to insiders.

Despite political efforts to pass a budget that will reduce its budget deficit to around 5% of GDP, investors have been driving up French borrowing costs on concerns over its mounting public debt-to-GDP ratio, high budget deficit and contracting economy.

Risks are being compounded by political uncertainty ahead of France's presidential election in April, which is stymying business activity and raising wider questions about EU unity as far-right and far-left candidates pull ahead.

Rising French yields and the widening spread in France's borrowing costs over Germany's have led to speculation over whether the ECB might need to deploy tools such as liquidity injections or its never-used Transmission Protection Instrument to protect other countries from contagion and support the euro.

This would be politically controversial unless France agreed to stringent fiscal discipline measures akin to those that southern nations signed up to during the Sovereign Debt Crisis — which could be complicated if far-right presidential frontrunner Marine Le Pen, who has a combative relationship with EU institutions, wins.

Ken Egan, head of European sovereigns at ratings agency KBRA, said fragmentation was always a challenge for the ECB, which does not want to see divergent financing conditions and high sovereign spreads across the bloc.

However, he said that in the case of France he believed we were "some way from having a serious conversation about ECB intervention."

"If anything, sovereign spreads have pulled back a bit and tightened, and some measures of market liquidity have also improved in recent weeks," he said by email.

"That doesn't mean the underlying risks have disappeared, but I think it's important to distinguish between periods of market volatility and a more fundamental breakdown in monetary policy transmission."

De Cos also takes up the mantle as Europe faces yet another surge in price pressures.

Euro area inflation rose to 4% in October, its highest level in more than three years. Energy was the biggest contributor, as the Iran war drives up oil and gas prices and tightens the supply of refined fuels.

That leaves the central bank facing a difficult policy balance, preventing higher energy costs from spreading into wages and wider prices while avoiding unnecessary damage to growth.

De Cos will inherit major supranational projects, including plans for a digital euro and Europe's long-delayed capital-markets integration. In a recent speech, Lagarde warned that each year without capital markets integration meant "more of our savings are invested abroad and more of our growing firms look elsewhere for funding."

France is not the only euro area country heading for a pivotal election next year, with votes in Finland, Greece, Italy, Estonia, Slovakia and Slovenia also expected, adding to the politically uncertain climate.

It is also not the only country grappling with higher government borrowing costs, public debt and a struggle to find fiscal headroom, a growing problem among many developed countries.

The ECB's Financial Stability Review meanwhile warned this month of mounting risks from European investors' growing exposure to the U.S. economy, and equity and credit markets' vulnerability to AI.

Outgoing head Lagarde, meanwhile, told the European Banking Congress in Frankfurt this month that her biggest concern was now cyber threats, an area she said policymakers had not paid enough attention.

"The one [worry] that keeps me awake, and many of you awake ​as well ... ​is ⁠AI applied together with cyber threat," she said.

#2700. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

Disclosures: Tidal owns/holds all the securities mentioned in the article.

#2699. Embiid sidelined for Sixers-Blazers with illness

Embiid sidelined for Sixers-Blazers with illness originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers ruled star center Joel Embiid out with an illness Monday night for their game against the Trail Blazers at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

Embiid first popped up on the Sixers' injury report at 11:45 a.m. ET when the team listed him as questionable. He was downgraded to out about 90 minutes before the 7 p.m. tip-off.

He was the team's top scorer in each of the last three games and finished one assist shy of a 26-point triple-double Saturday in the Sixers' win over the Bucks.

The Sixers entered Monday 2-1 this season in games without Embiid. They were 21-23 last year.

The Sixers went with a starting five of Tyrese Maxey, VJ Edgecombe, Brown, LeBron James and Jabari Walker. It's the second start of the year for Walker, who spent his first three NBA seasons with the Blazers and has been an early-season bright spot off the Sixers' bench.

#2698. Even the price of toys is on the rise. How Americans are scaling back holiday plans

As Amanda Brecht planned how her family would spend Thanksgiving this year, she had to make some difficult decisions.

Because of high gas prices, the 30-year-old decided she wouldn't drive across her home state of Michigan to celebrate with extended family. Instead, Brecht and her husband will spend the holiday with just their three kids. And with food prices rising, they'll be skipping appetizers and serving turkey breast instead of a whole bird.

"Thanksgiving is definitely going to look different this year," Brecht said. "Money doesn't grow on trees. You can only do so much."

With inflation accelerating, Americans like Brecht will see higher prices almost everywhere they look this holiday season. At a time of year synonymous with food, gifts and big spending, many consumers are instead pinching pennies, paring back their plans and making tough choices about how they'll celebrate.

Some are raiding their savings accounts and running up debt on credit cards. They're reporting downbeat outlooks about the economy in surveys. And they've become more selective with spending as inflationary pressures mount.

"Families are facing some tough choices this year," said Alexander Smith, an associate professor who researches behavioral economics at the Worcester Polytechnic Institute. "They might have to cut back on holiday spending in whatever way that they can."

A classic Thanksgiving feast will be harder to pull off as grocery prices rise.

The price of a 10-person Thanksgiving dinner increased 6% to $58.63 in 2026, according to an annual survey from the American Farm Bureau Federation. Faith Parum, the organization's deputy chief economist, said the price increase was a result of higher costs for labor and transportation.

Grocers including Walmart and Aldi have gained traction on social media by offering Thanksgiving meal bundles as a value-focused workaround to higher prices. Walmart's offering promises to feed 10 for $35, and includes a turkey and ingredients to make mashed potatoes, green bean casserole, cranberries, marshmallow-topped sweet potatoes and a pumpkin pie.

Still, some consumers will feel disappointed by having less choice in their menus due to higher prices, according to Joe Bruseulas, chief economist at tax firm RSM.

"We're missing the point here if all we're doing is focusing on the fact that Walmart will sell you a homogeneous dinner meal" at a discount, Bruseulas said. "People feel horrible that they have to go to that because the price of everything has just risen so significantly over the past five years."

Inflation is affecting more than Thanksgiving staples like turkey or stuffing. Food prices on average have jumped around 24% over the last five years, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. Some items have seen even sharper increases: Coffee and ground beef, for example, have each climbed by more than 40% in the same period despite pulling back from recent highs.

At the same time, millions of Americans lost SNAP benefits under policy changes included in President Donald Trump's "big beautiful bill." Food banks across the U.S. have seen foot traffic skyrocket this year as the reduction in federal nutrition aid coincides with rising prices in grocery aisles.

The Salvation Army said some of its food pantries in October provided double or triple the amount of groceries than they did in the same month a year ago. Demand is now so high that some food banks in Feeding America's national network are worried about running out of supplies.

"We want to make sure that our food banks are as full as they possibly can be going into the holiday season," said Linda Nageotte, Feeding America's president and operations chief. "Far too many of them have empty shelves."

High energy prices as a result of the war with Iran are also affecting Americans' holiday plans.

Slightly fewer Americans will travel at least 50 miles around the Thanksgiving holiday in 2026, marking the first year-over-year decline since the country was in lockdown from the pandemic in 2020, according to AAA. Stacey Barber, a vice president at the auto association, attributed this year's pullback in part to higher fuel costs.

AAA said that about nine out of every 10 Thanksgiving travelers will do so by car. While gas prices have come down from a May peak, they are still up about 18% year over year and could set a new record for the month of November, according to the organization's data.

Travelers are paying about 31% and 23% more for roundtrip domestic airfare around Thanksgiving and Christmas, respectively, than they did a year ago, according to booking platform Hopper. Airlines have blamed the ticket price hikes on rising jet fuel costs in the wake of the war.

"There will be a significant shell shock when they see the price of traveling," said Hayley Berg, lead economist at Hopper.

Berg said price increases would feel more noticeable on routes previously serviced by Spirit, the budget airline that shut down in May. Travelers are booking holiday flights earlier than they did in past years, a sign that prices are top of mind, she said.

Staying home for the holidays isn't a silver-bullet solution, either, as heating costs jump.

The average American household is expected to spend 7% more on home heating this winter than they did last year, according to the National Energy Assistance Directors Association. For the typical family, that equates to an increase of about $66 and a total bill for the season above $1,000.

To be sure, some families will feel it more than others. Heating oil, which is used mainly in homes in the Northeast, has jumped around 70% year over year. However, natural gas, which is one of the most common heating types in the U.S., costs about the same as last year.

Some U.S. states, like Massachusetts and New York, beefed up their home energy support programs to account for higher prices. But Mark Wolfe, NEADA's executive director, said federal aid offerings haven't been meaningfully expanded this year.

"Some of these increases are caused directly by the decision to go to war," Wolfe said. "Why isn't the federal government helping families pay these higher bills?"

The spirit of giving will take on a different meaning for consumers this year.

Retail sales during the all-important holiday season are expected to grow almost 5% this year and surpass $1 trillion for the first time, according to Bain & Company. But the consulting firm said most of that growth would come from inflation, rather than from consumers buying more products.

And Citi on Wednesday said that increased holiday spending would be mainly driven by higher earners this year. The bank said that's a result of the "K"-shaped economy, a term used to describe a scenario where better-off Americans splash out while others struggle.

Toy prices have climbed more than 9% over the past year and now sit at their highest levels since 2019, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. Manufacturers have raised prices in response to higher costs for transportation and plastic, according to Chris Byrne, an independent toy industry analyst. The artificial intelligence boom has driven up prices for the chips that power gaming devices and other electronics.

Byrne said that's causing shoppers to focus more on the quality of gifts rather than quantity.

"The toy industry always reflects the culture at large," Byrne said. "It's almost true economically as well. As inflation is rising, people are being a little bit more cautious about what they're buying."

Just 16% of respondents to a Morning Consult survey said they planned to spend more in the 2026 holiday season than they did a year ago. Respondents who were older or had lower earnings were more likely to say their budgets were shrinking, the survey found.

Some are doing away with gifts altogether. A poll from consulting firm TSG and the Electronic Transactions Association found 5% of Americans did not plan to buy any presents in 2026, up three percentage points from last year.

One shopper, Wendy Cooper, set a limit of one gift for each of her three adult children this year. For her teenage daughter, the 56-year-old Atlanta resident has scoured Five Below, the trendy value retailer, for budget-friendly presents.

"It's super stressful," Cooper said. "You have to weigh: Do you guys want lights on and a place to live? Or do you want gifts?"

Across the board, the Bureau of Labor Statistics' consumer price index reaccelerated after the U.S. first struck Iran in late February. Annual inflation rates have begun rising faster than wages in the same period, leaving consumers with less purchasing power.

Even if Americans keep spending for the holidays, economists caution that high prices during this busy shopping period can further drag on their economic outlooks. In the CNBC Voter Poll early this month, about three-fourths of participants rated the economy as poor or not good. Consumer confidence gauges had dropped significantly this year, but have regained ground in the runup to the holiday shopping season.

From her home in Northern Michigan, Brecht is doing everything she can to make Christmas feel special without breaking the bank.

The graduate student has been delivering DoorDash orders in her spare time and listing clothes on resale platforms to save up money for her kids' holiday gifts. Brecht is watching closely for discounts and will look for potential presents at thrift stores in the coming weeks.

If necessary, Brecht said she would turn to credit cards for some holiday purchases. She isn't alone: TSG and ETA's survey found that about one-third of Americans said they expected to go into debt during the holiday season, a roughly 74% increase from 2025.

"I don't really know how we're going to do it," Brecht said. "I'm just praying for good Black Friday deals."

#2697. Buyer beware? Nvidia's volatile swings expose risks of single-stock ETFs

Nvidia's latest bout of volatility is highlighting risks tied to single-stock exchange-traded funds.

Among the hardest hit: the GraniteShares 2x Short NVDA Daily ETF (NVD). The fund sank 63% on Monday after Jensen Huang got reinstated as the chip giant's CEO. Meanwhile, the firm's 2x Long NVDA ETF (NVDL) soared nearly 62% on the same day.

ETF Action founding partner Mike Akins joined "ETF Edge" as Nvidia shares soared 31% during Monday's session, wiping a large portion of the losses from the days following Huang's resignation. Shares cooled on Tuesday and Wednesday, however.

"Historically speaking, we do not see these types of large drawdowns followed by huge drives up in a healthy market," Akins said on Monday. "It usually tends to be when you're in a recession or in a bear market territory, which we're not technically in for the broader market, but we are in for this AI trade."

But it may not be time to go cold turkey on single-stock funds.

"[Leveraged ETFs are] definitely a retail trading vehicle…  I think it's OK to have a little fun trying to trade that," Akins added. "I'm certain there are people that are better at trading these than others. But on the whole, timing the market is very, very hard."

GraniteShares founder and CEO Will Rhind also joined "ETF Edge" on Monday as Nvidia single-stock short ETFs overshadowed his firm's new Anthropic launches: The 2x Short Anthropic Daily ETF (ANS) and the 2x Long Anthropic Daily ETF (AIL).

"We thought this was going to be the biggest news event of the day with Anthropic the first leveraged ETFs going live," he said. "I can tell you this is like a sideshow compared to what we're seeing with Nvidia."

Rhind suggests Nvidia's wild swings aren't dampening enthusiasm for single-stock ETFs.

"As long as there's enthusiasm in the underlying name… people will want to trade it and most likely want to trade it with leverage either on the long on the short side," added Rhind.

"Fast Money" tackled the risks tied to ETFs that are tied to single stocks, too.

"If this is somehow part of a portfolio, [then] that is a dangerous game to be playing," trader Guy Adami said on Monday.

Karen Finerman also addressed the strategy's downsides.

"Leverage is always the thing that kills. It's always leverage," said Finerman, co-founder and CEO of Metropolitan Capital Advisors, in the same interview. "You can survive anything but leverage."

As of Wednesday's close, the GraniteShares 2x Short NVDA Daily ETF plunged 55% so far this week while the GraniteShares 2x Long NVDA Daily ETF is up 31%.

#2696. Trump demands probe into ‘record setting error’ after third-quarter GDP data gets massive revision higher

President Donald Trump on Thursday falsely deemed the latest gross domestic product revision a mistake, claiming that it should be "immediately investigated."

Data released Wednesday from the Commerce Department revealed that the U.S. economy exhibited much stronger growth for the third quarter than initially estimated, showing that real GDP grew at a 4.3% annualized rate. That was revised upward from the department's advance estimate of 3.4%.

That revision was a "RECORD SETTING ERROR," Trump said in a Truth Social post, suggesting that the initial release could have influenced the outcome of the 2026 U.S. midterm elections, where Democrats flipped both chambers of Congress and even multiple governorships.

"I would like to wish a Happy Thanksgiving to even those who issued fake Economic Numbers, just prior to the Election, and corrected them yesterday, three weeks late, showing that the GDP was actually 4.3%, versus 3.4%, A RECORD SETTING ERROR, and one so grave that it should be immediately investigated," Trump wrote.

However, the difference between the advance estimate and the figure in the second release is far from being an all-time record. It was the largest revision since the 1.2 percentage point revision seen in the second quarter of 2020.

On an absolute basis, the latest revision was tied for 16th among all revisions from the advance estimate to the second release since 1990. It also tied four other quarters with a similar 0.9 percentage point difference — and was approximately double the average revision of 0.43 percentage points for all 147 quarters.

The upward revision shows Trump's policies are "delivering results" that defy expectations, White House spokesman Matthew Middleton told CNBC.

Revisions to U.S. economic numbers are not necessarily out of the ordinary. Initial reports count on partial survey data and early estimates. Those data are later updated with more survey responses — which sometimes leads to changes from the initial reports.

GDP data is revised repeatedly over a five year period. By the time the final revision is done, the average absolute error from the second release to the final is 1.14 percentage points.

The advance estimate for Q3 U.S. GDP, which came just days before Election Day in early November, was still better than the figure economists had expected at the time. It also showed a significant step-up in growth from the second quarter.

Because that was a strong showing for the economy, some on Wall Street consider this week's revision to be a confirmation of the economy's recent growth trend.

Jim Baird of Plante Moran Financial Advisors said immediately following the release that it "reinforces an increasingly familiar tension" that the economy is "performing quite well, but its resilience also gives the Fed an even stronger case for further rate hikes."

"The economy isn't merely hanging in there. It continues to demonstrate considerable resilience, with consumers spending, businesses investing and few signs that restrictive monetary policy is creating significant cracks in the broader expansion," the firm's chief investment officer said.

Traders are currently pricing in a nearly 100% chance of a hike at the Federal Open Market Committee's December meeting, per the CME FedWatch tool. They're also anticipating an additional hike in January.

— CNBC's Jeff Cox and Fred Imbert contributed reporting

#2695. Berkshire breaks out to record high with fourth-quarter rally reaching 19%

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway's red-hot shares are ending a strong week with four consecutive fresh record high closes.

Since the rally began with the start of the fourth quarter on October 1, the Class A shares have closed at record highs 11 times as they soared 19.1%.

The much more widely held B shares closed at new highs just 10 times but are also up 19.1% from their near-term low.

The gains are in sharp contrast to the S&P 500's 1.2% dip as investors appear to be seeing Berkshire as a relatively safe haven amid worries about AI stocks and spending.

Both Berkshire classes were up 3.9% on the week as the S&P advanced by 2.5%, its best week since early August.

Berkshire's B shares are now 7.5 percentage points ahead of the benchmark for the year so far.

After erasing their post-Buffett CEO announcement losses three weeks ago, Berkshire shares are now up almost 10% since last year's shareholders meeting.

They've gained around 29% since last August's lows and are up around 18% year-to-date.

Berkshire Hathaway is due to disclose its quarterly equity portfolio snapshot on Monday when it files its Q3 13F with the SEC.

We won't know for sure until then, but numbers in the company's third quarter 10-Q, released last Saturday, indicate Berkshire continued to reduce its Bank of America stake.

It would be the ninth consecutive quarter of selling.

As of June 30, Berkshire reported holding 483.4 million BAC shares valued now at $24.7 billion, it's fifth largest equity position.

On June 30, 2024, before the selling began, Berkshire owned just over 1 billion shares valued at $41.1 billion.

Since then, the number of shares in the portfolio has decreased by 53.2%, not including any Q3 sales, while the stock price has increased by 28.3%.

If there had been no reductions, the position would be worth $52.7 billion.

The belief Berkshire sold more BAC shares in the third quarter is based on a $372 million reduction in the cost basis for equities categorized by the company as "banks, insurance, and finance."

The 10-Q also reports $2.2 billion of pretax gains from equity sales.

Barron's notes Bank of America and American Express are the only financial stocks in the portfolio that have a cost basis low enough to generate a gain like that. Amex is one of the four stocks new CEO Greg Abel indicated would be held for "decades" to come in his February letter to shareholders.

In any case, Berkshire's almost 23% stake in Amex would have required it to quickly disclose any sales.

Based on the numbers in the 10-Q, the warrants Berkshire received as part of a 2011 $5 billion investment in the bank that it used in 2017 to buy 700 million shares at $7.14 each, and BAC's average stock price in the quarter, Barron's estimates 30 to 40 million shares may have been sold.

That would be roughly in line with the 30.2 million shares sold in the second quarter.

The case isn't as good, but Barron's thinks Berkshire may have bought more Alphabet shares in the period. That's based on a $2.3 billion increase in the cost basis for the category that includes Google's parent.

Overall, it looks like it will be a relatively quiet quarter with $2.4 billion in equity purchases and $2.8 billion in sales.

In the first quarter, Berkshire bought $15.9 billion and sold $24.1 billion of equities. In the second quarter, it purchased $23.5 billion and sold $3.7 billion.

All those numbers exclude the company's buybacks of its own stock.

In a term sheet filed early Friday, Berkshire Hathaway says it is selling more than 182 billion yen ($1.1 billion) worth of bonds with maturities between 2029 and 2056 and annual interest rates between 2.6% and 4.6%.

Bloomberg points out the rates, compared to benchmarks, are higher than its previous sale this year.

It quotes a fund manager as saying market sentiment "has become more sensitive," forcing issuers to make their offerings more attractive.

In April, Berkshire sold 272 billion yen ($1.7 billion) of bonds.

Berkshire uses the proceeds from the yen-denominated bonds to protect its ongoing investments in Japanese stocks from currency fluctuations.

Warren Buffett recommends Benjamin Graham's book, "The Intelligent Investor," and outlines what he considers Graham's three most important ideas.

WARREN BUFFETT: On investment books, if you're asking about that, I would recommend the first two books that Phil Fisher wrote back around 1960, "Common Sense [Stocks] and Uncommon Profits" and the second one ["Paths to Wealth Through Common Stocks"]. They're very good books.

You know, I obviously recommend, first and foremost, [Benjamin Graham's] "The Intelligent Investor," with chapters eight and 20 are the ones that you really should read.

Two of the — well, all of the important ideas in investing, really, are in that book, because there's only about three ideas. And those — two of them are emphasized in those two chapters...

One is that — to think of yourself — to think of investing as owning a business and not buying something that wiggles around in price.

And the second one is your attitude, which ties in with that, the attitude toward the market, that's covered in chapter eight. And if you have the proper attitude toward market movements, it's an enormous help in securities.

And the final chapter is on the margin of safety, which means, don't try and drive a 9,800-pound truck over a bridge that says it's, you know, "Capacity: 10,000 pounds." But go down the road a little bit and find one that says, "Capacity: 15,000 pounds."

Berkshire market capitalization: $1,259,897,054,755

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

#2694. Millville survives storm, stuffs Vineland on Thanksgiving

VINELAND – The 155th edition of the Millville-Vineland high school football game will be remembered for a long time around these neighboring towns.

While the scoreboard confirmed the Thunderbolts won the game, 17-0, the true victor on this Thanksgiving Day was Mother Nature.

While a steady rainfall along with gusty winds whipping around Gittone Stadium made the football tough to hold onto, it couldn't loosen Millville's grip on the victory trophy.

More: Everything you need to know for the 2026 high school football season

The Thunderbolts won for the seventh time in a row, increasing their lead to 69-67-19 in the all-time series, which began in 1893. Over the seven-year successful stretch, Millville has outscored Vineland, 296-33.

Most of the players were headed to the school buses as the clock hit zeroes, not even waiting around for the chance to touch the Tony Surace/John Pierantozzi Victory Cup.

"It was crazy," Millville senior quarterback Robbie O'Connor said about the elements, which would have challenged even the most dedicated mailman on this day.

"When you see all the other games get moved (because of the forecasted storm), maybe this game should have been moved, too. But we love football, and every single game we've played in the rain this year, we've played amazing. We just go out there and do our thing."

Despite the rough conditions, Millville handled its job without a hitch.

The Thunderbolts' special teams produced a safety (off a Vineland botched snap on a punt in the end zone) and a Jack Powers 27-yard field goal. The defense delivered five takeaways, including a scoop-and-score by Nyshon Jones while the offense accounted for the other TD with Jachere Graves's 1-yard TD run.

While there were a couple hundred die-hard fans in the stands on both sides of the stadium, the weather kept many in the warmth of their own homes.

And that's what Millville head coach Humberto Ayala was upset about the most.

"It was ridiculous (to play)," Ayala said. "This is a historic game, it's the seniors' last game. And in my personal opinion, it's not fair to those seniors and those kids, not to have the fans here for them on this last game of the season.

"This game means so much, it's the 155th time we've played them, and they should have put a better thought process into it, instead of worrying about a potential advantage for them.

"Honestly, we should have played (Wednesday night) or they should have moved it to another day. In the end, the result was the result and we knew what we were going to do."

In a rivalry that has always produced plenty of heat (even on this day), this was another undoubtably another log on the fire.

And that's OK with Vineland head coach Dan Russo.

"I'm happy we played," he said. "Last time I checked, it's football, not golf.

"This game is played on Thanksgiving and we're trying to keep this tradition alive. If we played on grass or if we were located down the shore, we would have moved the game to another day. I thought it was in our best interest to play on Thanksgiving."

Russo didn't deny that playing in the rainy and windy conditions suited the Fighting Clan better than the Millville offense, which is more pass-heavy. But he also pointed out that Vineland encountered a lot more problems holding onto the football than Millville.

"It certainly hurt us more than them (Thursday)," Russo said.

Reporters' notes from the game were no match for the elements either, but they weren't the only object to suffer a loss. The Surace/Pierantozzi Victory Cup was missing its large base, which had broken off earlier in the morning en route to Gittone Stadium.

Unofficial stats had O'Connor completing five passes for 35 yards. The weather kept the senior quarterback from making a serious run at two passing records. For one of those marks, he needed 285 yards to become just the second quarterback in South Jersey history to throw for 2,000 in three different seasons, joining Timber Creek's Devin Leary.

While he would have liked a better opportunity to go after the records, O'Connor put the game in perspective.

"This is one of the biggest rivalries in the entire country," he said. "It means a lot to our city, so it means a lot to us.

"It's a privilege to wear the orange and blue and I'm so happy I got the opportunity to do so these last three years."

The elements certainly made it tough to grabe the football, so imagine what it took to kick in Thursday's conditions.

After a bad snap thwarted his first field goal attempt earlier in the game, Powers gave Millville an 11-0 lead just before halftime with a 27-yards field goal.

The sophomore converted 6-of-11 field goal attempts and 28 of 31 extra points this season. Two of his field goals were from beyond 40 yards.

Millville finished at 7-5 for the second consecutive season and locked up its fifth winning campaign since Ayala took over the program. The Thunderbolts lost to Atlantic City, 35-14, in the Central Jersey Group 4 championship on Nov. 13.

Vineland went 5-5 in Russo's return to the program. Russo went 42-60, including the program's first-ever playoff wins, during his first stretch as the Fighting Clan's head coach.

"We're taking the right steps, we're moving forward," Russo said. "This was a fun group to coach and we're hungry to fix it. Now, we're going to get them prepared for next year."

Vineland lost to Sayreville, 29-27, in the Central Jersey Group 5 quarterfinal round.

Tom McGurk is a regional sports editor for the Courier-Post, The Daily Journal and Burlington County Times, covering South Jersey sports for over 40 years. #2693. NFL Black Friday preview: Steelers cut Aaron Rodgers, turn to Mason Rudolph against hot Broncos

Black Friday represents a new dawn for the Pittsburgh Steelers.

The Steelers shockingly benched Aaron Rodgers late Tuesday night, and then released him Wednesday. Pittsburgh faces the 8-2 Denver Broncos at Acrisure Stadium on Friday. It will be Mason Rudolph starting in Rodgers' place for the 6-4 Steelers, who are fighting for their playoff lives after losing two straight since their bye week.

The shock was less because of how Rodgers and the Steelers' offense had played lately — not well — but more so for how the Steelers and head coach Mike McCarthy had backed Rodgers and seemingly endorsed him, as recently as Monday. That all changed when the team benched him, prompting Rodgers to go on ESPN and rip McCarthy while declaring he wouldn't return to the team.

Rodgers reportedly issued McCarthy an ultimatum over total control of play-calling and the head coach declined, and sent the future Hall of Famer on his way in stunning fashion.

Amazingly, it appears as if DK Metcalf will play Friday, less than a week after being ejected from a loss to the Philadelphia Eagles … and it wasn't Metcalf's first such incident. He's amid one of his worst seasons to date and now must match up with Broncos All-Pro CB Pat Surtain II and do so with a backup QB.

Rudolph has seen mop-up action in four games this season. He will make his first start since 2025 against Denver, a team he's never faced. The Broncos have 31 sacks this season, and the Steelers have allowed 31 sacks.

Denver's defense has been closer to good than great this season, but its offense has picked it up after a slow start to the year. In the past four games, the Broncos have scored 26 or more points in each after hitting that mark only once in the first six outings.

The Broncos are riding a six-game win streak and chasing the top playoff seed in the AFC. The Steelers haven't lost at Acrisure this season, but they've failed to hit the 20-point mark in three of their past four games.

The Steelers are in desperation mode. They're currently in the seventh playoff slot and have a 57% chance to make the postseason, according to Next Gen Stats, as things stand right now. That might sound pretty good, and no team currently outside the playoff field is over 50%.

A loss would drop the Steelers' chances to 50%. A win would push them to 72%. Pittsburgh's next four games are against teams still fighting for the postseason.

It's not as if Denver has nothing to play for. It is currently the No. 2 seed in the playoff slotting, just one game behind the Bills, whom the Broncos host in Week 16 on Christmas. The difference between the top seed (which comes with a bye) and the next spot down feels massive.

But the Broncos are near-certainties to make the playoffs, currently sitting at 99% chances to get in. A loss would drop them all the way down to 98%. Their schedule prior to and after the Bills game is soft. It would be tough to lose to a team making a QB change in a short week, but it wouldn't damage Denver's playoff hopes much at all.

Since coming off injured reserve four games ago, Coleman has seen his share of the Broncos' backfield duty increase with each game. On Sunday against the Raiders, the rookie scored three TDs and ran for 111 yards for his best outing of his short NFL career.

The Broncos likely want to see what more Coleman can do, especially with J.K. Dobbins suspended for this game. Perhaps not coincidentally, during Coleman's strong four-game span, Bo Nix and Denver's passing game have produced four straight games with 200-plus passing yards.

The Steelers rank third in rushing yards allowed and fourth in yards per carry allowed. They've had a stout rush defense all season, especially recently allowing only 221 rushing yards in their past four outings. It could be a tougher assignment, however, if Alex Highsmith (ankle) can't go, as expected. Mike McCarthy said it will be "tough for [Highsmith] to make it this week."

Is there a less impressive team with a great record than the Denver Broncos? Tied for the best mark against the spread entering Week 12 (8-2 ATS), Denver has won six straight games — and could've fairly easily lost four of them. However, its schedule sets up remarkably well for a run at the AFC's No. 1 seed if the Broncos can keep winning as a favorite. Denver is a 3.5-point road favorite in this one at Pittsburgh, and it's hard to find a lot of reasons to be excited about the Steelers going with backup QB Mason Rudolph. And while Bo Nix's Total QBR is only 48.2, Denver's defense is a much better unit than Pittsburgh's. The Steelers' playoff chances continue to dim with a loss here. — Ben Fawkes

Check out more of Ben's work at the Yahoo Sports betting hub.

Scoring expectations are muted (36.5 total) as the Broncos and Steelers meet for a Black Friday afternoon game. Here are three fantasy themes to unpack as you watch the game.

1. Can Mason Rudolph get Pittsburgh's passing game going? Aaron Rodgers has not played well for several weeks, but it was still a shocker when his benching was announced Tuesday night. Rudolph has always been a capable backup through his seven-year career, but his overall stats profile to a less-than-average starter. Still, sometimes there's a bounce when a slumping offense gets a new man in charge.

2. Is Jonah Coleman too good to be true? Coleman was a one-man wrecking crew in Week 11 (three touchdowns, 129 total yards), and with J.K. Dobbins suspended for this week, Coleman's floor is especially high. Coleman is the current favorite for Offensive Rookie of the Year, a notable development given how little he played in the first half of the year.

3. Is Jaylen Warren still a Circle of Trust guy? Warren was seen as one of fantasy's right answers through the first two months, but he's in a 16-43-0 rushing funk over the last two weeks, with zero touchdowns. Rodgers' slump played into this, but Warren needs a decent start because Rico Dowdle is going to see work, too. — Scott Pianowski

#2692. James can still take over ‘any given moment,' and Sixers trust he'll pick right spots

James can still take over 'any given moment,' and Sixers trust he'll pick right spots originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

LeBron James isn't oblivious to the calendar and the clock.

He knows he'll turn 42 years old next month, he's ancient by NBA standards and decline is inevitable.

James also knows who he is and what he still can do, whatever the norms are for players of a certain age.

"I think his battle against Father Time is one of the most impressive things he's done over the course of his entire career," Heat head coach Erik Spoelstra said Wednesday before Miami's loss to the Sixers. "We all know and everybody always says that Father Time always wins. But right now he's giving Father Time hell."

James went on to record his first triple-double with the 15-4 Sixers, posting 25 points, 12 assists and 10 rebounds. The four-time MVP is at 126 regular-season triple-doubles and counting in his career.

Only Russell Westbrook, Nikola Jokic, Oscar Robertson and Magic Johnson have more in NBA history. James is 17 above Philadelphia native and Sixers legend Wilt Chamberlain.

With Joel Embiid sidelined by illness for a second consecutive game and Jaylen Brown ruled out at halftime with left hamstring tightness, James' do-it-all performance was timely. Tyrese Maxey's 30-point, six-assist outing was essential, too.

"I'm just trying to play the game the right way," James said. "I don't think my job is to dominate the game every night for this ball club. We have a Finals MVP (Brown), we have a regular-season MVP (Embiid), we have an up-and-coming superstar at point guard, we've got a young fella in VJ (Edgecombe) who's up and coming as well.

"My dues are paid with what I've done. If they need me to dust off the cape from time to time, then I can still accomplish that. But that's not my job. … But if they need it, I can do it at any given moment."

James' night featured a flurry of very deep three-pointers in the second quarter.

He started the season 10 for 46 beyond the arc (21.7 percent), but his percentage has risen to 31.6 with two sweet-shooting games in a row.

"The work has been put in and I'm not ever worried about anything as far as my game," he said. "The difference between the start of the season and now is that my legs are feeling better, my ramp-up is feeling better.

"I'm about to be 42 years old in a month. S—, it takes a little bit longer for the ramp-up, but I've been feeling pretty good as of late. I don't ever second-guess myself … because I know the work that I put in."

While James' rebounding was perhaps the least spectacular aspect of his game, Sixers head coach Nick Nurse appreciated his work on the boards.

James has remained a statistically solid defensive rebounder into his 40s and he's one reason why the Sixers have made a major improvement in that department. According to Cleaning the Glass, the Sixers' opponents have grabbed 28.4 percent of their own misses, which ranks sixth in the NBA. The team ranked 27th last season.

Asked about James' cape comments, Maxey shouted out, "Dust it off then!" with a grin.

Maxey's not sure exactly when James will dramatically elevate his game, but he trusts the 22-time All-Star to pick the right spots.

"I don't know," Maxey said. "Early in the game, he was telling me he was tired. I said, 'Well, we don't have time for you to be tired.'

"But he does a good job of just managing the game. He knows when he needs to score, he knows when he needs to pass. We're going to need a lot from him. I know he's seasoned, but we expect a lot of him and he does that."

Who says you can't tailgate an IPO?Traders are preparing for this week's blockbuster Anthropic IPO in different ways, including buying options on one publicly-traded fund that invests in private assets.The Destiny Tech100 (DXYZ) is a publicly-traded, closed-end fund that invests in equity, equity-linked instruments, or shares of private funds and venture companies to gain exposure to private companies. #2690. Rams shut down Packers in second half for a 26-13 win in NFL's first Thanksgiving Eve game

The Rams and Packers played to a tie in the first 30 minutes of Wednesday night’s game in Los Angeles, but the second half was all Rams.

Blake Corum ran for 151 yards and a score in final 30 minutes and the Rams defense shut out the Packers to pave the way to a 26-13 win. The victory is the fifth in a row for the Rams and they are now 7-4 with a Thursday night home game against the Chiefs on deck in Week 13.

The win moves the Rams ahead of the Packers in the race for NFC playoff spots and Thursday’s game between the Lions and Bears will have further impact on the chances for all four teams to advance to the postseason.

Corum’s touchdown came at the end of the Rams’ first possession of the second half and he finished the game with a career-high 32 carries for 184 yards.

That scoring drive ate up more than eight minutes. The Packers had a quick three-and-out, but briefly showed some life when linebacker Ty’Ron Hopper picked off Matthew Stafford to set the offense up in Los Angeles territory. Jordan Love turned the ball right back over when he hung a deep pass that safety Jaylen McCullough snagged out of the air. The Rams would then ride Corum and Kyren Williams on a pair of drives that covered more than 11 minutes.

The Packers’ last chance came with 6:13 left and they moved the ball to midfield before Love threw his second interception of the game. Cornerback Emmanuel Forbes got that one and the Packers were out of timeouts well before the two-minute warning.

Sticking with the ground game helped to limit Packers defensive end Micah Parsons’s impact in his return from last season’s torn ACL. He had a sack late in the first half, but Stafford only threw eight times after the break and the Packers’ run defense could not get their offense the ball often enough to get back into the game.

Love was 17-of-29 for 198 yards and a touchdown, but clearly missed wide receiver Christian Watson after he left the game with a concussion. They only picked up two first downs after halftime and they’ll hope that an extended break before their Week 13 trip to New Orleans is enough time for him to return to action.

The Rams have several injuries of their own to monitor as guard Bill Murray joined tight ends Davis Allen and Tyler Higbee in making early exits from the game. They’d like to have all those players back next week, but they showed on Wednesday night that Corum can sometimes be enough all by himself.

#2689. Ranking The Best 3-Level Scorers In The NBA Right Now

Being one of the best scorers in the NBA doesn't necessarily mean you're one of the best three-level scorers. Just look at Giannis Antetokounmpo, who's been putting up 30 PPG for years without needing much of a three-point shot, or Stephen Curry, who can go for 40 points without even touching the paint (though I'd say he's definitely inside this category).

When we talk about three-level scoring, we're looking at guys who can get their points in three different areas of the court. The first one is inside the paint, whether that's through post-ups, layups, dunks, or floaters. The second is the mid-range, where we get most of those pull-up jumpers and fadeaways, and the third is obviously scoring from beyond the arc.

What makes these guys so difficult to guard is that taking away one shot doesn't take them out of the game. Go under a screen, and they'll pull up from three. Play too high, and they'll get to the rim. Drop your big man too far into the paint, and they'll get an open jumper from the elbow.

For this ranking, we're looking at scoring production from each area, shooting efficiency, volume, and how good these players are at creating their own shots. We're also putting more weight on what they've been doing this season rather than their glory or reputation, with all stats coming from the 2026-27 regular season.

With that in mind, here are the 10 best three-level scorers in the NBA right now.

It feels a little weird having Kevin Durant this low on a list like this, considering he's probably one of the five best three-level scorers we've ever seen. But at 38 years old, the Houston Rockets forward is taking just 13.5 shots per game, his lowest volume since coming into the NBA.

The efficiency is still there, though. Durant is shooting 50.8% from the field, 39.7% from three, and 88.1% from the free-throw line, good for a 64.9% true shooting mark. He's also making 55.6% of his two-point attempts, so it's not like he's suddenly having problems scoring inside the arc.

Durant ranks sixth in the NBA in mid-range scoring at 4.5 points per game, which accounts for around 21% of his entire scoring production. He's still getting to those same spots around the elbows, shooting over smaller defenders in the post, and pulling up from pretty much anywhere inside the three-point line.

The biggest change is how often he's getting those shots. Durant is attempting only 4.1 threes per game and scoring six points in the paint, with another 6.2 points coming from free throws. He's getting fewer opportunities to attack the rim and letting guys like Alperen Sengun and Amen Thompson handle more of the scoring this year.

In terms of pure scoring ability, I'd still take Durant over a good number of players on this list. But there are younger guys producing a lot more across all three levels this season.

For someone who's only 6-foot-3, Donovan Mitchell gets an absurd amount of his scoring done inside the paint. The Cleveland Cavaliers guard is at 10.9 points from that area, which puts him right alongside Shai Gilgeous-Alexander and ahead of plenty of starting centers around the league.

Mitchell is shooting 59.8% on two-point attempts this season, and a lot of that comes from how explosive he is getting downhill. He's strong finishing through contact, can get above the rim when there's a chance, and has a good floater when the opposing big man steps up to contest the drive.

Then there's his three-point shot, which is still a huge part of his game even though he's shooting just 34.4% from deep this season. Mitchell has made 45 threes through 18 games and is taking 7.3 attempts per night, most of the time being able to get into his shot straight off the dribble (40% unassisted 3P).

The reason I've got him ninth is his mid-range production. He's 27th in the NBA with 2.1 points per game from that area, and while we know he can knock down those pull-up jumpers coming off screens, we don't see him go to them anywhere near as much as some of the guys coming up.

Still, averaging 25.0 points on 49.5% shooting from the field while getting almost 44% of your scoring inside the paint as a 6-foot-3 guard is impressive. Mitchell is just more dangerous at two of the three levels than he is at the other one.

There are very few long wings who have such a versatile scoring game as Jayson Tatum. The Boston Celtics star can back down smaller guards, drive past bigger forwards, hit turnaround jumpers from the post, and create his own three-point shot without needing a screen.

He's putting up 27.3 points per game, fifth in the NBA, while taking 20.1 shots and 8.6 threes per game. Interestingly enough, his scoring production from inside the paint and beyond the arc is almost identical, with both areas giving him around 8.8 points per night.

Tatum has already made 47 threes in 16 games, although his 34.3% shooting from deep isn't anything special. He's also shooting exactly 50.0% on two-point attempts, and his overall 43.3% shooting from the field and 57.6% true shooting are a big part of why he's not higher here.

My thing with Tatum is that I'd love to see him go back to his mid-range a little more often. He's giving the Celtics just 2.9 points per game from there, and considering his size and ability to shoot over defenders, he doesn't always need to settle for a difficult stepback three.

The ability is clearly there, and getting to the line 8.3 times per game (7.0 FTM) also helps him maintain his scoring numbers when those jumpers aren't falling. But there are guys averaging fewer points than Tatum who are currently doing a better job of balancing their shot selection and efficiency across all three levels.

When somebody asks me what a three-level scorer looks like, Devin Booker is usually one of the first players I think of. There isn't one specific shot that makes up most of his scoring, and he's one of the few guards who still spends a good portion of every game working from the mid-post.

Booker ranks third in the NBA in mid-range scoring this season, behind SGA and Scottie Barnes, who is developing amazingly in that area if you've seen a few Raptors highlights. Back to the point, around 22% of Booker's points come from that area, which is a much higher percentage than most of the other guards averaging over 20 points in today's NBA.

Watching him come off a screen and get to either elbow is probably the best example of how he uses all three levels. He can reject the screen and get to the rim, stop for a pull-up jumper against a drop, or step back behind the arc if the defender goes under the screen. He's also got the footwork to turn those mismatches into post-ups and fadeaways against smaller guards.

The three-point efficiency hasn't been great this season, with the Phoenix Suns star shooting 32.7% on 5.8 attempts per game. That's a big reason why his overall field goal percentage is down to 46.2%, and he's currently at around 59.0% in true shooting.

What's interesting is that Booker is still making 92.1% of his free throws and getting roughly 6.2 points per game at the line, so the touch is obviously there. If he gets his three-point percentage back into the high 30s, I could easily see him moving into the top five of this ranking.

If we were ranking the best post scorers in the NBA, Nikola Jokic would probably be my first pick. The Denver Nuggets MVP has pretty much every move you could ask for down low, and he doesn't need to be particularly fast or jump particularly high to get his shot off, even with his early-season struggles.

Jokic is sixth in the NBA with 13.9 points per game in the paint, meaning more than half of his entire scoring production comes from inside. He's shooting 60.0% on two-point attempts and 53.0% from the field overall, with just 17.9 shots per game.

The craziest part about his interior scoring is how many different ways he gets those points. There are the little hook shots with either hand, floaters from six or seven feet, spin moves against bigger centers, and those quick volleyball-type shots he gets off before the help defense has time to arrive.

Move him a few feet away from the basket, and he's still got his one-legged fadeaway, turnaround jumpers, and that Sombor Shuffle we all know by now. Jokic doesn't take a ton of mid-range shots, but it's one of the toughest parts of his game to defend because of how high he releases the ball.

What's keeping him outside the top five is his current three-point percentage. Jokic is just 20-of-67 from deep through 16 games, good for 29.9%, after shooting 38.0% last season. He's still at 61.3% in true shooting, but his three-point production isn't close to what some of the players ahead of him are giving their teams right now.

I don't think people realized how much better Victor Wembanyama has gotten as a pure scorer this season. As fun as it was watching a 7-foot-4 big man pull up from 30 feet during his first few years in the NBA, this version of Wemby is much harder to deal with as he's now cracking the 30 PPG mark, along with his alien shot-blocking and defensive aura. So yeah, Wemby has arrived as an offensive powerhouse now.

The San Antonio Spurs star is second in the NBA with 15.8 points per game in the paint, behind only Giannis. He's shooting 64.6% on two-point attempts and putting up 30.7 points in just 30.5 minutes per game, which works out to around 36.2 points per 36 minutes.

And unlike most centers who get that many points inside, Wemby is also attempting 6.7 threes per game. He's made 38 of those shots through 18 games, shooting 31.7% from deep. That's still below league average, but just having a center who can take that many threes changes how opposing centers guard him. And his 3.2 mid-range points per game rank 11th in the NBA, so he's getting plenty of shots between those two areas as well.

Wembanyama's shooting splits of 54.3% from the field, 31.7% from three, and 77.8% from the line give him a 63.0% true shooting percentage. If he becomes even a 36.0% three-point shooter on this kind of volume, we might be talking about the best scorer in the world within a year or two.

Jalen Brunson probably has the best post game of any point guard in the NBA not named Luka Doncic, which is funny considering he's only 6-foot-2. The New York Knicks star plays like a power forward for a few seconds every time he gets a defender on his back, using pivots, pump fakes, and little turnaround jumpers to get his points.

Brunson is fourth in the NBA in mid-range scoring at 4.8 points per game, with another 7.2 coming from inside the paint. He's not particularly explosive getting to the basket, but he's excellent at slowing down, getting defenders off balance, and using his body to create space for a shot.

Then look at what he's doing from three this season. Brunson has already made 53 triples in 18 games, shooting 39.6% on 7.4 attempts per game. He's getting more than a third of his points from beyond the arc without giving up the mid-range game that has made him so good over the past few seasons.

The problem is his current efficiency inside the arc, as he's shooting just 44.4% on two-point attempts and 42.7% from the field overall. His 54.8% true shooting percentage is easily the biggest weakness in his case for a top-three spot right now.

Still, I love how balanced Brunson's scoring game is. There aren't many guards in the NBA who are getting nearly five mid-range points, over seven paint points, and almost nine points from threes on a nightly basis.

This might be the most surprising name in the top three, but have you seen Jamal Murray's shooting numbers this season? The Canadian is averaging 25.8 points on 50.0% shooting from the field, 45.6% from three, and 88.3% from the free-throw line, almost on that elite 50/40/90 club.

That's good for a 65.5% true shooting percentage, the highest of anybody on this list. And he's doing it while taking just 16.9 shots per game, which is four fewer than Brunson and more than eight fewer than Luka.

Murray has made 52 threes through 16 games, taking 7.1 attempts per night. Those numbers alone would make him one of the best perimeter scorers in the NBA right now, but he's also in the top 10 in mid-range scoring at 3.5 points per game and is giving the Nuggets another 6.9 points inside the paint.

Obviously, playing next to Jokic helps a lot. Their two-man game gives Murray open pull-up jumpers when defenses drop, the chance to attack the rim when Jokic brings the opposing center to the break, and plenty of good looks from three. But Murray still has to make those shots, and he's doing it at an incredible rate.

He's also making 53.2% of his two-point attempts, meaning he's currently more efficient than Brunson both inside and outside the arc while averaging almost the same number of points.

I wouldn't necessarily have Murray third if we were talking about pure shot creation over the last few years. But if we're ranking these guys based on what they're doing right now, it's very difficult to argue against his combination of scoring volume, shot versatility, and efficiency.

There's really not much of a debate on Shai Gilgeous-Alexander being the best inside-the-arc scorer among guards in the NBA. The Oklahoma City Thunder's reigning MVP is getting around 18 points per game between his paint and mid-range scoring, and nobody else on this list comes close to his production from the mid-range.

SGA leads the entire league with 7.7 mid-range points per game, 1.7 more than second-place Barnes and 2.2 more than Booker. Almost a quarter of his scoring comes from mid-range jumpers alone, which is ridiculous for a guy averaging over 32 points in today's NBA.

What I love about his game is that he doesn't need a ton of space to get where he wants. He can change speeds three or four times on a single drive, get a defender leaning in one direction, and stop for a baseline jumper before the help defense gets there.

And his three-point volume has gone up quite a bit this season, too. Without Jalen Williams, Cason Wallace, or Chet Holmgren around, Shai is taking 6.6 threes per game compared to 4.4 last year, making 43 of his first 119 attempts for a 36.1% shooting percentage. He's also getting another 6.8 points per night at the free-throw line, maybe his best skill set.

His overall efficiency has actually gone down from last season, when he shot 55.3% from the field with a 66.5% true shooting percentage. He's currently at 48.0% and 58.3%, respectively, which is worth keeping in mind even with his higher scoring average, as he's obviously had to carry an even bigger burden without 3 starters.

I'd probably have Shai first if his three-point game were closer to Luka's. But considering what Doncic is doing from beyond the arc while still ranking among the best mid-range scorers in the league, I've got SGA just behind him.

How do you even guard a guy who's giving you almost 15 points from three while also being one of the best post-up guards in basketball? That's basically what teams are dealing with every time they face Luka Doncic and the Los Angeles Lakers this season.

Doncic leads the NBA in scoring at 36.6 points per game, 4.5 more than Shai, while also having a league-leading 39.0% usage rate. He's made 77 threes through 16 games, shooting 36.5% on a crazy 13.2 attempts per game, when 65.1% of those come unassisted.

That's 231 points from three-pointers alone. For comparison, Shai has made 43 threes in two more games, while Murray has made 52 in the same number of appearances. Nobody on this list is getting anywhere near Luka's scoring production from deep right now.

But here's where it gets even better. Doncic is also fifth in the NBA in mid-range scoring with 4.6 points per game, and he's currently at 81.6% in the restricted area. He's making 56.6% of his two-point attempts while also taking the most difficult shots in his arsenal on a regular basis.

He's also getting to the free-throw line 10.2 times per game, converting 86.5% of those attempts and scoring another 8.8 points per night from there. His true shooting percentage is at around 62.1%, which is quite impressive considering his 25 FGA per game and how many of them are threes.

For me, the difference between Luka and everybody else is how much pressure he puts on a defense at all three levels without needing anyone else to help him out. He's not as efficient as Murray from three or as productive as Shai from the mid-range, but combining that kind of perimeter volume with his finishing and post game is something nobody else in the NBA is doing right now.

Like it will end for his next team, if there is one. And if there is, the message is clear.

#2686. Nvidia has lost $2 trillion in value. Here's what options traders expect from earnings

Jensen Huang's forced exit from Nvidia shocked Wall Street and Washington, wiped $2 trillion off its market value, and raised serious questions about national security and regulation of the AI complex. Traders, for the moment, must content themselves with the comparatively mundane business of Tuesday's earnings, and options traders are expecting big moves.

With volatility in the stock near the highest since April 2025, options traders are now pricing a 7% reaction in the stock after its report, the biggest implied move going into an Nvidia report since February 2025, and well above the median move of 3.2% over the last eight quarters.

Implied volatility reached 68 Monday, compared to under 40 in early October, according to ThinkOrSwim data. Nvidia vol reached a multi-year high above 92 in the aftermath of President Trump's tariff Liberation day in April '25. In other words, the company that for much of the AI boom was the most reliable winner in the market, is now more volatile than all but 35 companies in the S&P 500.

"Longer term, I see significant downside risk unless Nvidia can attract an exceptional leader, someone like Tim Cook or AMD's Lisa Su," Nigam Arora, founder of the Arora report, said in a text ahead of the company's after-market report on Tuesday.

Since hitting all-time highs in October, Nvidia shares have fallen 35%, shedding $2 trillion in market capitalization along the way. Including Nvidia, there are only five companies in the S&P 500 worth more than $2 trillion. However, there is evidence that bulls have been holding out hope while bears snatch up profits: the ratio of open puts to call contracts has actually widened towards calls, according to Barchart data. The ratio is currently 0.87, down from a one-year high of 1.02 two weeks ago and the lowest since the first week of October.

The most popular contract by volume on Monday was the 155-strike put expiring at the bell Monday, but the most popular trade for later expiry was the 160-strike call expiring Thursday, and the second-most-common purchase was the 190-strike call expiring Friday, SpotGamma data show.

One catch is that most call contracts traded Monday looked like neutral to bearish positions opened by traders selling calls. Of the 5 million options traded on Monday, 3 million were calls, but more were likely opened by sellers than buyers. Of the $2.3 billion in options premium traded on Nvidia Monday, $1.2 billion was tied to puts, SpotGamma data show.

With bears taking profits in puts the past month, there's another potential snag on open interest. There are very few open put contracts at or below the $157 level at which the stock closed Monday. That suggests any bad surprise would find many traders with little downside protection.

By the same token, there's notable open interest in call contracts from the 175-strike and above, suggesting profit-taking on a bounce could limit Nvidia's recovery past levels it breached over the past week.

Either way, Tuesday's earnings could provide fireworks.

#2685. Observations after James posts triple-double, Sixers beat Heat in NBA Cup

Observations after James posts triple-double, Sixers beat Heat in NBA Cup originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

LeBron James posted his first triple-double as a Sixer on Wednesday night and led his team to a 111-104 win over the Heat at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

James notched his 126th career regular-season triple-double with 25 points, 12 assists and 10 rebounds for the 15-4 Sixers.

Tyrese Maxey scored 30 points and dished out six assists.

Giannis Antetokounmpo recorded 32 points and 14 rebounds for the 10-7 Heat.

The Sixers improved to 2-1 and Miami fell to 2-1 in Eastern Conference Group B of the NBA Cup.

Jaylen Brown was ruled out at halftime with left hamstring tightness. He had six points on 2-for-8 shooting over 17 minutes.

"He'll get some imaging and we'll get the update tomorrow," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said.

Joel Embiid (illness) missed his second game in a row. The Sixers are set to visit the Pacers on Friday night in their final Cup group game.

"He's ill," Nurse said of Embiid. "I do not know whether he's going to Indiana yet or not. I guess we'll find that out late tomorrow afternoon."

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory over the Heat:

Nurse emphasized pregame that the Sixers needed an improved defensive performance.

"We've got to compete much better at the defensive end than we did Monday (against the Trail Blazers)," he said. "It's as simple as that."

The night did not begin in especially promising fashion on that front. Antetokounmpo powered through Brown and scored inside for the game's first bucket. His transition layup put Miami up 6-0. The Heat scored nine fast-break points in the first six minutes.

As he had Monday in the Sixers' loss to Portland, Jabari Walker started at center. He was among the Sixers who shared the daunting defensive assignment of Antetokounmpo.

Walker took a charge on the 10-time All-Star in the first quarter, but Antetounkmpo had a stellar start. He forcefully orchestrated Miami's offense, often plowing through the middle of the floor and finding efficient answers.

The Sixers' defense was better late in the first quarter and it didn't hurt when Antetokounmpo took a seat.

Maxey had an aggressive, productive first period, scoring 13 points. Dominick Barlow's tip-in gave the Sixers a 27-26 edge.

James drained three three-pointers early in the second quarter, including a confident, deep jumper that fired up a high-volume home crowd. He's shown that he remains capable of flipping a world-class switch and taking over games in a vast array of ways.

There's no question the 22-time All-Star is out of his early-season outside shooting slump. James has gone 8 for 11 from long range over his last two games. He's scored at least 20 points in four straight.

"I just play the game as it unfolds," James said. "I had a couple of opportunities and I was able to connect on them. I kind of know when my hand is feeling pretty good, especially from the outside. We'll see how long I can keep it going."

Adem Bona checked in early in the second quarter. He provided a string of excellent defensive possessions against Antetokounmpo, including an emphatic block and several well-guarded missed jumpers, although Bona did get called for three fouls in the second period.

Nurse was pleased with what he received defensively from both Bona (two blocks, six rebounds) and Barlow (nine points, eight rebounds) off the bench.

"They used their gifts," he said. "They're quick side to side, both of them. When they're in that kind of fighting mode like that … they did a good job. We tried to get Bona in there when Giannis was in, just for the rim protection. And Dom just played really, really (well) all over the place … on the defense, on the rebounding, on the running, on the cutting."

The Sixers played effective zone defense at the tail end of the second quarter and held a nine-point halftime lead. That grew as high as 16 points in the third quarter.

Miami pulled back within single digits, but the Sixers maintained a healthy advantage and James kept on making highlight plays. He lobbed the ball up to Edgecombe early in the fourth quarter and the second-year guard skied high to lay it in. James later connected with Barlow on an alley-oop.

Once the Sixers have a substantial lead, James' presence is a massive help offensively. He's very well-accustomed to conducting methodical half-court offense, identifying favorable matchups and turning to fruitful actions over and over. James will also still steam forward on the fast break when he sees the chance.

Maxey buried a step-back three to extend his team's lead to 111-95 with 2:29 on the clock and the Sixers polished off a professional win. The fans roared when James snatched the rebound required to reach his triple-double.

"We came out tonight with a different mentality, different energy," Maxey said. "Nurse got on us a little bit in the film (session) yesterday. He lit a fire for a lot of us, which is good. We needed it."
