#2682. AMD dives on short seller report of alleged China exposure

Shares of chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices close down 7.72% to $489.19 on Thursday after investment firm Hunterbrook Media issued a report alleging that the company "has its own China problem."

Hunterbrook disclosed that it had taken a short position in AMD.

The report claims that "after the U.S. blacklisted Chinese server maker IEIT Systems, the company kept getting newer AMD firmware, which enables AMD chips to run in servers."

CNBC did not independently verify this information.

Hunterbrook also said that new export rules in Washington could "tighten restrictions on AMD's foreign business," alleging that AMD "bills over 20% of its revenue to customers in China and Hong Kong," and "depends on a Chinese partner to finish most of its chips."

The allegations come amid investigations of rival chipmaker Nvidia over its sales to companies in Asia with ties to China. AMD has been viewed as a beneficiary of the uncertainty surrounding Nvidia.

"The claims misrepresent AMD's business practices and relationships," an AMD spokesperson said in a statement. "AMD complies with applicable US export control laws and regulations and maintains rigorous processes to ensure compliance across our global operations. We remain focused on serving our customers and upholding the highest standards of integrity."

Lisa Su, CEO of AMD, told investors on the company's recent earnings call that China is an "important" market and that the semiconductor giant wants to be fully compliant with U.S. export controls.

AMD continued to slide after hours, trading down 0.45% to $487 shortly after 4 p.m.

#2681. Leeds United are in the race to sign 18-year-old wonderkid

Leeds United are among the clubs tracking Leicester City academy winger Bismark Owusu ahead of the January window, with growing competition emerging for one of the Foxes' most highly rated young attackers.

According to TeamTalk, Leeds are in the mix alongside Brentford and Crystal Palace for the 18-year-old, a right-sided forward who has built real momentum through Leicester's youth system. The report states that Owusu "is attracting more and more interest from several clubs", with English and European sides now watching his situation closely.

Owusu's numbers at youth level explain why clubs are circling. Last season, he recorded 12 goals and seven assists in the Under-18 Premier League, a return that underlined his attacking output and pushed him firmly onto recruitment radars. He "has made steady progress through the Foxes' youth ranks" and has remained a regular contributor this term, while also stepping up for Leicester's Under-21s in the Premier League Cup and Premier League 2.

There has been no senior debut yet, although the assessment from the report is clear, Owusu "is very highly rated". That matters when clubs are planning for the medium and long term, especially in a market where top-end young talent is increasingly identified earlier and pursued more aggressively.

A key factor in this developing story is contractual status. Owusu "only recently turned 18 and is not yet tied to a professional contract at Leicester", which naturally increases external interest and places pressure on Leicester to act quickly. The club will want to keep hold of a player with clear upside, but TeamTalk adds that "the volume of interest from both English and continental clubs means they face a challenge in keeping him".

That challenge is sharpened by recent history. Leicester have already seen elite academy talent move on, most notably Jeremy Monga's exit to Manchester City in a £12.5million deal last summer. In that context, Owusu's future becomes one to monitor carefully as January approaches.

For Leeds, this feels like the type of move modern recruitment departments often explore early, before a prospect's value rises further. TeamTalk says "Leeds and Brentford are both exploring the possibility of a move when the January window opens", while "sources have also added Crystal Palace to the mix". Italian and Dutch clubs are also said to be monitoring the player.

Another detail could prove relevant in any cross-border interest. The report notes that "The youngster's of an Italian passport, in addition to eligibility for England and Ghana, is seen as a potential advantage for any clubs looking to complete a deal around Europe."

At this stage, "No formal bids or advanced negotiations for Owusu have been confirmed", but the direction of travel is obvious. Leicester are "working hard to secure the future of a talent who has serious potential", while Leeds and others weigh whether admiration turns into a concrete move.

This is exactly the sort of report that grabs the attention. Leeds have shown before that they are prepared to think beyond the obvious, and Owusu sounds like a player being identified before he reaches the wider spotlight. When a young winger posts 12 goals and seven assists at Under-18 level, clubs do not watch by accident.

The intriguing part is the profile. A right-sided attacker with production, age on his side and room to develop fits the kind of signing that can strengthen the pipeline rather than demand immediate first-team pressure. That feels sensible, especially if the club are trying to build depth with genuine upside instead of chasing only expensive, ready-made options.

There is also a familiar warning in this. If Brentford and Crystal Palace are involved, Leeds will know the competition is serious and well informed. Those clubs tend to do their homework. Add interest from Italy and the Netherlands, and it becomes clear that this is no casual link.

The contract angle makes it even more interesting. If Leicester have not yet secured him professionally, there may be an opening, but these situations can change quickly. The important thing is that Leeds are present early. Supporters will always get excited by senior names, but well-timed academy recruitment can be just as important. If this one progresses, it could be a very smart piece of business.

Wide receiver Amon-Ra St. Brown is another star for Detroit and he made a tough catch to pick up 17 yards on the way to the end zone. He also drew a pass interference penalty to set the Lions up on the 1-yard-line and Gibbs took care of things from there.

The Bears rallied for a touchdown to open the second half, but they're going to need to start getting some defensive stops to pull off a comeback win in Detroit.

#2678. Anthropic was supposed to save the AI trade. What went wrong?

Anthropic's historic IPO — the largest in U.S. history — delivered a big win for the company and its investors. But it failed at another task: reviving animal spirits in the AI trade.

The Nasdaq Composite closed down 2.25% amid a broader market pullback with the S&P 500 declining 2% to 7,175.

Investors had been looking forward to the Anthropic IPO as a "clearing event" — reviving the tech sector after months of choppy trading, the Nvidia espionage scandal, and growing concerns that AI chip sales to Asia are likely to slump as Washington cracks down on potential violations of export controls.

Some of those worries resurfaced Thursday as a short seller, Hunterbrook Media, alleged in a report that firmware sold by chipmaker AMD had turned up in U.S.-blacklisted companies. AMD stock closed down more than 7%. The chipmaker told CNBC it complies with U.S. law.

Adding to the worries, the House Foreign Affairs Committee released documents showing that a subagency of the Commerce Department approved the sale of more than 879,000 AI chips to Chinese companies. That also raised the prospect of an Asia sales slowdown under stricter export controls.

Many investors and analysts continue to express confidence in the AI trade, noting that Anthropic's successful market debut — the stock closed up 17% from its IPO price and raised more than $81 billion — bodes well for capital spending across tech.

But investors acknowledged that this is a high-tension moment and said it's time to be more strategic about the trade.

Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, described himself as a "circumspect bull," making the comparison to market conditions in 2022 when the big indices tanked in the back half of the year.

"You could have still made money that year. You just needed to have the right combination of longs and shorts," he said.

Part of the challenge, Niles said, has been a price war between the two cutting-edge frontier labs, Anthropic and OpenAI. That has posed a threat to the overall revenue and profit potential of AI and the capex going into it. Fierce competition on price is producing collective losses between the two top companies, he notes.

"The combined revenue growth for OpenAI plus Anthropic went from growing 75% sequentially in Q2 to 48% in Q3," he told CNBC. "The combined losses went from $7.9 billion to about $8.4 billion from Q2 to Q3."

While uncertainty is high now, some resolution may be on the horizon if a greater degree of commercial and regulatory clarity emerges over the next few months.

Trading experts also told CNBC that the tech sector's troubles Thursday were a more serious matter than Anthropic simply siphoning capital from other parts of the market.

"There's a reset of AI in general," said JJ Kinahan, senior vice president at Cboe Global Markets.

Investors for years have profited in tech by buying the dip, he said, but "there's been an attitude change that [you] can't buy things blindly and they'll all go up." Nvidia's ongoing declines have shaken confidence in the buy-the-dip strategy, he said.

Anthropic's next few trading days will be a key gauge of the market's strength. Friday will be a "wild card," Kinahan said. It could be a "final flush out" for the market, or a bounce that leaves the market in "no man's land" while volatility remains elevated.

A better gauge may be Monday, when options on Anthropic start trading, providing insight into whether retail investors will back the company in force — providing more of a vote of confidence in the AI trade.

"If people think we're going up [on Anthropic] they'll buy stock, but retail particularly will just buy calls," he said. "There's a bigger test coming Monday."

For now, investors continue to sell AI-related stocks.

Neoclouds, the generally highly indebted AI-dedicated computing providers at the outer edges of the AI trade, were down substantially Thursday, with CoreWeave closing down more than 6%.

Hyperscalers such as Alphabet and Amazon.com, viewed as relatively defensive AI bets, also finished lower.

One bright spot: Cybersecurity stocks continued to rally, with Palo Alto Networks and CrowdStrike both closing up. AI is finding security holes across software infrastructure, creating a scramble to patch the vulnerabilities before hackers can exploit them.

— CNBC's Daren Fonda contributed reporting.

#2677. Questions & Antlers: Early season impressions, featuring Nets Daily’s Anthony Puccio (Part 2)

Last week, we introduced you to Nets Daily's Editor-in-Chief, Anthony Puccio, with the premise that he, as a long-time Brooklyn Nets fan and writer, knows what we're going through this season following our beloved Milwaukee Bucks. In response, you came screaming off a high pick and roll, turned the corner—drawing the strongside defender—and hit him seams up in the corner with your questions. As you'll see below, he didn't let you down with the finish. Nylon.

stoneAge: Did you expect the rebuild to take so long and how far away are the Nets from being a playoff team again?

AP: I expected it to take long, but I figured they would have moved some assets to speed this thing up. I was wrong. We're looking at a Nets team currently two-three years away from playoffs—and that's being generous.

stoneAge: Which of the Nets' current players are part of their future core and what do the Nets lack the most to reach the next level?

AP: Mikel Brown Jr., Egor Dëmin, and Nolan Traore. The Nets lack a lot of things, particularly size in the front court. This isn't new. They lack athleticism. They lack three-point shooters. And they lack basic fundamentals required to build on good habits for a sustainable culture.

Mitchell Maurer: How do you navigate a landscape where so many people in the same geographic area are head-over-heels for the Knicks? A title is (as we've seen around here…) a surefire way to attract bandwagon fans, who are as vocal as they are anonymous 'round these Internet parts. The Nets are not a "nothing" franchise but they clearly don't carry the same weight (broadly speaking) as their cross-town counterparts. It's not a fair paradigm, to be considered an "afterthought" and I'm curious if there's similarities with how Bucks fans may have felt back in the heyday of the Packers and/or Brewers (who also just won a title!)

AP: Nets fans don't want to admit it, but there's absolutely an inferiority. Before the Knicks' chip, the one thing Nets fans had on Knicks fans was a better on-court product since 2000. Now we don't even have that! Put it this way: if you tell someone you're a Nets fan in NY, depending on who you tell, they might say, "Who?" Or "Why?"

Terrapin Revisited: What was it like to adjust expectations when the Nets went from title contenders to "Well, what exactly are we now?" after their big trades? Any recommendations for Bucks fans on how to adapt?

AP: There's no way of sugar-coating it—it feels like a dead end. You adapt when you simply learn to accept this is the new reality, and you never know when it might return. My recommendation: lean on the community to talk. It's difficult without picks to look forward to. Expect the worst, hope the best. Don't turn on each other though. (Editor's note: Sage advice—and one of the things I love about Brew Hoop).

Terrapin Revisited: What have you enjoyed most about the Nets during this rebuilding era? In other words, what can fans focus on as far as the bright side of such teams?

AP: Watching people in the organization make a name for themselves, hustling and shining in the shadows of the down years. Ideally it a player, but it can be any personnel. College basketball, too.

Terrapin Revisited: Do you have any insights on what to expect from veterans on the team at this time? A number of Bucks' vets are disappointing this year, I'm not sure how much that is to be expected when the stakes they are playing for are a lot lower vs. how much that provides some useful insights on the vets. What have you seen from different vets on the Nets?

AP: Right now I see two vets on the Nets who want to get their shots up and head out. It feels like they embrace the leadership role until the losses actually pile up.

Terrapin Revisited: What recommendations can you make on how to watch the development of young players on teams like the Nets and Bucks? What might we watch for—and what might we need to set aside—in order to more effectively watch at present, and appreciate what we have?

AP: It's all a matter of expectations and perspective. Seriously. Watch for where guys say they're working on their games.

Pono: Based on the Nets' rebuilding experience, how should the Bucks navigate developing young players with potential and very young players with potential? For context, last season Ryan Rollins emerged as the Bucks' top young player but this season the full-on rebuild approach has altered his role and, early on, stunted his growth.

AP: This is where the G League needs to become a necessity. Get those very young players burn instead of rotting on bench, trying to force their games. Let them have some time dominating at the lower level, or learning, getting stronger, and ready for when the time comes.

BarryDort: What do you think of the new NBA draft lottery? It seems like it puts more bad teams at the mercy of the ping pong balls. Unless you can somehow get a top five pick, it's really hard to find difference makers as you know from the Nets' last three drafts.

AP: Honestly, it doesn't feel like a long term solution to stop tanking.

Nate O.: With six first-round picks the last two drafts and 11 through 2033, it feels like the Nets are a great example of how to stack assets. I'm curious if you feel like this is a position of strength to be operating from, and only good things can unfold, or are there any other moves you wish the Nets had made or concerns you have moving forward?

AP: Concerns. They have eight players on rookie contracts, and getting them all necessary burn to develop has been extremely difficult. Taking five picks in one round felt a little silly at the time, and it's simply a handful for the coaching staff.

Southern Marxist: The Bucks front office has had to totally shift gears from spending any unused asset in an effort to get Giannis a second championship to accumulating and developing talent. Based on your observations of the Nets front office, what advice would you give the Bucks front office?

AP: Don't wait too long and be as transparent as possible. Fans know things aren't easy right now; I think they'd prefer to know what the direction is instead of paying for a product that's headed nowhere—or looks that way. The Nets keep their fans in the dark related to a lot of things here, and it comes across as disrespectful to paying customers.

I can't tell the experts how to do their job but at least communicate with fans. It's such a dramatic dropoff for the base of the brand—the face of it to—are gone. Reassure people that you can get back and how.

#2676. Here's a roadmap to the Aaron Rodgers developments, one of the craziest stories in years

If you were traveling on Wednesday or otherwise not plugged in for one of the most rollicking, unpredictable days in years if not ever, you’re in luck.

We’ve reconstructed the full timeline as to the events regarding now-former Steelers quarterback Aaron Rodgers.

It actually started on Monday, when coach Mike McCarthy was asked whether he has considered using one of the team’s other three quarterbacks when the offense is struggling. McCarthy, whose body language in hindsight suggests he was dreading the question, said without equivocation, “Aaron Rodgers is our quarterback.”

On Tuesday, Rodgers took his usual reps at practice. He conducted his usual midweek press conference. Unbeknownst to most of the media or the general public, the Steelers were changing their play-calling procedures to have quarterbacks coach Tom Arth, and not McCarthy, communicate the calls to Rodgers through the speaker in his helmet.

Then came Tuesday night. At 11:10 p.m. ET, the Steelers issued a statement — not from coach Mike McCarthy or G.M. Omar Khan or owner Art Rooney II but from team spokesman Burt Lauten — that Mason Rudolph will start on Friday against the Broncos and Rodgers will be inactive.

The news forced the Broncos to make a quick pivot in their preparations from Rodgers to Rudolph. As one source put it, the Broncos weren’t sure whether it was a good thing or a bad thing.

It was definitely a bad thing, all things considered, for the Steelers.

By Wednesday morning, reports emerged that it was a coach’s decision. At 8:00 a.m. ET, McCarthy informed the team of the decision.

Rodgers wasn’t there. As it turns out, he was excused.

Enter Pat McAfee. He teased a surprise guest at 1:40 p.m. ET. As expected, it was Rodgers.

Rodgers took a flamethrower to McCarthy, airing out dirty laundry that created a major mess for his now-former team. He exposed the fact that, at halftime of the Week 11 loss to the Eagles, McCarthy broke down the locker-room gathering by saying, “1-2-3 Packers.”

Rodgers also said McCarthy was falling asleep during some meetings and missing other meetings, expressing (in passive-aggressive fashion) concern for McCarthy’s health.

But Rodgers also said it was nothing new, and that McCarthy had done the same things in Green Bay, when they worked there together a decade ago.

Rodgers also made it clear he wouldn’t stay with the Steelers. After his session with McAfee and company, Gerry Dulac of the Pittsburgh Post-Gazette reported that the Steelers were expected to release Rodgers. By the official close of business, they’d waived him.

A question lingers as to whether the Steelers will try to deny Rodgers the balance of his $22 million salary. It won’t be easy; they’ll need to both void the guarantees and wipe out his eligibility for termination pay (in the form of his remaining salary) under the Collective Bargaining Agreement.

McCarthy met with reporters on Wednesday, after all hell had broken loose. He admitted to the “1-2-3 Packers” comment, calling it an “[e]mbarrassing moment.”

McCarthy also said that Rodgers was benched after issuing an “ultimatum” that was “clearly outside of his job description.”

ESPNFL thereafter reported that McCarthy “has the full support” of the organization, and that the team is “not planning to make any immediate changes.” The key word may be “immediate"; with few viable options only two days before a Black Friday game against the Broncos, changes could come later.

The overriding takeaway is that someone with the Steelers knew or should have known that McCarthy and Rodgers couldn’t co-exist. Because, obviously, they didn’t co-exist.

If, as Rodgers claims, McCarthy believed Rodgers got him fired in Green Bay, McCarthy shouldn’t have wanted a reunion with Rodgers. And if, as Rodgers claims, McCarthy was doing the same kinds of things in Green Bay that he did in Pittsburgh, Rodgers shouldn’t have wanted a reunion with McCarthy.

The reunion has now ended abruptly, with seven games left in the 2026 regular season. It remains to be seen whether Rodgers wants to play with another team, or whether another team wants him. Any team that signs him risks being on the receiving end of a future gripe session from Rodgers in which he lists all the things that team and/or the coach did wrong — without ever acknowledging his own role in the reality that it didn’t work out.

The good news, if there is any, is this: If Rodgers never plays again, his final NFL pass will have been a touchdown.

Which is a better outcome than if he’d retired after the 2025 season. His final pass that year was a pick-six in the playoffs.

#2675. Cathie Wood’s Ark Invest was a big buyer of Anthropic as she tells CNBC she’s ‘quite optimistic’

Cathie Wood was a major buyer of Anthropic following its debut Thursday on the Nasdaq, adding shares in all her major funds.

Wood's firm, which already owned private shares of the artificial intelligence company in its Ark Venture Fund leading up to the initial public offering, bought $90.8 million worth of Anthropic in six funds, according to a trade notification from the firm.

"We've owned Anthropic since early '23," Wood told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Friday. "It's our best performing equity by far in ARKVX, our venture fund. ... Our confidence has grown with time as it really took over the enterprise" AI business.

Anthropic shares jumped 17% in their first day, but closed well off the highs of the session and were headed for sizable losses Friday. The Nasdaq, which was expected to get a boost from a major IPO reviving enthusiasm about the technology trade, fell by 2% on Thursday.

"We're climbing a wall of worry," said Wood. "Everybody is worried about AI hype and the political situation after the election, but I think we'll look back and say, OK this is more like the '80s and '90s, climbing a wall of worry you know with bricks much like what we have today: inflation, interest rates, wars, new technologies and so forth so quite optimistic here."

The Anthropic stakes are relatively small across the funds, but Wood typically will buy shares in companies she likes steadily during long stretches. Wood said she is "averaging down" with her purchases of Anthropic.

In her $6.1 billion Ark Innovation ETF, Anthropic is a 0.3% stake. The biggest holdings in that fund are Tesla and SpaceX, representing more than 21% of the fund together.

Ark Innovation, which retail investors piled into during the pandemic as it gained about 150% in 2020, tumbled the subsequent next two years, losing 67% in 2022 alone. Wood's following was dented after the big losses.

The fund had a comeback stretch as the bull market revved back up from 2023 to 2025, but has fallen on hard times again this year, down more than 10% in 2026. The S&P 500 is up 4.8% this year.

Wood dismissed comparisons of today's environment to the dot-com bubble.

"Back then if an Anthropic had gone public, it probably would have gone up threefold the first day," she said. "That's how crazy things were."

#2674. Micah Parsons: Our play in second half Wednesday night was inexcusable

Packers defensive end Micah Parsons made his return to the lineup against the Rams on Wednesday night, but he wasn’t able to help his team to a win in Los Angeles.

Parsons picked up a sack late in the first half that sent the game to the break with the score tied 13-13, but he couldn’t make an impact in the second half because the Rams barely threw the ball. After running 12 times for 46 yards in the first half, the Rams ran 35 times for 194 yards while outscoring the Packers 13-0 in the final 30 minutes.

After the game, Parsons said it is “just inexcusable” to allow opposing teams to “dictate games like that” and said the aftermath has to be an increased level of accountability for the entire defense.

“We need to figure out who’s going to be a dog or not,” Parsons said. “That’s just the reality of it. That’s just how I really feel about it. I wasn’t able to get on the field because of it, because they didn’t want to waste my plays on first and second down I think the whole thing is just bad and we’ve got to figure out who’s willing to play with us or who’s playing against us at this point.”

The Packers don’t have the luxury of having time to figure things out. The loss dropped them to 6-5 on the season and the makeup of the NFC means that they’re going to have to win most of their remaining games to have any chance of advancing to the postseason.

#2673. Cesc Fàbregas speaks on potential Barcelona return

Como head coach Cesc Fàbregas has this week spoken out on the possibility of taking his talents back to his former stomping grounds at La Liga champions Barcelona.

This of course comes amid ongoing speculation tipping the Spaniard as a potential long-term replacement for Hansi Flick in Catalunya's capital.

Fàbregas and Flick's contracts with their respective clubs are due to expire in the summer of 2028. The former, meanwhile, has long been earning for himself a reputation as one of European football's standout up-and-coming tacticians.

On the bench at Italian outfit Como, Fàbregas has defied the odds en route to establishing his club as one of Serie A's leading lights.

Boasting Barcelona DNA courtesy of his time spent at La Masia, the former Blaugrana midfielder is in turn considered an almost inevitable future arrival back at the Camp Nou.

Speaking during an appearance in front of the media this week, Fàbregas was therefore drawn on this very topic. And the Spaniard went on to provide an in-depth response, as follows:

"I'm one of those who live day by day. Thinking about the future usually doesn't get you anywhere.

"I'm from there, I grew up at that club, and I know everyone, but I've never liked thinking too far ahead. The times I have, it hasn't gone well. Life is like piloting a plane; you never know when it's going to brake. That's why we have to try to enjoy ourselves. When it's time to compete, compete; when it's time to enjoy ourselves, enjoy ourselves; when it's time to be with family, be with family. I try to be a balanced person within the competitiveness and demands of being the coach of a club like Como, where we are growing, and I have so many responsibilities.

"I enjoy it and know how to maintain the high standards to keep growing at this club and, above all, in my job. But like I said, I focus on the day-to-day, because thinking about the future usually doesn't get you anywhere. I love real football, and Barça plays at an incredible level."

#2672. Tony Romo re-emerges, as an employee of a gambling company

The unofficial path for a sports-media second act is becoming more and more clear.

Tony Romo, whose career with CBS ended earlier this year in the aftermath of a DUI arrest that would have been overlooked if the company was otherwise happy with his work, will be the “chief predictions officer” at Betr.

The press release leans in to Romo’s reputation for being able to predict plays before they happen. This overlooks the fact that he got away from doing that after his first year or so in the CBS booth. It also glosses over the reality that his access to practice and production meetings equipped him with the ability to make “predictions” that (based on his inside information) also could be called “spoilers.”

It’s not entirely clear what he’ll be doing. The release says that Romo will “serve as a prominent face of the Betr brand, appearing in original content, delivering sports predictions and engaging fans across the company’s expanding ecosystem of gaming products.”

Of course, Betr won’t want his predictions to be too accurate — as it relates to its sportsbook. The sportsbooks only win when the customers lose. If the customers win too much, the sportsbook will land in one of two chapters: 11 or 7.

Prediction markets are different. The companies make money on the transactions, with the participants balancing out wins and losses in the zero-sum game of who’s right and who’s wrong.

The next question is whether this is the first step for Romo, or whether it’s his primary post-CBS play. With the NFL and its broadcast partners aligned with the Coke and Pepsi of sports gambling, Romo’s relationship with one of the various companies that hopes to establish itself as the RC Cola won’t improve his shot at getting back into a prominent position in a broadcast booth.

Regardless, Romo is back. At least he’s not working for an illegal offshore sportsbook.

Either way, his message will be the same to the masses as it is to those who are looking for a fresh foothold in sports media.

#2671. Knicks Bulletin: ‘We haven’t arrived’

In the least surprising development in the NBA on Wednesday, the Knicks beat the Cavaliers.

Here are very few quotes from yesterday's affair and elsewhere. Enjoy Thanksgiving today, and make sure to save the leftovers!

On the benefits of potentially missing the NBA Cup knockout rounds:

"It is important (to win and potentially advance), but the reality of it is, when you look at the schedule, and you see that our percentage [to advance] is not that high, and so they kind of shared with me what our schedule would look like if we don't go and if we do go. And you look at it, you're like, 'Whoa, that's a lot of days off if you don't go.' And so again, you want to compete; you want to win. But if you don't, in my opinion, when you look at the schedule… you're like, there's an opportunity if you got some guys banged up to get rest and/or to work on some things in between the games that you have if you're not involved in the Cup."

On the physical and mental toll of competing in the NBA Cup:

"I didn't really break it down to an 83rd game, but when I talk about the Cup, it adds a level of stress maybe on your mind and your body because the games are intense as you go along and you're playing more games in a short amount of time.

"I remember it was like, game, game, game, and then you win the Cup, and you celebrate it that night, and you hop on a plane and go play in Indiana the next night. And so it's great. You like the competition and the setup, but it adds a lot of stress to you physically and mentally. I'm saying this because especially with the extended season we had last year and how taxing the games were for our playoff run mentally and physically, I'm trying to be smart, so if — knock on wood — we end up advancing, I'll try to be smart about it."

On refusing to chase regular-season wins at all costs and learning from Golden State's 73-win season:

"I try not to chase wins, especially early in the season. You want to win, but I'm not going to win every game at all costs.

"I wasn't part of the staff [for the first Golden State title] [Golden State] won Steve [Kerr's] first year. Then they lost in the second year, but they set a regular season record of 73 wins, and one of the things that he used to tell me, they gassed themselves trying to get this record and come playoff time they just didn't have enough in the tank to close it. And so that always sits right here for me."

On calling Pacôme Dadiet into his office and threatening to remove him from the rotation over his lack of effort:

"I didn't think he played hard in the game before. And I actually called him into my office and showed him some film. I told him, 'As a young guy, if you don't play hard, I can't play you.'

"And I said, 'Believe me, I got a lot of guys I can go to. I'm not afraid to go to somebody else. So the least you could do is play hard.' And I showed him how he wasn't. And I thought [Wednesday], he played hard."

On leaving OG Anunoby's injury management to the medical staff:

"There are a lot of medical terms that are above my head. They tell me and I don't really pick up on the injury. If he's out, I change my minutes sheet. And then I'll just wait for them to tell me when he's back. [Wednesday], OG's individual work happened before shootaround and I didn't even know he had individual work until his player development guy told me later on. You never want a guy to hurt himself more. But I don't think about it."

On the Knicks still having plenty of room for improvement despite winning 12 of their last 15 games:

"We're 12-3 since the start, but our s–t still does stink. We haven't arrived, and we still have to embrace the little things."

On Brown experimenting with an unusually deep rotation against Cleveland:

"I looked at the bench in the first quarter and realized, 'Wow, we played like 10 people in that first quarter.'"

On endorsing Tom Thibodeau as a potential head coaching hire for struggling NBA franchises:

"I made three playoffs with Thibodeau as my coach so his track record is one of success. So of course if I was a team that wanted to get back on track, I'd look at Tom Thibodeau to be my coach."

On Thibodeau's tactical knowledge, dedication and ability to prepare his teams to win:

"It's the X's and O's and the amount of care he puts into his craft, his team. He steps into that gym every day and, you know, you can say anything you want about the way he coaches, but one thing I know is his team is prepared to win a game. So that's a testament to who he is as a coach, the preparation he puts his team through and also his ability to have his team ready for the moment."

#2669. As Anthropic shares tank, investors scramble to hedge. It could be very expensive

As Anthropic shares tumble toward their IPO price of $83, investors are looking for ways to hedge. Unfortunately, they may have to wait until Monday, when options on the AI giant are expected to list.

If Anthropic breaks its IPO price, it will represent a 27% decline from Thursday's intraday high of $113. The wild price swings offer clues on how expensive it might be to buy protection on the new tech giant once options begin trading.A 20% intraday move points to an implied volatility somewhere between 100 and 150 for Anthropic options, according to estimates from single-stock market-makers on the Cboe trading floor in Chicago.

Options traders are also looking at how SpaceX traded in the days following its offering for clues on how options might price.

Implied volatility in SpaceX started at 116, but that didn't deter options traders big and small from piling into the contracts tracking the stock, with about 1.7 million contracts traded on the first day for a total options premium of about $2.5 billion. Far out-of-the-money call contracts were some of the most popular trades as the stock soared 40% in its first three sessions.One key difference between now and then? Anthropic is trading against the backdrop of S&P 500 volatility close to 30, compared to 17.3 when SpaceX debuted. "I expect ANT options to be super active," said Tom Sosnoff, co-founder of the trading platforms ThinkOrSwim and TastyTrade. "I expect heavy options volume."One thing Anthropic and SpaceX have in common is heavy retail involvement in both IPOs, with retail brokerages receiving a 20% allocation from SpaceX and reports pointing to between a 15-20% allocation from Anthropic on Thursday. "Round one of the Anthropic IPO easily goes to the individual investor," Sosnoff added. "Retail participants bailed early at the best prices of the day while institutional investors paid up to top off their underwhelming allocations. I believe retail was aggressively reloading in the mid-90s."Further signs of retail demand will become evident when options likely begin trading on Monday. One thing to watch: the spread between out-of-the-money call and put contracts, which was heavily skewed toward calls in the case of SpaceX. "I would be careful comparing Anthropic to SpaceX," said Dennis Davitt, CIO and Co-Founder of Millbank Dartmoor Portsmouth. "From a market-structure perspective, the two are dramatically different. The availability and cost of borrowing will be different, the companies are at very different stages of maturity, and SpaceX has allowed employees to monetize portions of their holdings over time. All of those factors matter when pricing options."

#2668. Kamren Kinchens: No ill intent on hit that concussed Christian Watson

The Rams defense pitched a shutout in the second half on Wednesday night and they did it without safety Kamren Kinchens in the lineup.

Kinchens was ejected from the game just before halftime because of a hit to the head of Packers wide receiver Christian Watson. Kinchens was initially flagged for unnecessary roughness and the penalty was upgraded to a disqualification after video review by league officials.

After the game, Kinchens said he couldn’t really argue with the decision but that he didn’t regret the play because Watson’s injury was an unintended consequence of trying to make a play.

Watson was ruled out for the rest of the game with a concussion and will have over a week to clear the protocol before the Packers’ Week 13 game against the Saints.

As stocks continue their tech-driven sell-off, prediction markets traders are seeing a bleaker outlook for the remainder of the year.

In that scenario, the Stock Trader's Almanac thinks more churn is to come. "The low is more likely in mid- to late December than this week."

#2666. The clock is ticking on Mike McCarthy's time with the Steelers

When it comes to the nuclear war of words that Aaron Rodgers started on Wednesday, the end result is the same as it would be if the world’s superpowers ever started hunting and pecking the launch codes.

Rodgers, as multiple former Green Bay Packers are making clear, is destroying his reputation and legacy as a teammate by breaking the fundamental code of the locker room. But he’s also destroying Mike McCarthy’s reputation and legacy as a coach.

For a change, Rodgers’s rant was centered in reality. ESPNFL reports that the things Rodgers said about McCarthy are true.

Which could make it impossible for the Steelers to keep McCarthy beyond the 2026 season.

His only path to salvation is to win. Starting on Friday, when the 8-2 Broncos come to town for a game against a Steelers team that, despite the sudden dysfunction, is 6-4.

It’s really that simple. The next seven games will become the litmus test as to whether McCarthy’s now-documented snoring-and-ice-cream-sandwiches flaws are outweighed by his capabilities.

Of course, the fact that Rodgers rolled a grenade into the locker room on his way out won’t make that any easier. Which could be one of the reasons why Rodgers did it.

Maximum chaos will make it harder for McCarthy to win. Which will make it harder for McCarthy to make it to Year 2. Which will create the impression that Rodgers has been vindicated.

Sure, the initial word is that the situation doesn’t impact McCarthy’s standing. But the most important aspect of Wednesday’s report from ESPNFL that McCarthy “has the full support of the organization” carries an important qualifier.

The Steelers are “not planning to make any immediate changes.”

So they’re not firing McCarthy or asking him to resign before Friday’s game. What happens if the Steelers lose by double digits to the Broncos? What happens if the current two-game losing streak becomes an extended skid that sweeps them out of the AFC postseason field?

The answer will become obvious based on how the final seven games unfold. In the immediate aftermath of Aaron Rodgers dumping Agent Orange on the organization, it’s hard to imagine McCarthy surviving this.

Ultimately, the decision will be made by owner Art Rooney II. And the outcome could show where the Steelers reside on the line that divides the functional from dysfunctional franchises.

Functional teams admit mistakes and commence rectifying them. Dysfunctional teams deny the facts and double down.

#2665. Steelers Owner Makes Major Decision on Mike McCarthy’s Future After Aaron Rodgers’ Rant

Steelers take aim at Mike McCarthy. Getty

The Pittsburgh Steelers will be sticking with Mike McCarthy as their head coach.

The Steelers released future Hall of Famer Aaron Rodgers after he was benched and then took shots at head coach Mike McCarthy.

Pittsburgh is off to a 6-4 start and is holding onto a playoff spot.

After Rodgers went on a lengthy rant about McCarthy and why he isn't a good coach, Steelers owner Art Rooney II is sticking by the coach.

Rooney broke his silence on the incident. He spoke to Gerry Dulac of the Pittsburgh Gazette and said he will be sticking with McCarthy as the Steelers head coach.

"I just want to say that I have 100% confidence in Mike McCarthy and I expect he'll be our coach this season, next season and beyond," Rooney said. "The big thing, what happened this week, is insubordination can't be tolerated in any organization. When you start getting ultimatums, you can't live with that."

Rooney said Rodgers never once said anything about McCarthy and his health to him during his tenure with the Steelers.

So, Rooney admits he is disappointed with how Rodgers handled the situation and went public with it all.

"I'm disappointed the way it came out," Rooney said. "We certainly had hopes that he would be successful here, and I thought at times we certainly got on a roll. We had high expectations. It's easy to say, knowing what I know today, what would I do? But we had a good experience last year, and so I kind of wasn't expecting it to go this way this year."

McCarthy is in his first year as the Steelers head coach.

Not only does Rooney plan to stick with McCarthy, but he believes the head coach did a good job defending the quarterback.

Rooney believes McCarthy took bullets for Rodgers when the offense was struggling, instead of the blame going to the quarterback.

"The sad thing is Mike took some of the bullets for Aaron in terms of some of the plays that were called. Particularly in the red-zone troubles that we had," Rooney said. "Mike took some of the bullets for things that were going wrong down there. He tried to protect him, and it just wasn't working."

The Steelers will now turn their attention to the Denver Broncos on Friday afternoon.

#2664. The Nasdaq is in freefall. Here's one stock to look at

I asked Claude after Friday's close what I should do about the tanking stock market. Its advice was surprisingly useful: avoid panic selling and look for opportunities. A 40 VIX, which it touched Friday, might provide those opportunities. Specifically, a higher VIX (higher options premiums) signals fear, but it also creates an opportunity to sell elevated premium in some premium stocks, like Amazon.

Amazon's cloud business is growing at its fastest pace in nearly five years. AWS revenue rose 39.3% YoY in the third quarter, and management reported a $578 billion backlog. Trailing 12-month operating cash flow rose 29% to $168.3 billion. However, infrastructure investment accelerated, resulting in more than $42 billion in negative cash flow. The competing forces of accelerating growth and the cost of supplying it could keep the stock in its range over the next four weeks. Its shares are only off 14% from its recent highs, comparatively better than the Nasdaq Composite's near 19% decline.

AWS demand supports the lower end of the range. The backlog gives Amazon a basis for adding capacity even as investors scrutinize the cash cost. Cash spending could temper an upside rerating.

The next leg higher may require evidence that new capacity can earn attractive returns at this scale, and that evidence may be in short supply over the next couple of months.

Sell the Dec. 18, 2026 AMZN 235/285 strangle for an indicative $5.90 credit per share.

Max reward — $590 per strangle if AMZN finishes between $235 and $285 at expiration, before fees.

Breakevens at expiration — $229.10 and $290.90.

Max risk — Unlimited on the uncovered call, but one should think of the trade as risking getting short at a net price above $290, a nearly 13% premium to today's closing price. The downside loss would reach $22,910 per strangle if AMZN fell to zero. The credit is roughly 2.3% of a $260 reference share price over four weeks, or nearly 30% on a simple annualized basis. That comparison uses the share price as its denominator. Actual return on capital depends on margin and the cost to close or settle the trade.

Buying in bear markets, as the Nasdaq just touched, is never easy. But those opportunities don't present themselves too often. What are you going to do with it?

#2663. The stealing, running Mavs are the perfect cast for a high-IQ duo that could change basketball

The Mavericks just beat a very good Denver Nuggets team through aggressive defense, forcing steals, and running on offense. It is indeed a new day in Dallas.

Kyrie Irving, a player that has pretty much seen it all by now, didn't even have an efficient night (5/19 on field goal attempts, 1/8 from three). He did, however, lead the team to a victory that shows the whole league that the Dallas Mavericks now are the exact opposite of who they used to be.

In a gesture as moving as it was telling, Kyrie showed the world that he and Cooper Flagg have the rarest of connections. This is a high-IQ duo who could change the trajectory of what basketball looks like. And they may be on the way to doing that as we speak.

It is not only the gesture that's important here, it's the ultimate look away pass and the back door cut by Flagg that shows a connection between the two, usually built through years of ups and downs. It was almost like they could read each other's minds. This is what real chemistry looks like.

Kirk Henderson said it best: "There are lookaway passes, then there's something like this where I feel as if Kyrie's head never even moved the direction where Coop cut to."

Jamal Crawford interpreted the gesture as "me and you", and either way this is a gesture of connection, we – as in us – we got this. And even though Kyrie meant Cooper, we all feel it a little bit.

You may be watching from across the globe, you may not even be a Mavs fan, but anybody watching, knowing what this team and city have gone through recently, felt it.

Because no one has worked harder to get the Dallas Mavericks back together than Kyrie Irving. After it was torn apart, he did everything he could to hold it together. He mentored, he was everyone's vet, he led, he coached when injured – he showed the way and the light in the dark times.

And Dallas is reaping the benefits right now. That is also why it felt so touching, seeing the vet who worked so hard to hold it all together, make this gesture to the young superstar with the highest ceiling imaginable.

They took away his running mate, his brother and a genuine friendship and on-court chemistry, resulting in the most negative period in Dallas, in which he forced himself to play too much and carry more than he should have. His resulting ACL injury took him away from his craft for more than a year, and now – finally – his hard work is really paying off. Kyrie Irving deserves this.

And despite the spiritual experience it can be to watch Irving play, especially when he has chemistry with his teammates, he didn't carry the team to this extraordinary win. Neither did his co-star Flagg really.

It was a cast of teammates who decided to outwork, outhustle and believe in their connection, along with a well-prepared coach, who had clearly done a lot of work to get this team ready. It was no easy feat. But the Mavs made it look easy.

They had 13 steals. The league leader averages 10.6 (Los Angeles Lakers). The Mavs average 9.44 this season, but led by the surprising hero of the last games, Caleb Martin, with five, they kept the pressure on the passing lanes all game, stressing the Nuggets in a way we don't see every day. Not only that, they had 16 steals last game versus Utah, and a whopping 50 steals the last four games (which all happen to be wins).

Martin started this game on fire on both ends, secured the first six points by knocking down two threes in a row and continued to force five steals in the first half, constantly the first to run in transition.

This is exactly the leadership a team needs from a role player next to superstars, and with Zaccharie Risacher next to him, the two have the potential to cause havoc defensively on every team in the league, followed by easy transition points, if they keep this up.

Caleb Martin explained the game plan in the half:

"High sense of urgency. A high level of respect for a great team," he said during the broadcast.

He went on to explain that steals were an emphasis, as well as just being active. "The more active we are," Martin said, "sense of urgency, the better a team we are, positive things happen".

And not even interview questions were about to distract Caleb Martin, who is on a mission in Dallas right now.

When asked in the half whether the loud colors of the court, which are usually a little on the bright side during Cup games, was giving him a headache, too, he answered deadpan:

"I don't know, I'm trying to win a game right now."

#2662. Kyle Shanahan on Brandon Aiyuk signing with Commanders: "They got a good player"

The Aaron Rodgers rigmarole has pushed plenty of other stories out of the spotlight. Including the sudden second act for Brandon Aiyuk.

That’s still weeks away. But it will be here quickly. And Commanders-49ers is one of the various potential permutations that could play out as the playoffs approach and the postseason commences.

#2661. Lions score touchdown after Dan Campbell opts for safety on Bears' illegal batting penalty

The high-powered Thanksgiving matchup between the Detroit Lions and the Chicago Bears delivered early, with a wild sequence just before halftime. After accepting a potentially risky safety on an unusual Bears penalty, the Lions converted a touchdown to close out the first half with a 19-10 lead.

The potentially game-changing sequence started after the two-minute warning in the second quarter, with Chicago quarterback Caleb Williams getting sacked and fumbling the ball at the Bears' two-yard line.

Once out of Williams' hands, the ball bounced past multiple players into the Chicago end zone. Bears offensive lineman Darnell Wright, in an effort to stave off a safety, used a close fist to bat the ball out of the end zone, where Lions defensive back Brian Branch was able to get his hands on it.

But Wright's efforts to get the ball out of the end zone were not allowed: The lineman was flagged for "illegal batting," which results in a safety if the call is accepted.

The situation presented Lions head coach Dan Campbell with a decision: Take the safety and try to put together a touchdown drive before halftime, or decline the penalty to get the ball and set up a red-zone drive.

Campbell decided to go for the safety, giving him and the Lions a minute and change to grab a touchdown and justify the choice.

Initially, the drive got off to a concerning start: The Lions, who had already been called for two false starts earlier in the quarter, picked up two more false start penalties in a row to set up third-and-12. Jared Goff then erased some of the distance by finding Jahmyr Gibbs for an 11-yard reception, but the catch fell just short of first down.

On fourth-and-1, though, Detroit pulled off a gutsy and successful quarterback sneak, giving Goff the two-yard rush to keep the drive going. That set up a massive 39-yard throw from Goff to Jameson Williams, before another first down to Gibbs, and a successful touchdown throw to Sam LaPorta.

In an interview on the broadcast after halftime, Campbell told CBS that the number of timeouts left — the Bears had two at the time, while the Lions had all three — and his faith in his players led him to go for the full nine-point play. He praised Jameson Williams and Goff for their plays, and said that he "just felt good" about the offense's chances to find the end zone.

Illegal batting, the penalty that sparked the whole sequence, is a relative rarity in the league. One of the only recent examples came against the Lions, with Seattle Seahawks linebacker K.J. Wright batting a fumble out of the end zone in 2015, though the foul was not flagged. The NFL later admitted that the penalty should have been called.

#2660. After his Pittsburgh exit, what's next for Aaron Rodgers?

As Aaron Rodgers enters waivers, the first question is whether any team will claim the balance of his one-year contract.

Obviously, no one will do it without ensuring that Rodgers actually wants to play for that team. Unless, of course, a team or two hopes to troll Rodgers by forcing him to show up to play where he doesn’t want to play. Like Marty Schottenheimer did to Deion Sanders in 2002.

So, yes, it would be hilarious if the Jets decide to put in the proper paperwork.

The more likely outcome is that he’ll clear waivers and become a free agent. It will give him the ability to sign with any team, at any time.

No clear-cut contender currently needs a starter. And it would make no sense for Rodgers to sidle up to a quarterback-needy also-ran, like the Cardinals.

Some have suggested that Rodgers could join the Patriots, who will be missing Drake Maye while they try to parlay 5-5 into playoff position. His August comments about the Mike Vrabel-Diana Russini situation would potentially be an impediment to that outcome.

There’s a spicy possibility of Rodgers aligning with the Ravens, who still play the Steelers twice. Is he that petty? At this point, it’s impossible to say he’s not.

Rodgers’s best play could be to wait. Let fate decide. Last year, Broncos quarterback Bo Nix broke his ankle late in a playoff win over the Bills. If Denver’s instant desperation caused them to consider dusting off Drew Brees (despite the fact that he can’t throw a football with his dominant arm), they would have surely called Rodgers, if he’d been available.

Something like that can happen again. Of course, a team that loses its starter to injury between now and the Super Bowl would have to want to do business with him. Which means accepting the very strong possibility that, in time, Rodgers will have grievances to air about his experience.

Because if it goes poorly, it won’t be his fault. There will be other reasons for his struggles. Reasons that he has shown he has no qualms about sharing in his national-media safe space.

During his 22-year career, Rodgers has played for three NFL teams. He ended up disgruntled in each location. If he adds a fourth franchise to the list, he’ll likely find a way to be disgruntled there, too.

Unless it’s a very short-term arrangement. As short as one or two playoff games.

#2659. Top Wall Street analysts favor these 3 dividend stocks for strong returns

Investors often favor dividend stocks for their regular income and the promise of potential share price appreciation. But selecting the right dividend stocks from a huge pool of companies can be difficult.

Top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend-paying stocks, as their recommendations are backed by thorough analysis of a company's financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Devon Energy (DVN), an independent oil and natural gas exploration and production company, reported better-than-expected third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of 32 cents per share for the fourth quarter, payable December 30. Based on the fixed dividends paid over the trailing 12 months, DVN yields 2.85%.

After hosting a virtual investor meeting with management, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Devon, with a price target of $59. Mehta said that he is now more confident in management's outlook for delivering strong near-term capital returns as well as efforts to drive operational efficiencies and productivity gains.

Devon sees an opportunity to increase share repurchases, driven by solid free cash flow (FCF), contributions from the Eagle Ford divestiture and continued improvement in the balance sheet, Mehta said. Devon expects share buybacks worth more than $1 billion in Q4 2026, up from $414 million in the third quarter, backed by a strong pricing backdrop and improved capital efficiency.

Mehta, a 5-star analyst, said Devon expects about $6.3 billion of excess FCF in 2027, including about $3.5 billion of net proceeds from the Eagle Ford sale. That would enable the company to accelerate buybacks while progressing toward its goal of reducing gross debt to about $9 billion by early 2027. Mehta noted that under DVN's framework to return up to 70% of annual FCF, management favors share buybacks as it believes the stock is discounted compared to large-cap peers.

"Management expects continued capital efficiency gains from merger synergies, extended laterals, surfactant adoption and development of the newly acquired federal acreage," Mehta said.

Mehta ranks No. 876 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 8.1%. See Devon Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Energy Transfer (ET), is a limited partnership that operates about 140,000 miles of pipeline and associated energy infrastructure. The company recently announced third-quarter results showing net income per common unit growing about 18% year-over-year, to 33 cents. ET's cash distribution for Q3 2026 was 34.25 cents per common unit, up more than 3% year-over-year. At an annualized distribution of $1.37 per share, Energy Transfer yields more than 7%.

TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Energy Transfer with a price target of $25. The company delivered better-than-expected Q3 results and announced the 1.2 billion cubic feet per day (bcf/d) South Mississippi Pipeline, a 215-mile, 36-inch natural gas line linking the Perryville Hub to the Florida Gas Transmission (FGT) network, Gabelman said. The project is supported by 20-year firm transportation agreements and is expected to enter service in 2030. ET expects to ultimately own 51% of the $1.6 billion project.

The 5-star analyst said that Energy Transfer lowered its 2026 capital spending outlook to $5.5 billion (midpoint of the guidance range) from $5.75 billion due to project timing. Energy Transfer expects capex to rise to $7 billion in 2027, while Gabelman projects $5 billion in both 2028 and 2029, based on disclosed projects.

Gabelman also highlighted management's commentary about optimization initiatives contributing an incremental $800 million to 2026 earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization, a larger benefit than in recent years. "A return to less commodity volatility should the [Middle East] war end could bring some relative downside, though this is offset by continued strong growth prospects," he said.

Gabelman ranks No. 397 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.4%. See Energy Transfer KPIs on TipRanks.

Oil and gas exploration and production company ConocoPhillips (COP) delivered better-than-expected Q3 performance. The company announced an 11% hike in its quarterly dividend to 93 cents per share, effective with the Q4 dividend payable on December 1. Based on the dividends paid over the trailing 12 months, COP yields about 2.6%.

Following the Q3 results, Wells Fargo analyst Sam Margolin repeated a buy rating on ConocoPhillips, citing strong potential for further dividend increases. Margolin also trimmed his price target to $185 from $189 to reflect timing effects and the WAHA forward curve, though he said that COP could still re-rate as its natural portfolio lifecycle provides stronger protection against inflationary pressures than peers. The Waha forward curve tracks future natural gas prices based on the West Texas Waha hub.

Margolin, another 5-star analyst, highlighted that ConocoPhillips' Q3 2026 adjusted earnings per share of $3.27 was well above Wells Fargo and the Street's estimates of $3.04 and $3.05, respectively. Earnings were driven by solid price realizations, partially offset by higher depletion, depreciation and amortization and purchased commodities.

Margolin also noted COP's dividend increase and said the accelerated buyback enabled by current commodity prices could further support long-term dividend growth. He sees the possibility of ConocoPhillips' dividend growth rate exceeding its current 6% cost of equity into the end of the decade. Margolin expects COP to sustain an 8% dividend compound annual growth rate through 2029 while repurchasing $6.4 billion of stock annually, and reducing net debt-to-capital to less than 9% by the end of 2028 based on the assumption of an average Brent crude oil price of $75 a barrel.

"The message on the 3Q call was similar to that of 2Q, reiterating the moderation in reinvestment rate and commitment to dividend growth through the Willow start-up and after," Margolin said.

Margolin ranks No. 189 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 69% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.6%. See ConocoPhillips Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2658. The biggest money-making energy trade looks over. What to buy instead

Is one of the greatest money-making trades of the last few years coming to an end?

No, I'm not talking about Nvidia or any of the data center- or AI-related power companies (although they have been crushed lately too… more on that on CNBC TV. Tune in!).

I'm talking about whether the epic boom in refinery-related stocks is unwinding. Over the last year, the jump in some of these companies has been nothing short of remarkable.

That jump is largely due to massive increases in margins, earnings, and cash flow. Those have occurred because of a big jump in what is called a crack spread. That is the difference between the price of oil and the finished price of refined products such as diesel and jet fuel. For most of this year, spreads have been soaring as disruptions and attacks on facilities and shipping in the Arabian Gulf, Hormuz, and deep inside Russia have played havoc with the market. In the last week or so, however, something big has changed: many of those spreads have started to tighten.

This seismic drop in spreads is causing a selloff in the aforementioned refinery companies.

If spreads continue to fall, these stocks may well keep selling off from recent highs. The upshot for investors may be that, as the shares come down, analysts see more upside. The median price target for Phillips 66 (PSX) now indicates a 20% gain from here, while smaller-cap Par Pacific (PARR) is double that.

Here are a few other key points to focus on heading into the Thanksgiving-shortened week.

I like to keep politics as much at arm's length as possible — we all know that energy and politics (sadly) often go hand in hand. Energy policies coming from Washington, D.C., can drive — or destroy — investment dollars. With all the big shifts in the White House over the last 10 years (four years of Trump, four years of Biden, and now 2 years into Trump #2), energy policies can blow through Washington faster than a summer squall hits a wind turbine. But because federal policies can mean so much for specific companies, sectors, and investments, it's important to follow the news flow.

That said, I'd like to take a moment to introduce you to the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, known as BAAJA (and pronounced like the state in Mexico). Like much legislation, BAAJA is an obscure but important bill working its way through the American Congress. BAAJA does a lot of things, but what Power Insider readers care about is its focus on energy permitting reform. In a nutshell, BAAJA would help speed up the building of new electricity infrastructure. Again, that's an overly simplistic view of a big bill, but it needs to be on your radar. The utility trade group Edison Electric Institute came out in favor of the legislation this month after the industry received some 11th-hour changes. See more about it in our recent interview with EEI President Drew Maloney here.

The bill passed the U.S. Senate in a vote late Thursday night. Now it moves to the House. Support for its final passage is also coming from the big LNG trade group, whose executive director said he "applauds the Senate for its bipartisan effort to pass meaningful, bipartisan, and durable permitting reforms aimed at delivering greater predictability, consistency, and certainty in the permitting, production, and export process for U.S. LNG. This legislation moves us closer to untangling red tape and eliminating overlapping agency reviews that can keep U.S. energy projects delayed for years."

The Natural Gas Supply Association also chimed in, saying it urges Congress to see the bill as "a resource-agnostic and balanced reform agenda for boosting American energy security. It keeps strong environmental reviews in place, but it draws a firm line against the endless procedural delays that tie critical energy projects in knots for a decade."

In my view, passing BAAJA is an important test of Congress's appetite for helping the energy and electrical power industry, lowering costs, and speeding time to market. The old adage was correct: time is money. Literally. If BAAJA moves on through the House, we could see an upside move in some utility stocks and any company related to energy infrastructure.

Speaking of politics, the news is getting lost in the massive Anthropic (ANT) IPO, but the Texas Commission on Environmental Quality (TCEQ) — whose importance I wrote about in the last edition — just decided to resume permitting for data centers. The Texas Tribune did a nice write-up on it, and you can read it here.

It's tough to say, but it's true. The two are inextricably linked by policies created at both the federal and state levels. As I wrote in the last edition, some federally elected officials may have less impact on state-level issues — like the data center buildout — but they certainly have power over national laws. While many of you are enthralled, disgusted, or confused by sordid tales of Nvidia and Jensen-gate, keep one eye open on what happens in Washington, D.C.

Have a great week, and have a wonderful Thanksgiving, everyone.

Perhaps the big investor story — which is, like most things lately, related to Jensen-gate and Nvidia—is the sizable selloff in many AI, neocloud, and data center power-related stocks.

Painful for current shareholders, but big drops can also bring big opportunities. Wall Street believes that's the case, at least right now. Look at some of the potential upside based on the stocks' prices versus their price targets.

We spoke with the CEO of Applied Digital (APLD) about all of this on CNBC last week, and you can watch it here. He highlighted how the market may be getting these stock stories wrong because, at least with his company, he has long-term contracts with solid-credit companies. In that way, Applied Digital is more like a landlord than a "pure-play" AI company.

My take → While I'm not going to recommend a specific stock, I would add that it is critical for investors to understand the differences between these companies. While they may have some things in common, they are also different, with different business models, customers, leases, and sources of revenue.

Speaking of recommendations… here are two ideas for you:

A recent decline presents a nice opportunity in SOLV Energy (MWH). UBS analyst Jon Windham upgrades SOLV to buy from neutral, believing the market is "underappreciating several key tailwinds for SOLV given uncertainty around data center demand and higher interest rates." UBS highlights how the stock has fallen lately despite making two deals and beating and raising recent guidance. UBS says now is an "attractive entry point" for SOLV. (Side note: MWH is a great stock ticker.)

While markets overall fall, BMO remains bullish on Bloom Energy (BE). After speaking with the company, the analyst is upgrading Bloom to outperform, saying he remains "increasingly confident of BE's commercial traction" and that he still has conviction even with the recent drop in shares. While analyst Ameet Thakkar did cut his target to $230 from $260, that's still 50% upside from the current price. Bloom shares have tumbled 45% since October 1.

I was recently asked for my opinion on the price of oil. Here's my take. The market clearly wants to push oil contract prices lower and has for a few months. But, as I've said and reported for a while, don't confuse the futures (paper) price with the actual price. Yes, shipping rates and insurance rates have come down from recent highs — and there is a good amount of oil now flowing through Hormuz — but the cost of a physical barrel of crude is still well above the futures contract. If we get real, longer-lasting peace around Iran, oil prices will fall. There is plenty of oil in the world. That said, if bombs and missiles start firing again, oil prices will firm. I know that's a non-answer answer, but the oil market is truly headline-driven right now. My late good friend Boone Pickens (later in life, he didn't like being called by the "T" in his first name) used to avoid giving a set price target and instead give a directional quote, so I'll do the same: the odds of oil being lower in two months than it is today are 75%.

Last week, I had a one-on-one with Vimal Kapur, chair and CEO of Honeywell International (HON), at an RBC conference in New York City. While the conversation wasn't filmed, I can tell you that Kapur offered investors reassurance that his order book is doing very, very well. This is important because Honeywell is huge in the things that make other things work. Its sensors go into a ton of energy facilities, from refineries to LNG facilities. Honeywell is a leading indicator of spending and buildouts, so if it keeps getting orders, it's a good sign for macro energy.

Bottom line: Watch Honeywell stock as a potential "tell."

This week's Inside Line interview takes a different approach. We hit our friend Craig Johnson up for actionable insights into energy and stocks. Craig is managing director and chief market technician at Piper Sandler. He recently appeared on Power Lunch to talk about markets and stocks. He mentioned being bullish on energy, so we wanted to dig in just a little bit more.

This week, we had the pleasure of chatting with Balaji Krishnamurthy, president of Chevron Australia. We spoke at length about natural gas, the impact of disruptions, and long-term demand from Chevron customers in the Asia-Pacific region.

This is a bit of a local RBI for the New York City area, but one I felt was important to highlight. New York's electric grid operator just came out with a massive warning for everyone in our region: in just a couple of years, there may not be enough electricity supply to power our needs. A new report shows a deficit in seven years, with a nearly 2 GW power hole in a decade. That's a number of big power plants' worth of energy. Get your solar panels on and buy that backup generator! I'll call this one random, but scary.

Catch up with more on energy including interviews and video content from CNBC and Power Insider.

#2656. Besiktas vs Hapoel Beer-Sheva: Europa League match suspended after police clashes over Palestine banner

The Europa League game between Besiktas and Israel's Hapoel Beer-Sheva was suspended for around 30 minutes on Thursday when police clashed with fans of the Turkish club after they displayed a banner in support of Palestine.

The game, being played on neutral ground in Batumi, Georgia, was halted in the 26th minute but restarted after the interruption.

Turkey's Haberturk broadcaster said Georgian police used pepper spray against the Besiktas fans, who also unfurled a Palestinian flag, prompting referee referee Morten Krogh to stop the game with the score 0-0.

In a statement, Besiktas accused the Georgian security forces of using "disproportionate" force against supporters before and during the game.

Georgian police entered the stands and ⁠used pepper spray on Besiktas fans, Turkish outlet NTV reported.

"We strongly condemn the security forces for their use of disproportionate force against our fans," the club said. "We also extend our best wishes for a speedy recovery to all our fans affected by these incidents."

Besiktas accused the Georgian security forces of using 'disproportionate' force against supporters (AP)

The report added that Turkish fans chanted "Down with Israel" and that Besiktas players and director of football Onder Ozen spoke to supporters before play restarted to calm the situation.

The Turkish Football Federation condemned the treatment of Besiktas supporters on X, saying they were subjected to unacceptable behaviour and discrimination. It said it would press for an investigation.

The TFF's statement read: "We strongly condemn the unacceptable attitudes, conduct and interference directed at Beşiktaş supporters, both inside and outside the stadium, before and during our club's UEFA Europa League match against Hapoel Beer-Sheva in Georgia.

"No practice that disregards the universal values of sport, human dignity, mutual respect and the principles of fair play, or that involves discrimination, exclusion or security failures, can be tolerated.

"Nor can there be any question of remaining silent in the face of such conduct, which endangers our supporters' safety and targets their fundamental rights and freedoms.

"As the Turkish Football Federation, we declare unequivocally that we will follow this matter to its conclusion to ensure that the events are fully investigated, those responsible are identified and the necessary sanctions are imposed without delay.

Georgian police used pepper spray against the Besiktas fans, according to Turkey's Haberturk broadcaster (AP)

"There must be no tolerance for covering up these events, allowing those responsible to evade accountability or permitting similar violations to recur.

"We emphasize that we stand firmly with our club, Beşiktaş, and our supporters, who faced a series of problems, particularly serious security failures, and extend our best wishes for a speedy recovery to all our supporters subjected to interference and mistreatment.

"Let no one forget that we will not remain silent in the face of disrespect, discrimination and security violations directed at representatives and supporters of Turkish football.

"We will act decisively on national and international platforms to ensure that all necessary steps are taken, particularly with UEFA, and that those responsible are held accountable."
