#2655. How Moe Wagner and Danny Wolf have become offensive bright spots for the Nets

It's pretty doom and gloom in NetsWorld right now, and that's justified. Sitting at 3-13 after Monday night's blowout against the Minnesota Timberwolves, the team has now suffered nine double-digit losses this season.

When a young, rebuilding team isn't winning, it's one thing, but getting completely outmatched consistently is another. That's pretty much what's been the case for Brooklyn, as nine of their 13 losses have come by 10+ points.

Interestingly, one of the biggest questions entering the season surrounded Brooklyn's frontcourt. It wasn't clear how the rotation would take shape.

Day'Ron Sharpe entered the season as the team's starting center following Nic Claxton's departure, while Moe Wagner arrived in free agency. Wagner's health was in question after he played just 66 games over the previous two seasons, including time missed due to a torn ACL. Danny Wolf was another question mark after an up-and-down rookie campaign.

So far, the frontcourt has produced more offense than expected. Brooklyn's rim protection remains a work in progress, and its defense has struggled at every level. But offensively, two of the team's biggest surprises have been Wagner and Wolf.

Offensively, two of Brooklyn's biggest surprises have been Wagner and Wolf.

Wagner has been known for his offensive game since entering the league—most notably his success from beyond the arc and his consistency.

Although injuries have hindered him significantly over the past two years, Wagner is starting to pick up where he left off before the ACL injury forced him out of action for 98 games.

Through 16 games, Wagner owns a 70.7% effective field-goal percentage, ranking in the 98th percentile among bigs, per Cleaning the Glass. A large part of that production comes from his remarkable 62.5% shooting from three-point range. That percentage almost certainly won't hold over an 82-game season, but it's an encouraging start to his Brooklyn tenure.

What's even more encouraging, though, is the success beyond just the 3-point line. For a big like Wagner, who isn't necessarily touted for rim protection or stout defense, being a multi-faceted offensive contributor is crucial.

Overall, he's returning to the offense that worked at the peak of his career in Orlando. Last season with the Magic, just 44% of his overall attempts came at the rim, while 25% came from the midrange. This year, those numbers have shifted to 60% and 19%, respectively. That's much more in line with what we saw from Wagner during his two most efficient seasons (2023-24, 2024-25), with his effective FG percentage finishing north of 60% in both of those campaigns.

Wagner has also improved drastically at creating his own shot. Last season, 88% of his made baskets at the rim were assisted. Meanwhile, that has dropped to 61%, despite a 15-percentage-point increase in his shot usage around the basket.

Obviously, there's only so much that Wagner could be expected to do. Signed to a two-year, $18.4 million contract this offseason, he was widely viewed as the Nets' backup big behind Sharpe. So far, that has held. Sharpe has started all 17 of Brooklyn's games, while averaging 24 minutes per game. Meanwhile, off the bench, Wagner has averaged 17.1 minutes a game.

Entering the season, one of the biggest concerns with the Nets' roster was where points would come from off the bench. So far, their bench scoring hasn't been too shabby, ranking 13th in bench points per game (38.7). More impressively, though, their bench ranks 11th in 3-point percentage (35.7%). Wagner is clearly a big reason behind that.

Having a big who can stretch the floor while also generating offense around the basket is a luxury.

The bigger question mark entering the year is what the Nets really had in Wolf. Just like the four Flatbush 5 members behind Egor Dëmin, whose start to the year had been disappointing, the Michigan product had a lot to prove heading into the season.

So far, though, that development has been encouraging. Just like Wagner, the 27th overall pick in last June's draft has emerged as a threat from beyond the arc. After shooting just 32% from deep as a rookie, he is converting at 46.7% this season, ranking in the 95th percentile among bigs.

Worth noting, though, he only played four minutes in Wednesday's win over the Milwaukee Bucks after exiting with left knee soreness.

There has also been an uptick in volume: 54% of his overall shot attempts have come from the 3-point range, up from 44% a season ago. His mark this year ranks in the 88th percentile among bigs. Beyond that, 35% of his overall attempts have come from non-corner 3s, which ranks in the 91st percentile.

Wolf's output has also had a large impact on Brooklyn as a whole. Last season, the Nets scored 5.1 fewer points per 100 possessions with him on the floor than with him on the bench. This season, they're scoring 5.4 more points per 100 possessions with him out there, ranking in the 83rd percentile among all players.

Additionally, the Nets' effective field-goal percentage improves by 3.2 percentage points with him on the floor, while the offensive rebounding percentage also rises by 8.3 percentage points. The latter ranks in the 96th percentile among players who have logged a minute this season. For context, he has only played 244 minutes this season, but those numbers are still fascinating.

Even more interesting is how he's been used. After opening the season outside of Jordi Fernández's rotation, Wolf has worked his way into the lineup, but in an odd spot: as the Nets' starting shooting guard. How many people had that on their bingo card entering the season?

In fact, six of his seven starts this season have come at the 2.

Against the Magic on Friday night, Fernández inserted Wolf into the starting five for Dëmin, and Wolf responded with a 20-point outing. In his second straight start, against the Minnesota Timberwolves on Monday, he finished with 11 points, four assists, and five rebounds on 4-of-7 shooting, including 3-of-5 from deep.

As our own Anthony Puccio noted earlier this month, Wolf played every second of his initial breakout performance against the Charlotte Hornets alongside either Sharpe or Wagner. That trend has held mostly true since then, letting him work away from the basket and avoid many traditional big responsibilities.

Is he truly a 5 in the NBA? There need to be more reps to make the case for or against it, but he doesn't ideally fit as a shooting guard. At 6'11", it's just not a natural fit, and it is easy for him to get exposed guarding shifty playmaking guards at the top of the key. Even though he was a ball handler at Michigan, being a guard in college versus the NBA are two completely different responsibilities. Nonetheless, the versatility is highly encouraging.

Regardless of where Wolf ultimately settles in the long term, he and Wagner have given the Nets more offensively than anyone could have asked for before the season.

#2652. Nvidia surge: What investors should do with the stock and the AI trade now

Nvidia shares gapped higher at the open by more than 30%, on pace for its biggest one-day gain in 23 years on Monday, following the news just before the bell that former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang is going to be reinstated.

Investors and traders said that while this reflex rally may have more to run, cautiousness is warranted on Nvidia and the AI trade because of the overhang of investigations into the alleged Chinese espionage involving the company and others, as well as the possibility for data center moratoriums as Democrats take control of Congress and key governor posts in January.

"Am I as bullish on Nvidia now as I was on Friday? Of course not, you can't be," said Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, which counted Nvidia as its largest position as he bought on the dip over the last few weeks. "I'm trimming back."

"All the same issues that were here before Jensen resigned are still here," he added.

The day before Huang resigned on Nov. 12 as CEO, Nvidia closed at $173.73. It's intraday high on Monday was $171.50, up from its recent low around $125 as it was selling off in the wake controversies. The VanEck Semiconductor ETF closed at $506.96 the day before his resignation. It reached a high of $469.35 before losing steam as trading wore on Monday.

Mike Khouw, YieldMax strategist at Tidal Financial Group, told CNBC on Monday that there were "sighs of relief," "applause" and even "cheering" among investors at the news of Huang's potential reinstatement. But he also said there are still considerable risks facing the AI trade.

Jensen Huang was not the only problem at Nvidia, he said, and there are still many vulnerabilities in the overall AI trade, which should give investors pause. Credit-sensitive companies are still at risk of faltering, Khouw added.

The national security implications of the scandal that has rocked Nvidia and the global AI trade more broadly aren't going away any time soon even as the Trump administration assured the Nvidia board there were none. Investors on Wall Street are expressing strategic concerns about ways to play the tech space just as lawmakers in Washington are demanding answers.

Democrats are demanding answers on what exactly happened with Nvidia and the Chinese government.

"The reckless decision to bring Jensen Huang back as CEO does not change the fact that Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and the company's board still owe Congress answers about the company's compliance with U.S. export controls," Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-Mass.) said in a Monday statement. "The Trump Administration also needs to explain to Congress what 'input' was given and by which senior officials in the United States government to support his reinstatement."

House Ways and Means ranking member Richard Neal (D-Mass.) stressed the importance of Congressional oversight in determining the best course of action to move forward. The outcome of any Congressional investigations will likely be crucial for Nvidia's long-term viability as an investment opportunity, especially since the company does so much international business.

"This is an export control issue," Neal told CNBC on Monday. "Congress is not subservient to this president or any other president as it relates to trade and tariff policies. This is a trade related issue."

Many of the same Democrats will call for a slowdown in the data center buildout, a strong issue for the public partly responsible for their Blue Wave earlier this month.

"You're going to have to work your way through what these data center moratoriums all mean," said Niles. "Now I think the broader market keeps going but I am not as convinced on the move in the data-center centric names from here."

To be sure, many Wall Street analysts were rushing to upgrade Nvidia's stock on Monday after Huang's reinstatement. Most restored price targets well above $200 with one as high as $270. But many traders think the rebound may fail before getting back to those levels.

Famed short seller Michael Burry, who made a boatload betting against Nvidia with put options as it cratered, told his Substack audience that he thinks the rebound here could be "outsized," especially with low volume in the market overall because of the Thanksgiving holiday this week.

But he too sees the rally eventually losing steam.

"Jensen being back without much more information might even restore NVDA to the high 100s," Burry wrote. "I won't short it on this information I have today until it gets closer to or maybe hurdles $200."

#2651. The Lions, despite their resurgence, are 1-8 in their last nine Thanksgiving games

The Lions commenced their ongoing resurgence in 2022. That hasn’t translated to sustained success on Thanksgiving.

They’re 1-8 over the last nine Thanksgiving Day games.

The next day, the Bears fired coach Matt Eberflus. It paved the way for the eventual hiring of Lions offensive coordinator Ben Johnson, who returns to Ford Field today for the second time.

Which makes the 1-8 trend even more surprising.

Enjoy the day. We’ll be here throughout the games, posting all relevant updates and other NFL news. It’s a privilege, not a burden, to spend time on Thanksgiving doing what we always do. And we’re thankful for the fact that you choose to consume it.

#2650. Blake Corum supplants Kyren Williams as the Rams' top tailback

It’s been slowly building throughout the season. On Wednesday night, it became abundantly clear.

Blake Corum has become the Rams’ top tailback.

On his 26th birthday, Corum had a career night. Thirty-two carries, 184 yards, and a touchdown. Throw in 21 receiving yards, and he had 205 yards from scrimmage.

“Happy birthday, man,” coach Sean McVay told reporters after the game. “He was on the set of Netflix so we didn’t even get to enjoy it with him. So, I know that kind of pissed me off, honestly.”

Corum’s gains have come at the expense of Kyren Williams. Despite a report that he’s been dealing with turf toe for a month, this week was Williams’s first appearance all year on the injury report.

And while the fact that Corum got the start last night and 58 snaps while Williams was limited to 38 may have had something to do with the toe, as Mike Tomlin says, “One man’s misfortune is another man’s opportunity.”

Corum seized the opportunity on Wednesday night, as part of a very un-Rams-like offensive output that included 241 rushing yards and 132 passing yards.

For the season, Williams has 662 rushing yards and an average of 4.4 yards per carry. Corum has 731 yards rushing with a 5.4-yard average.

Lurking in the background (but soon moving to the foreground) is the business side of the situation. Corum is in the third year of a slotted, third-round rookie deal. He’s making $1.582 million in 2026, and he’s eligible for a second contract as soon as the regular season ends.

Williams is making $11 million per year, on a deal that runs through 2028.

They’ll need to reward Corum at some point, which could force them to make a decision about Williams.

That’s clearly coming. Last night’s breakout showing in a standalone game shows that it’s coming quickly.

#2649. Kalshi daily volume of ‘perps’ trading begins to top its prediction markets

Kalshi is known for its prediction markets across sports, politics and culture.

But now, for the first time, perpetual futures, Kalshi's second financial product, has surpassed the notional trading volume of its event contracts on consecutive days.

On Thursday, perpetual futures — commonly known as "perps" — on Kalshi saw $3.4 billion in notional volume as artificial intelligence laboratory Anthropic stock debuted on public markets while the rest of the market sold off. That exceeded about $3 billion in notional volume for Kalshi's prediction markets on the same day.

Perps are futures-style contracts where there is no expiration and traders do not need to own the underlying asset. Contracts instead continuously track the price of an asset, with funding payments to keep the contract price aligned with that of the market. Funding payments are made periodically between holders of short or long positions.

Perps trading surpassed event contracts one day in September, but on Friday perps topped prediction markets volume for a second day. Now Kalshi expects that performance to continue as it introduces perps tied to more individual stocks. On Monday, for example, Kalshi launched contracts linked to Anthropic.

Kalshi was the first to debut perps in the U.S. after winning federal regulatory approval in May to list perps tied to cryptocurrencies.

Perps trading volume includes any leverage that speculators acquire along with their trade, while event contracts are fully collateralized — for now.

Since the original launch in May, Kalshi has received approval to introduce perps tied to metals, crude oil, stock indexes and individual stocks.

Offshore perps — those available on exchanges not regulated in the U.S. — generated more than $90 trillion in notional trading volume in 2025, so it's not surprising that the product has grown so rapidly on Kalshi, according to Udesh Jha, chief risk officer at Kalshi Klear, the company's clearing house.

"We're just in very early innings. We're launching products, we're growing the participant base."

Perps were Kalshi's first product to mark its transition from a prediction market-focused platform to a full-blown financial exchange where various assets are traded. Last week, reports said that Kalshi is planning to offer trading on equities on its platform in the coming months.

At the same time, the company's perps product has come under fire. Some incumbent exchanges where futures are traded — which saw their stock prices decline following the approval of U.S. perps — believe the product was improperly approved. CME Group is suing the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, the federal derivatives regulator, for approving perps.

Others have simply expressed concerns about the efficiency of the markets. CNBC previously reported that trading volume on Kalshi's most popular perps markets — tied to bitcoin and ether cryptocurrencies — was dramatically greater than open interest, or cash actively in the markets. It's a phenomenon that isn't common with U.S.-regulated traditional futures, nor other offshore perps markets.

Kalshi said at the time it has "zero concerns" about the distinction, and that it appears because less leverage is offered compared to offshore exchanges and there are fewer incentives for traders to park capital on its perps markets.

While Kalshi started with cryptocurrencies, Owen Lau, an analyst at Clear Street, said perps tied to individual stocks likely represent the most promising opportunity for growth in the U.S.

"That's how I foresee the way perps will penetrate further into traditional asset classes," Lau said. "Perps on single stocks and [key performance indicators on individual companies] could change how investors hedge their portfolio or even make a directional bet."

Kalshi's growth has been powered by the fact that right now it's the only exchange that can actively list perps in the U.S., though crypto exchange Coinbase received approval in May to route U.S. users to offshore platforms for trading. That monopoly status, however, will probably prove short lived.

Coinbase has filed to list single-stock perps in the U.S., and President Donald Trump in August said the CFTC is working to bring Hyperliquid — a dominant player in offshore perps activity — to domestic traders.

But Jha at Kalshi isn't worried about the competition.

"What will carry us is ... making sure that we have the right products for the marketplace, making sure we have the right surveillance of the products that we are trading."

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2648. Eagles vs. Cowboys: 5 offensive keys for Philadelphia at Dallas

The Philadelphia Eagles have an opportunity to build offensive momentum Thursday against a Dallas Cowboys defense that allows 151.5 rushing yards per game. Joe Milton will make his third consecutive start, and offensive coordinator Sean Mannion will face a Dallas defense that changed play-callers ahead of Week 11.

Prescott's recent touchdown production makes sustaining drives and maximizing scoring opportunities particularly important for Philadelphia.

Here are five offensive keys for the Eagles.

Philadelphia should test Dallas' defensive front early with Saquon Barkley, allowing Jordan Mailata and the offensive line to establish a physical presence.

The Cowboys' struggles defending the run represent Philadelphia's most obvious opportunity to control possession and reduce the burden on Milton.

Smith should be involved early rather than becoming an option exclusively in obvious passing situations.

Quick completions to Philadelphia's top wide receiver can generate manageable second and third downs while giving Milton opportunities to develop a rhythm before attacking deeper areas of the field.

Williams can disrupt a running play before Barkley reaches the line of scrimmage or prevent Milton from stepping into a throw.

Drew Kendall and Philadelphia's guards must communicate effectively when Dallas alters its defensive front. Avoiding interior penetration will be essential to keeping the offense on schedule.

Dallas Goedert and Zach Ertz give Philadelphia experienced options in the middle of the field, particularly against a defense looking to disrupt Milton's timing.

Multiple-tight-end personnel can help Philadelphia disguise its intentions, create favorable rushing opportunities and provide additional protection when needed.

Philadelphia ranks fifth in the NFL in red-zone touchdown percentage, and that efficiency must travel to Dallas.

Considering Prescott has thrown nine touchdown passes over his past two games, settling for field goals after lengthy possessions could leave Philadelphia chasing points in the second half.

Detroit Lions head coach Dan Campbell looks on during the second quarter of a game against the Chicago Bears in Detroit, Thursday, Nov. 26, 2026. Getty Images

Chicago Bears' Darnell Wright swats the ball out of the end zone during an NFL football game against the Detroit Lions in Detroit, Thursday, Nov. 26, 2026. AP Photo/Paul Sancya

#2646. Art Rooney II expresses support for Mike McCarthy, calls Aaron Rodgers not a "good teammate"

After Wednesday’s unprecedented chaos, it had to happen. And it did.

Steelers owner Art Rooney II has addressed the events that resulted in the benching and then the firing of quarterback Aaron Rodgers.

“A change had to be made, and I certainly agree with the change and the way it was handled,” Rooney told Gerry Dulac of the Pittsburgh Post-Gazette in a Thursday interview.

The change was sparked by the notion that Rodgers gave coach Mike McCarthy an ultimatum: “Either let me call all the plays or just release me.” Instead, McCarthy benched him.

It went to the next level when, as many expected, Rodgers scurried to the McAfee show to take shots at McCarthy for missing meetings, showing up late to meetings, and falling asleep in meetings — among other things, including the ultra-embarrassing “1-2-3 Packers!” moment from halftime of Sunday’s loss to the Eagles.

“There’s maybe a time and place after the season if he wanted to kind of unload,” Rooney said. “He talks about wanting to be a good teammate, but you don’t do that to your teammates a couple days before the game, that’s for sure. . . .

“In general, I think, historically, what’s in the locker room stays in the locker room. That’s the way it’s always been. That’s the way it should be as far as I’m concerned.”

Rooney also confirmed that the team supports McCarthy, despite Rodgers’s rant and reports confirming the facts underpinning the petty delivery of them.

“I just want to say that I have 100 [percent] confidence in Mike McCarthy and I expect he’ll be our coach this season, next season and beyond,” Rooney told Dulac. “The big thing, what happened this week, is insubordination can’t be tolerated in any organization. When you start getting ultimatums, you can’t live with that.”

Rooney also was asked whether Rodgers raised with the owner supposed concerns about McCarthy’s health.

“Nothing,” Rooney said. “Mike’s as healthy as the rest of us. Health was not an issue. I’m not sure where that part comes [from].”

Rooney also addressed something that has been largely overlooked during the fast-moving drama: Rodgers’s subpar performance.

“The sad thing is Mike took some of the bullets for Aaron in terms of some of the plays that were called, particularly in the red-zone troubles that we had,” Rooney said. “Mike took some of the bullets for things that were going wrong down there. He tried to protect him, and it just wasn’t working.”

They should have known it wouldn’t work between Rodgers and McCarthy. It’s still unclear why either one of them thought it would. McCarthy shouldn’t have wanted a reunion with Rodgers. And Rodgers shouldn’t have wanted a second act with McCarthy.

Then again, it’s not as if Rodgers had many choices. In 2025, he had few takers. In 2026, he had fewer. If he was going to ensure that his final NFL throw wasn’t a pick-six by playing another year, Pittsburgh wasn’t just the best place.

#2643. AI debt markets shudder as Nvidia's top 'banker' returns

AI credit markets are shuddering again with Jensen Huang back as CEO of Nvidia.

Fears about the massive debt being taken on to fund the artificial intelligence buildout climbed Tuesday as Wall Street weighed what Huang's return to Nvidia means for its bankrolling of the AI boom.

Huang is considered one of the top financiers, or bankers, in the AI industry, spearheading deals with neoclouds like CoreWeave and other firms to buy chips, backed in some cases by Nvidia guarantees. His return to Nvidia has revived prospects for the company to continue "circular financing" of AI chips and data centers — raising concerns among credit investors.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child of AI debt concerns, saw its stock sink 13% and the yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 top 17%. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

At the same time, the spread on the neocloud company's five-year credit default swaps jumped 8.5% to over 1,200 basis points. Credit default swaps are insurance against nonpayment and are considered a barometer of perceived risks. Rising CDS spreads mean investors view the debt as riskier.

Huang was reinstated as CEO on Monday after stepping down on Nov. 12 amid allegations of multiple affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy. His reinstatement on Monday was cheered by investors, who pushed stocks higher, yields lower and spreads on CDS tighter. However, stocks retreated, bond yields rose and CDS spreads widened on Tuesday.

Ben Emons, founder and chief investment officer at FedWatch Advisors, called it a waning of the "Jensen effect."

The moves suggest "markets see government intervention as 'moral suasion' that carries credit and interest-rate risks," he said.

President Donald Trump told CNBC on Monday he "convinced" the board to bring Huang back.

CoreWeave wasn't the only name seeing heightened concerns in CDS spreads. Oracle's five-year CDS spread jumped 7% to 355.68. Even highly rated hyperscalers Meta Platforms and Amazon.com saw spreads widen by more than 2%. Nvidia's CDS added 3.4%.

Larry Holzenthaler, senior portfolio manager for fixed income at Catalyst Funds, believes the drama that unfolded with Huang's return to Nvidia won't necessarily sit well with credit investors.

"A lot of old school credit people are going to look at what kind of materialized there, the administration's involvement in putting him back in, and I think a lot of credit participants are going to step back and tiptoe away from that a bit," he said. "Credit market investors typically don't like that type of drama or uncertainty."

While Nvidia has commitments across the AI ecosystem, it is the neoclouds that have been under the most scrutiny. Many of the companies, which rent out AI computing power and cloud capacity, are highly leveraged and have relied on Nvidia financing to reduce their exposure to the bond market.

With so much riding on Nvidia's funding, Huang's next move is being closely watched.

During his brief absence, chief financial officer Colette Kress said during the company's earnings call that Nvidia would be "rigorously evaluating future investments" and will remain disciplined in how it commits capital.

That suggested Nvidia was going to be more judicious with financing, says Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"That concern should be put to rest with Jensen [Huang] back," he said.

Investor Steve Eisman, of "Big Short" fame and host of "The Real Eisman Playbook" podcast, believes Huang feels emboldened now that he's back in charge.

"[H]e'll double down on the circular financing," Eisman predicted on CNBC's "Squawk Box" Tuesday.

After Huang's departure, Eisman believed that the ecosystem the CEO created by pouring money into AI companies would likely unwind and leave some of the companies in "big trouble."

"Now that he's back, they're not," he said.

Others are not so certain the level of funding will continue.

"Given scrutiny of disclosure around financing and guarantees … we do not expect Mr. Huang's return to bring a quick reversion to broader customer financing," Stifel analyst Ruben Roy said in a note Monday.

Nvidia said on its earnings call that it has $118 billion in guarantees, as of Oct. 25, to back AI projects.

Concerns about the level of debt to fund the AI infrastructure buildout had already taken hold before Huang's departure and reinstatement.

"These issues have not disappeared just because the White House pressured the weak board to reinstate Jensen Huang," said economist David Rosenberg, founder and president of Rosenberg Research.

John Lloyd, global head of multi-sector and corporate credit at Janus Henderson Investors, believes having Huang back at the helm "reaffirms his existing go-to-market strategy." However, the credit market is still taking a cautious look at the neoclouds as it waits for more clarity on long-term supply/demand dynamics.

Still, Lloyd is cautiously positive on CoreWeave, which he owns in portfolios he manages, and noted all the data point to a "pretty healthy environment right now." That includes strong residual values on graphics processing units and spot compute rates, he noted.

"The company is doing everything in its power to execute," he said. "They don't need the high yield market at this time."

In the meantime, investigations into Nvidia and Huang are ramping up. Huang is expected to appear before three House committees next week. Nvidia is conducting an independent investigation into his activities, and California Attorney General Rob Bonta has launched a probe. At the federal level, President Trump said Monday that U.S. Attorney General Todd Blanche "has confirmed that the U.S. Government does not possess evidence that he has engaged in any unlawful conduct."

"There's other shoes to drop," Rosenberg said. "I don't think that the book has been closed on this file."

#2640. Nvidia's billion-dollar board bet: Who's protected as lawsuits mount?

Jensen Huang is back as CEO of Nvidia, after President Donald Trump urged the company's board to rehire Huang. But investigations into the company, its executive leadership and its board continue as outside criticism mounts.

"Clearly they caved to external pressure," said Betsy Atkins, an author and longtime public-company director. "The board failed," she said, when it initially allowed Huang's removal. "The board's role is to think of all stakeholders."

California Attorney General Rob Bonta is investigating whether Nvidia misrepresented information to investors. "There is classic corporate securities law on the books in California that prevents investors and the public from being misled by companies," Bonta said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" on Tuesday.

Directors are already defendants in a shareholder lawsuit accusing them of failing to oversee Nvidia. A separate securities class action targets the company and Huang, its co-founder.

Now the board’s insurance choices are part of the story. A risk advisor with direct knowledge of Nvidia’s coverage says the company built an approximately $1 billion D&O tower, insurance jargon for directors and officers insurance policies from different companies that combine to cover one massive risk.

Nvidia "loves insurance," the advisor said. “They buy insurance as if they have no balance sheet.” A billion-dollar tower would essentially mean Nvidia "bought the market," purchasing all the coverage it could get, according to an industry expert with 25 years working in financial insurance. The expert asked not to be named out of concern for past or potential future clients.

But Nvidia directed all that coverage to Side A only, according to CNBC's source. Side A protects directors and officers personally when the company cannot indemnify them. Side B covers the company for legal defense costs or settlements on behalf of directors and officers, and Side C protects the company when it's named as a defendant in a suit such as a securities claim.

The risk advisor says Nvidia believed it had "the war chest" to protect itself against a securities class action suit.

That choice is raising questions about whether the board protected itself at the company’s expense. CNBC asked Nvidia for comment but received no response.

An insurance CEO characterized a billion-dollar policy as a lot of coverage, but said a Side A-only allocation was a surprising move because it results in imbalanced coverage, protecting only directors.

That structure is not automatically a governance failure, according to a D&O insurance expert with long experience in coverage for banks, drugmakers and other businesses that often face litigation. Side A-only programs are common among companies with the capital to manage the corporate risk internally.

"For a company with Nvidia’s resources, retaining the company-side risk can make financial sense," the advisor said. It's Side A D&O that's "imperative to corporate governance."

"It is a capital-allocation decision," the expert told me. The company decides that buying insurance for its own balance sheet is not worth the premium. In other words, Side B and Side C are considered too expensive for the coverage the company gets.

The question for shareholders is whether the board’s decision to hold that risk was a sound use of the company’s resources and whether the company can absorb the costs if the claims grow.

A shareholder derivative complaint filed Nov. 11 names Huang, directors and other officers. It alleges that they failed to oversee risks involving Huang’s relationship with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang and made misleading statements. It also alleges that some defendants sold roughly $2 billion in Nvidia stock while holding material nonpublic information. Those remain allegations, not findings.

A separate securities class action, filed Nov. 9, names Nvidia and Huang. It seeks damages for investors who bought Nvidia shares between Oct. 9, 2025, and Nov. 8, 2026, alleging the company and Huang misled investors.

The distinction matters: The derivative suit is brought on Nvidia’s behalf, while the class action seeks recovery for investors.

"Insurance is already at work," says Michel Léonard, chief economist at the Insurance Information Institute, describing standard policy mechanics, not Nvidia’s specific policy. "Once the suit is filed, D&O starts paying defense costs, long before anyone proves anything."

A claim still has to meet the policy’s terms, and defense costs can reduce the remaining limits. Nor does a drop in Nvidia’s market value by itself establish an insurable loss. Plaintiffs would need to prove legally recoverable damages; the policy would respond only to covered losses.

The board has already taken steps it says are aimed at accountability. Nvidia announced an independent investigation on Nov. 8, and independent directors retained outside counsel two days later. Huang resigned as CEO and a director on Nov. 11; the board appointed CFO Colette Kress interim CEO. Nvidia said it had no reason to believe national security or company secrets had been compromised by Huang's extramarital relationships.

Atkins, who joined the board of Wynn Resorts in 2018 after founder Steve Wynn resigned, argues that allegations are not conclusions and that boards should also weigh governance concerns against the value a founder brings to a company’s innovation. Nvidia's investigation is continuing even as Huang resumes leadership.

“Will companies’ hyper-accelerated innovation speed be the same without them? I don’t think so,” said Atkins, author of "Don't Waste a Board Seat: A Decision-Maker's Guide to Choosing Directors Who Create Value."

Nvidia’s 2026 proxy also says the company has indemnity agreements with its directors and officers — separate from its insurance. Those agreements provide for the company to cover certain costs and liabilities when the law permits.

#2637. Semiconductor chips are the new oil. Here's why

The artificial intelligence boom has pushed semiconductors past oil and gas in global export value for the first time, according to a Deutsche Bank analysis of International Trade Centre data.

Yet the milestone arrives at an awkward moment, just as investors reassess the outlook for artificial intelligence spending and drive down the price of semiconductor stocks. Last week, before Monday's breathtaking reversal, semiconductor stocks suffered their worst weekly loss in a quarter century, with the PHLX Semiconductor Index (SOX) plunging nearly 11% last Friday alone, capping a more than 17% weekly decline.

Nvidia soared more than 31% Monday after the leading maker of artificial intelligence chips had fallen for a seventh straight week, plunging 46% during that stretch. Then it fell again, dropping almost 10% on Tuesday and Wednesday. Advanced Micro Devices tumbled 20% in two days last Thursday and Friday. Micron Technology sank 27% over the seven days ended last Friday.

"For decades we've talked about the petrodollar. Perhaps increasingly we need to think about the chip dollar," Jim Reid, global head of macro and thematic research at Deutsche Bank wrote in a report this month.

Semiconductor exports overtook oil and gas for the first time during the January-March quarter of 2026, reaching about $1.61 trillion, compared with $1.53 trillion for oil and gas, the report said, using four-quarter rolling totals.

The gap widened in the second quarter, when semiconductor exports totaled about $1.60 trillion and oil and gas exports reached about $1.47 trillion. Disruptions to oil supplies through the Strait of Hormuz after the Iran war helped widen the gap. Daily oil flows through the Strait collapsed nearly 77%, to 4.9 million barrels in the second quarter of 2026 from 21.6 million barrels in the fourth quarter of 2025, according to the U.S. Energy Information Administration.

Yet the demand for semiconductors has proven insatiable in the face of soaring data center construction, boosting global trade in the increasingly scarce AI component. Hundreds of billions of dollars of orders for graphics processing units and data centers aim at providing AI computing power, Rothschild & Co. Redburn wrote in a report last Thursday.

"Compute will be the new commodity fueling this transformation on a global scale," Rothschild analyst Cara Thomson said.

The shift toward chips has macroeconomic implications just as the explosion in oil trade did in the 1970s.

Oil-exporting countries historically recycled much of their surpluses into the purchase of U.S. Treasuries and other bonds, helping finance U.S. deficits and underpin demand for other government debt.

Reid at Deutsche Bank said that since semiconductor-surplus countries in Asia are more closely tied to U.S. tech companies, some chip-export earnings could flow back into U.S. technology stocks, startups and data centers, further helping to finance the AI investment cycle.

The changing dynamics in global trade could also tilt the mix of global financial flows, Reid said. "So welcome to the chip-dollar era: potentially more growth, more excitement, more volatility, and structurally higher yields" in corporate and government debt.

#2634. Wild safety in Lions-Bears game has NFL world reacting on Thanksgiving

Wild safety in Lions-Bears game has NFL world reacting on Thanksgiving appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

On Thursday afternoon, the Chicago Bears hit the road to take on the Detroit Lions in a key Thanksgiving Day showdown between two NFC powerhouses. The first place spot in the NFC North was on the line in this one, as the Lions and Bears both entered this matchup with sparkling 8-2 records.

A wild moment occurred in the second quarter of this one when the Bears fumbled the ball into the end zone and intentionally batted it back out into the field of play, which, by NFL rules, is a safety, giving two points to the Lions.

Mike Garafolo of the NFL Network weighed in on the play on X, noting that "The last illegal bat in the end zone I can remember was K.J. Wright's that wasn't called in a #Seahawks win against the #Lions in 2015. Detroit is on the other side this time."

Lions head coach Dan Campbell had the option of accepting the points from the safety and getting the resulting point, or declining that and taking the ball where it ended up after the Bears batted it, and wound up choosing the former.

Meanwhile, Bears fans were salty about a previous fumble that they thought should have gone their way.

"Bears fumble in first quarter, fall on it. Lions player takes it away super late. Lions ball. Lions fumble, fall on it, bears player takes it away almost immediately. Not bears ball. Bears should be up 7 at least. Genuinely inexcusable," complained one fan.

The Bears were already battling through adversity after tight end Colston Loveland was carted to the locker room due to an injury, and they wound up taking a 19-10 deficit into the locker room for halftime.

#2632. NFL legends, Hall of Famers put Aaron Rodgers on blast for Steelers rant

This time of year, everyone typically expresses what they are grateful for.

The now former Pittsburgh Steelers quarterback went on to the Pat McAfee Show on Wednesday, lighting up the organization and head coach Mike McCarthy. That prompted the Steelers to swiftly cut the future Hall of Fame quarterback.

Pittsburgh Steelers quarterback Aaron Rodgers attends a news conference after an NFL football game in Philadelphia, Sunday, Nov. 22, 2026. AP Photo/Chris Szagola

As the day progressed, reactions surfaced all over the internet. Many people came to the defense of Rodgers, pointing out that everything he stated during his interview was accurate and true. While others had a very different perspective, none louder than Hall of Fame tight end Shannon Sharpe.

Sharpe, while on Wednesday night's episode of the Night Cap podcast, absolutely eviscerated Rodgers' character.

"Every time I critiqued Aaron Rodgers, go back and look at CBS. I said he was arrogant. I said he was aloof, he was flippant, he was condescending."

"That is why he is a four-time MVP and only went to one Super Bowl, because he is not a leader of men. He's always been selfish. If you notice, he's never accepted responsibility."

Sharpe very nearly made it personal at that point, before backtracking.

"Everybody that he's fell out with from his own family. But I ain't gonna talk about his family. But I'm not gonna talk about his family because it seems like he's trying to reconnect and I commend him for that."

"But what'd they say at the Jets, they got his ass up out the building."

The three-time Super Bowl champion tight end then made eyebrow-raising comparisons to other legendary quarterbacks.

Pittsburgh Steelers quarterback Aaron Rodgers (8) checks with head coach Mike McCarthy during an NFL game against the Cincinnati Bengals, Sept. 27, 2026. USA TODAY Network via Reuters Connect

"I've been calling him a terrible leader, but y'all didn't want to listen… This is why he couldn't go to another team and do what Tom Brady did, which was lead a team to the Super Bowl. Go to a team and lead the team to the Super Bowl like Peyton Manning did.

"Does he have more God-given talent, one thousand, two hundred, and 12 percent. But does he have half the leader they ever was?"

Given Rodgers' history throughout the league, it is incredibly difficult to argue with anything Sharpe said.

But he is far from alone among legendary and elite players to put ARod on blast.

Russell Wilson, a potential future Hall of Fame quarterback, joined the Thanksgiving Day pregame show on CBS and ripped Rodgers as well.

"The reality is Aaron Rodgers wanted to call the plays, right? And that's the difference here…. The reality is the ultimatum was the ultimatum…. If not, I want my release, essentially. And so they made that decision…. It's about Aaron Rodgers's legacy.

Pittsburgh Steelers quarterback Aaron Rodgers (8) reacts after a game against the Philadelphia Eagles at Lincoln Financial Field on Sunday, Nov. 22, 2026. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

"If I'm his family members, if I'm somebody close to him, I'm his significant other, whatever it may be: 'Is this how you want to go out?' After all the success he's had, is this how you want to go out?… This is a disappointment."

Rodgers will turn 43 years old next week. He is without a team, and likely staring at an unceremonious end to an illustrious career.

But that was how this story seemed destined to end.

#2625. What we learned from the Spurs’ loss to the Trail Blazers

One of the few things I hope for as a spectator is that teams playing the late-night game bring it. It's too hard to stay awake as it is after a long day, so fans need excitement to keep them from falling asleep. Fortunately, it was not a problem for these teams, yet the only issue the San Antonio Spurs had late was their basketball IQ.

I had this game circled on the calendar because the physicality was off the charts the last time they played on Nov. 7. It appears it was not a distant memory for the Portland Trail Blazers, as the ESPN broadcast highlighted how they felt embarrassed by that game and wanted their lick back. Nonetheless, it was grown-up hoops and it wouldn't be a surprise if these squads didn't like each other.

Wednesday night being an Emirates Cup game added to the fun because the winner would keep their hopes alive of getting to the quarterfinal, still needing to get by Friday's game. Keep in mind that getting to the next round guarantees the players $60,499 and half of that for the two-way guys.

I wish that coach Mitch Johnson hadn't stuck to his usual substitution patterns in the third quarter, because when Wembanyama sat the Spurs were instantly outscored by eight. They got back on track when Victor checked in to clean up the mess, and the team likely could have separated itself had he had a longer shift. Games like this with absences to key guys magnify every decision. While the most part Mitch was good, I shook my head as he tried to buy time for the starters with Carter Bryant and Ja'Kobi Gillespie at the start of the fourth.

Johnson was probably thinking about preserving his main guys for later games, but this wasn't this a big one? Every now and then, 40 minutes against good teams is not so bad, and he probably would have broken 50 points for the third time this year.

Aside from their cup chances, the three late turnovers and failure to stop Portland's two 3-pointers ruined Wemby's 44-point masterpiece. Of course, the Trail Blazers deserve credit, but the Spurs also beat themselves down the stretch. Stephon Castle's eight-second violation was a killer.

Props to coach Micah Nori. He has a serious team, while he's basically on a prove-it deal, and they've been without Ja Morant. A lot of it has to do with them having one of the most balanced teams in the league, and it helps that Damian Lillard is still one sport'a clutch shooters. He has come back from his Achilles tear with his first step intact, and his outburst in the second half was vintage.

Without De'Aaron Fox and Dylan Harper, the Spurs were without two of the league's best penetrators (sorely needed against a high-level defense) which is a big reason why Wemby was the only one to score more than six points in the lane. The Spurs only had two quarters in which the offense was running smoothly (posting a 112.1 offensive rating) and that is one of their four-worst showings of the season. Portland was able to slow them down by making them pass less often.

Wembanyama stepped up. He poured in jumpers as Portland got physical with him. He kept the pressure on Donovan Clingan and fouled him out with four minutes left. In the past, Wembanyama wouldn't always assert himself in those situations and now it's like radar is beeping when his man gets five personals.

The Spurs mostly get the shot they are looking for when running the horns set, which has a screen to the left and right for the ball handler. The team has the best results on these when the screener is the scorer and the ball handler is the decoy.

Bryant should model his game after Keldon Johnson's. The latter barely scored but has mastered the art of hustle and had 12 rebounds, including five offensive, on Wednesday.

Portland is arguably the best paint-attacking team in the league, averaging 61.7 drives per game, and Deni Avdija has a lot to do with that.

Wembanyama had 12 points in the third quarter when he was subbed out and had only logged 21 total minutes to that point. As his rampage was paused, I was reminded of the old joke about how the only person who could stop Michael Jordan was Dean Smith.

#2624. Levski Sofia coach says Marseille fan clashes 'unacceptable'

Levski Sofia coach Julio Velazquez said violent clashes between fans of the Bulgarian club and Marseille supporters ahead of their Europa League game on Thursday were "unacceptable for football".

The lead-up to the match at the Stade Velodrome was marred by incidents in the stands between the two sets of supporters, including the throwing of projectiles and flares.

Several Marseille fans ran from the stand where they were situated about 10 minutes before kick-off, appearing to pursue a group of Bulgarian supporters.

They breached the stand designated for away fans, where around 500 Levski supporters were gathered.

Security personnel eventually managed to restore calm as the game kicked off on time.

"It's a very regrettable episode -- these are incidents which are unacceptable for football," said Velazquez.

"I support the fans who were caught up in this incident. I hope nothing serious has happened. I'm very saddened by this situation."

Ligue 1 club Marseille said it was "working to understand the precise circumstances of these incidents".

There were a few further flare-ups between the fans, notably during the half-time break.

"We are living through violent times in our society, so this is very, very regrettable," said Marseille coach Bruno Genesio.

The home team went on to win the game 1-0.

#2618. Matt LaFleur on Packers' run-game struggles: "We're trying"

The Packers weren't able to stop the run on Wednesday against the Rams. To apply a twist to a classic line from John McKay, they made up for it by not being able to run.

"Right now, we're a pass-heavy offense and we're trying to sneak in some runs there to at least keep the defense honest," coach Matt LaFleur told reporters after the 26-13 loss when asked about his team's offensive identity.

"We're trying, for sure," LaFleur said. "We're trying."

He also was asked whether they tried to run it enough.

"Obviously not, not when you lose the game like that," LaFleur said. "So I would say the answer to your question is no. I mean, you've got to convert third downs too. You've got to get those opportunities to get some first downs. And the second half, I think we had one drive that was a seven-play drive or whatever and everything else was like three-and-out or two-and-out and we turned the ball over."

On a night when the Rams had 241 yards rushing against only 132 yards passing, the Packers mustered only 55 rushing yards on 14 attempts.

Not having Josh Jacobs didn't help matters. Without Jacobs (who's recovering from a broken finger), the Packers' backfield consisted of Kaleb Johnson (eight carries for 13 yards), MarShawn Lloyd (one carry for 10 yards), and Chris Brooks (one carry for four yards).

Receiver Savion Williams ran the ball twice for 18 yards, making him the leading rusher for Green Bay. Quarterback Jordan Love added one for seven, and receiver Bo Melton ran one time, for three yards.

And, no, that's not good enough. Especially on a night when receiver Christian Watson was lost early to a concussion.

Even if the Packers can muster enough wins over the final six weeks to qualify for the postseason, it will become very hard to win in January if they can't run or stop the run.
